ABSTRACT:

This final technical report details the successful design, construction, and operational validation of
an innovative dual-loop CO₂ capture technology designed to achieve deep decarbonization (>99%)
from Natural Gas Combined Cycle (NGCC) power plants that results in the electricity with carbon
intensity of approximate 42 kg CO₂-eq/MWh, less than the electricity produced by solar PV. The
integrated process couples a primary aqueous solvent (in this project, a water lean solvent – WLS)
absorption loop for bulk CO₂ removal with a secondary potassium hydroxide (KOH) polishing
loop featuring electrochemical regeneration. This architecture leverages the higher exergy
efficiency of the primary loop while utilizing the fastest kinetic of the secondary loop to capture
dilute residual CO₂, achieving an overall capture rate of 99.9% and co-producing pure hydrogen
after moisture being condensed and dehydrated.
 Technical feasibility was established through a comprehensive 2,000-hour experimental
campaign on a bench-scale fully-integrated unit (using 4” absorber and 4” stripper) with the
feeding flue gas flowrate in the range of 8-20 cfm, confirming the attainment of Technology
Readiness Level (TRL) 4. The project executed extensive parametric testing followed by 1,000
hours of continuous steady-state testing, demonstrating exceptional process stability with the
electrochemical regenerator exhibiting less than a 10% reduction in electrical conductivity over
the duration of the campaign. Operational characterization gathering on the bench unit revealed
distinct energy profiles for the hybrid system: the primary loop required approximately 280 kJ
mol⁻¹ for bulk removal, while the polishing loop required approximately 1,600 kJ mol⁻¹
specifically when reducing dilute CO₂ concentrations from ~740 ppm down to <50 ppm. (Please
note those energy values/numbers can only be viewed as relative relationship and should not be
extrapolated as absolute values required for CO2 capture). Furthermore, dynamic testing validated
the system’s flexibility for utility applications, demonstrating a rapid process response time of <30
minutes to changes in flue gas flowrate.
 Emission monitoring confirmed that the dual-loop architecture effectively mitigates
solvent losses, utilizing a water wash to remove entrained aerosols to <1 ppm. The Techno-
Economic Analysis (TEA) indicates a cost of capture of $59.3/tonne and a Levelized Cost of
Electricity (LCOE) of 71.7 $/MWh at the overall capture efficiency of 99.8% of total carbon in
the flue gas stream, with sensitivity analysis identifying an economic optimum when the primary
loop captures 97% of the total CO₂. A Life Cycle Assessment (LCA) confirms the technology’s
potential for net-negative emissions, determining a Global Warming Potential (GWP) of 52 kg
CO₂-eq/MWh—significantly lower than the baseline—which further decreases to 42 kg CO₂-
eq/MWh when crediting the displacement of conventional hydrogen production.

1. EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
1.1 Overview
 The major accomplishments coming from this project are:
1. To show the capability of achieving net negative carbon emission (e.g. the CO2 in the effluent
 at stack exit is below the 400 ppm, less than the CO2 in the combustion air) from a flue gas
 stream containing 4 vol% CO2, the project team designed, tested, refined, and characterized a
 combined process (or dual loop process), including a primary loop (conventional temperature-
 swing thermochemical process) followed by a polishing loop (electrochemical process), from
 which the thermochemical process removed more than 95% of CO2, and the electrochemical
 process removed the remaining CO2 while offering value-added products like pure H2 to offset
 the overall cost.
2. To develop and improve the solvent regeneration associated with the electrochemical process,
 the project team: (1) evaluated titanium as an anode material option to enable stable operation
 for more than 120 hours; (2) prepared and tested electrochemically and durable catalytic
 electrodes thermally coated with iridium oxide and platinum oxide, achieving approximately
 4 V operation per cell for about 1,500 hours; and (3) modified a commercially available
 stacking water electrolyzer with 10 electrode pairs to demonstrate the scale up potential for the
 electrochemical polishing process for low concentration of CO2 capture.
3. To characterize the dual loop process, the project team completed a 2,000-hour test of capturing
 a 4 vol% CO2 stream. This test included 3 campaigns, spanned from January 2025 to August
 2025, and the campaigns were1,000-hour steady-state operation, 500-hour dynamic operation,
 and 500-hour stressful condition with accelerated degrading solvent regeneration.
 o From 1,000-hour steady-state operation, the project team achieved 99.9% carbon capture
 efficiency at 8 cfm for 24-hour 5-day non-stop operating, during which the energy
 consumption for the thermochemical primary loop and electrochemical polishing loop
 were, respectively, about 280 kJ mol-1 and 1,600 kJ mol-1. Please note those energy
 values/numbers can only be viewed as relative relationship and should not be extrapolated
 as absolute values required for CO2 capture due to short reaction column, heat loss, wall
 effect on solvent liquid distribution and other factors that are typically existing on the bench
 scale study. Furthermore, the electrochemical solvent regenerator was able to retain its
 solvent regeneration process at 9.6 A as the charging current for at least 1,000 hours.
 Calculated according to the polarization curves, electrical conductivity of the solvent
 regenerator was reduced merely by about 9.5% over 1,000 hours.
 o From 500-hour dynamic operation, the project team achieved process response time of <30
 minutes by changing the flue gas flowrate. Furthermore, it was found that, for the present
 setup, an increase in gas flowrate by 1 cfm likely led to increasing the effluent CO2
 concentrations by 4-10 fold due to the limit of effective contacting surface.
 o From 500-hour stressful condition operation, the project team increased the reboiler
 temperature and solvent circulation rate to intentionally accelerate the solvent degradation.
 From the gas-side analysis, it is found that an increase in the solvent circulation rate by 1
 lb h-1 resulted in approximately a 0.11 ppm increase in NH3 emissions resulted from the
 increased dissolved oxygen; and an increase in the stripper bottom temperature by 1 oC
 resulted in approximately a 1.43 ppm increase in NH3 emissions. Moreover, from the
 liquid-side analysis, 3 thermal degradation products, urea (N,N′-bis(2-
 hydroxypropyl)urea), AMEA2P (1-[(2-amino-1-methylethyl)amino]-2-propanol), and

 6

 MEOZD (5-methyl-2-oxazolidinone), were identified, of which AMEA2P and MEOZD were
 significantly produced when the stripper bottom temperature was more than 130oC. Lastly,
 adding 100 ppm A2P into the potassium based inorganic solvent for the electrochemical
 polishing loop could cause increases in the cell operating voltage, which was likely due to
 the charge transfer impediment caused by amine molecules around the electrodes.
4. Techno-economic Analysis (TEA): The comprehensive TEA confirms that the dual-loop
 process is cost-competitive. The analysis indicates a Levelized Cost of Electricity (LCOE) of
 approximately 71.7 $/MWh. The cost of CO₂ capture is estimated at $59.3/tonne, and the cost
 of CO₂ avoided is $71.5/tonne. The Bare Erect Cost (BEC) for the primary capture loop and
 compression is calculated at $249.3 million, while the secondary loop capture and processing
 equipment BEC is $72.4 million. Sensitivity analysis demonstrates that optimizing the primary
 loop capture rate to 97% can minimize the overall cost to $69.2/tonne.
5. Life-cycle Analysis (LCA): The LCA evaluated the Global Warming Potential (GWP) of the
 technology based on a functional unit of 1 MWh of electricity delivered. The results
 demonstrate that the dual-loop system achieves net-negative emissions. Without accounting
 for the displacement credit of the hydrogen co-product, the system has a GWP of 52 kg CO₂-
 eq/MWh. When the hydrogen produced is credited as displacing hydrogen from Steam
 Methane Reforming (SMR), the GWP drops to 42 kg CO₂-eq/MWh. The analysis identified
 upstream methane leakage as the dominant contributor to the remaining carbon footprint.
6. EH&S: The Environmental, Health, and Safety (EH&S) assessment identified no obvious
 concerns or apparent barriers to commercial deployment. The assessment noted that ammonia
 (NH₃) emissions and solvent degradation products (such as urea, AMEA2P, and MeOZD) are
 the primary chemical risks, which are manageable with standard mitigation strategies.
 Specifically, NH₃ emissions were found to correlate positively with stripper temperature and
 solvent circulation rates, necessitating optimized operating parameters to maintain safety and
 compliance.

1.2 Key Results
Absorber, Electrochemical Regenerator and Integrated System Design (Task 3):
 The project team designed a dual-loop process to treat flue gas containing approximately 4
vol% CO₂ and 12 vol% O₂, integrating a thermochemical primary loop for bulk removal with an
electrochemical polishing loop for deep cleaning.
• Integrated System Configuration: The primary loop utilizes a water-lean solvent in a
 conventional absorber-stripper setup. To reduce the solve makeup and protect the downstream
 electrochemical loop, the treated flue gas passes through a water-wash column and a knockout
 drum to remove entrained amines and aerosols before entering the polishing absorber.
• Polishing Absorber Design: The polishing absorber is a 316 stainless steel column measuring
 72 inches in height and 8 inches in diameter. It utilizes a counter-current flow design with two
 independent spray levels: the upper nozzle distributes freshly regenerated KOH solution, while
 the lower nozzle recirculates the bulk solvent mixture. To overcome mass transfer limitations
 identified during early modeling, the column internals were optimized by replacing standard
 random packing with stainless steel wool, which increased the packing density to
 approximately 1,430 m²/m³.
• Electrochemical Regenerator Design: The regeneration system was scaled up from a single
 cell to a stacking flow electrolyzer containing 10 pairs of electrodes, providing a total effective
 surface area of 840 cm². The unit features an open flow-field design to minimize hydraulic

 7

 pressure drop. The electrodes are composed of titanium mesh thermal spray coated with
 iridium and platinum oxides, selected to ensure durability and reduce the overpotential for gas
 evolution reactions.
Fabrication, Modification, Installation and Commissioning (Task 4):
 This task involved the fabrication of the designed components and critical modifications to
address material compatibility and sealing challenges identified during initial testing.
• Electrode Replacement: Initial fabrication utilized nickel-based alloy anodes. Testing
 revealed that these corroded in carbonate-containing solutions, causing flow channel
 blockages. The team replaced them with titanium electrodes, which stabilized the operating
 voltage at approximately 5.5 V and eliminated corrosion.
• Primary Loop Materials: Early tests showed that the tetraglyme-based water-lean solvent
 damaged PVC and rubber components at elevated temperatures (~60°C). The team replaced
 these wetted parts with stainless steel and Teflon to ensure long-term integrity.
• Modification for Sealing: During commissioning of the stacked regenerator, gaps formed
 between the membrane and gasket, leading to solvent cross-mixing (anolyte and catholyte)
 which diminished pH swings. To resolve this, the team designed and fabricated 3D-printed
 nylon compression blocks embedded within the inlet/outlet of flow channels. These blocks
 sealed the gaps effectively, preventing cross-mixing for over 2,000 hours of operation.
• Commissioning Results: The polishing loop was commissioned using KOH concentrations
 of 3 M, 3.5 M, and 5 M. Results demonstrated a linear relationship where higher KOH
 concentrations reduced remaining CO₂. Analysis predicted that 6 M KOH would be required
 to meet the 99.8% capture target with the original design; the subsequent switch to high-density
 stainless steel wool packing allowed the system to meet these targets at achievable
 concentrations.
Parametric Evaluation and Data Analysis (Task 5):
 In Task 5, the primary loop demonstrated flexibility of effective CO₂ capture of 85–95%+
when operated at gas flow rates of 8-14 cfm, L/G ratios of 1.8-2.2, and stripper bottom
temperatures of 114-118 ℃. Multiple electrode materials were evaluated for solvent regeneration:
Pt-Ti was used in the baseline electrochemical regenerator, Ni-Fe exhibited superior regeneration
performance with the minimal achievable voltage of 3.3 V, and titanium showed the best long-
term durability, maintaining stable operation for at least 120 hours. The Aquivion® and Nafion®
membrane also showed strong durability, operating for 150 hours with minimal change in current-
voltage behavior. The in-house single-pair solvent regenerator configured with a titanium
electrode performed reliably over a wide range of charging currents with no observed solvent
leakage and electrode corrosion. The membrane integrity was maintained using a 3D-printed
polymer compression block to prevent cross-mixing. Downstream polishing achieved >99.8% CO2
removal, reducing treated flue gas concentrations to below 100 ppm at 8 cfm. During integrated
operation, a 140-hour test with 700 ppm inlet CO2 at 8 cfm achieved approximately 63% overall
CO2 capture.
Polishing Loop Modeling (Task 6):
 Following the same modeling methodology established for the primary capture loop, a rate-
based Aspen Plus® model using KOH was developed to predict performance in the polishing loop
and evaluate design sensitivities. The model incorporates mass transfer, reaction kinetics, and
thermodynamic equilibria to simulate CO₂ absorption under relevant operating conditions. It was
utilized to investigate the critical trade-off between chemical driving force (fresh feed rate) and
hydraulic operation (internal circulation rate). Contrary to standard absorption configurations, the

 8

simulation results (Exhibit 3.3.2) quantitatively demonstrate that superior carbon capture
performance is achieved by minimizing the internal solvent circulation rate (Total L/G ≈ 0.1). By
limiting the internal pumparound, the system minimizes back-mixing and preserves a pristine,
high-alkalinity zone at the top of the absorber dominated by the fresh KOH feed. This "Low
Circulation" configuration allows the system to consistently achieve <100 ppm effluent CO₂ even
as the bulk solvent rich pH drops to ~11.2. In contrast, higher circulation rates dilute the fresh feed
with carbonated solvent, forcing the system to operate at a much higher average pH (and thus
higher chemical consumption) to meet the same removal target.
Long-term Evaluations and Data Analysis. (Task 7):
 In Task 7, on April 25, 2025, the system reached 1,000 hours of steady-state operation with
an overall capture efficiency of ~99.9% at ~4 vol% inlet CO2. A polarization test at 1,000 hours
showed a solvent conductivity of ~3.14 S·m⁻¹, representing only a 9.5% decrease from the initial
value. A 500-hour dynamics test completed May 30 demonstrated that capture performance
responds within 30 minutes to changes in flue-gas flowrate or CO₂ concentration and showed no
disruption after a 15-minute shutdown of the solvent regeneration heater. As of August 2025, a
500-hour stress test under accelerated degradation conditions showed that increasing solvent
circulation or stripper temperature produced higher levels of known thermal-oxidative degradation
products, but no new compounds. When 100 ppm A2P was introduced, the solvent regeneration
voltage rose from 4.0 to 4.9 V over three weeks at a 9.6 A charging current, indicating increased
electrochemical load under stressed chemical conditions.
Techno-economic Analysis (TEA) (Task 8):
 The comprehensive TEA confirms that the dual-loop process is cost-competitive. The
analysis indicates a Levelized Cost of Electricity (LCOE) of approximately 71.7 $/MWh. The cost
of CO₂ capture is estimated at $59.3/tonne, and the cost of CO₂ avoided is $71.5/tonne. The Bare
Erect Cost (BEC) for the primary capture loop and compression is calculated at $249.3 million,
while the secondary loop capture and processing equipment BEC is $72.4 million. Sensitivity
analysis demonstrates that optimizing the primary loop capture rate to 97% can minimize the
overall cost to $69.2/tonne.
Life-cycle Analysis (LCA) (Task 9):
 The LCA evaluated the Global Warming Potential (GWP) of the technology based on a
functional unit of 1 MWh of electricity delivered. The results demonstrate that the dual-loop
system achieves net-negative emissions. Without accounting for the displacement credit of the
hydrogen co-product, the system has a GWP of 52 kg CO₂-eq/MWh. When the hydrogen produced
is credited as displacing hydrogen from Steam Methane Reforming (SMR), the GWP drops to 42
kg CO₂-eq/MWh. The analysis identified upstream methane leakage as the dominant contributor
to the remaining carbon footprint.
Environmental, Health, and Safety (EH&S) (Task 10):
 The Environmental, Health, and Safety (EH&S) assessment identified no obvious concerns or
apparent barriers to commercial deployment. The assessment noted that ammonia (NH₃) emissions
and solvent degradation products (such as Urea, AMEA2P, and MeOZD) are the primary chemical
risks, which are manageable with standard mitigation strategies. Specifically, NH₃ emissions were
found to correlate positively with stripper temperature and solvent circulation rates, necessitating
optimized operating parameters to maintain safety and compliance.

2. BACKGROUND AND TECHNOLOGY DESCRIPTION

 9

2.1. Project Background and Objective
 To address the technical challenge from the low CO2 (~4 vol%) and high O2 (~12 vol%)
concentrations in natural gas combined cycle (NGCC) flue gas along with a very high CO2 capture
efficiency (95+%) and 20% cost reduction from National Energy Technology Laboratory (NETL)
reference case B31B in baseline report Rev. 4, [1] the University of Kentucky Institute for
Decarbonization and Energy Advancement (UK IDEA) has been developing a dual loop process
to lower the capital cost by 50% and offset the operating cost with net-negative CO2 emissions and
H2 production. The objectives are to: 1) design, retrofit and research a dual solvent CO2 capture
system (CCS) on the existing UK 0.1 MWth bench-scale facility using natural gas-derived flue
gas, augmented to match NGCC CO2 and O2 concentrations with 99+% CO2 capture efficiency;
2) conduct a techno-economic analysis (TEA) to document the benefits of the proposed technology
and identify technology gaps for the next scale of development; 3) assess environmental, health
and safety (EH&S) issues relating to the solvent and electrodes and their degradations during long-
term operation and extrapolate to commercial-scale application; 4) conduct a life cycle analysis
(LCA) to document the practical lifetime; 5) develop a technology maturation plan (TMP) for
future technology development. This project is 45 months in duration, divided into 3 budget
periods (BPs). There are 10 Project Tasks, 11 Milestones and 11 Success Criteria. All project
success criteria were satisfied and are shown in Exhibit 2.1.1

Exhibit 2.1.1 Success Criteria for DE-FE0032134

2.2. Process Description
 Exhibit 2.2.1 presents the process flow diagram for the proposed dual-loop process that
includes 2 processes driven by the thermochemical and electrochemical absorption/regeneration
processes, respectively. This technology is aimed at capturing CO2 from natural gas combined
cycle (NGCC) flue gas containing approximately 4 vol% CO2, achieving an overall capture
efficiency greater than 99% and co-producing pure hydrogen (H2).

 11

Exhibit 2.2.1. The process flow diagram for project DE-FE0032134, which is aimed at achieving greater
than 99% carbon capture efficiency for capturing CO2 from natural gas combined cycle flue gas containing
approximately 4 vol% CO2. The process includes 2 sub-processes which are operated via thermochemical
and electrochemical work principles, respectively. The features of the process are: (1) the thermochemical
process utilizes water lean solvent to achieve cost-effective bulk CO2 removal, and (2) the electrochemical
process used water electrolysis to polish the flue gas stream with less than 100 ppm CO2 emission while
co-producing H2 to further offset the operating cost.

 In the primary, or thermochemical, capture loop, flue gas containing approximately 4 vol%
CO2 enters the bottom of the absorber, where 85-95% of the CO2 is removed through counter-
current contact with a water-lean organic solvent distributed over 250Y stainless-steel structured
packing elements. The CO2-rich solvent is then sent to a conventional heat-driven stripper, where

 12

it is regenerated and produces CO2 with a purity greater than 99% after moisture condensation.
The treated flue gas, containing the remaining CO2, passes through a water-wash column to
remove entrained amines and aerosols to below 1 ppm before being directed into the polishing, or
electrochemical, loop.
 In the electrochemical polishing loop, the flue gas contacts an inorganic KOH solvent in a
packing-free open tower, where the solvent is continuously regenerated by alkaline water
electrolysis. This step further reduces the CO2 concentration to below 100 ppm prior to
atmospheric release. The electrochemical regenerator simultaneously restores KOH to its active
form and produces high-purity hydrogen (99.9%) as a valuable by-product. The regenerator is
constructed as an alkaline water flow electrolyzer, consisting of a pair of metal electrodes separated
by a cation-exchange membrane.
 Overall, the proposed dual-loop system is expected to offer significant capital cost savings
compared to conventional carbon capture technologies, while also offsetting operating costs
through negative CO2 emissions and H2 generation.

3. PROJECT TECHNICAL RESULTS
3.1 Polishing Loop Process Design, Construction, Characterization, and Improvement
 In comparison with the thermochemical primary loop process, the polishing loop process
driven by electrochemical water electrolysis was new to the project. As a consequence, the project
team from UK-IDEA made a great effort of designing, fabricating, testing, and refining the
electrochemical process during BPs 1 and 2. Key improvements included scaling up the solvent
regeneration system from a single electrode pair electrolyzer to a stacking flow electrolyzer with
10 electrode pairs, incorporating catalytic materials like iridium oxides to lower the operating
voltage, and enhancing carbon capture efficiency by increasing the packing density within the
absorber [2,3]. The finalized electrochemical polishing loop system used in BP 3 for the long-term
parametric test consisted of a stacking solvent regenerator equipped with 10 pairs of catalytic
titanium electrodes and a CO₂ absorber packed with stainless steel wool to maximize gas-liquid
contact. In the following, the details of process design, fabrication, characterization, and
improvement will be disclosed.
 Exhibit 3.1.1 illustrates the process design of the electrochemical polishing loop, including
additional treatment steps. The flue gas from the thermochemical primary loop first passes through
a water wash column and then a knockout drum before entering the polishing loop. These steps
are designed to remove excess amines and aerosols prior to electrochemical treatment. In the
electrochemical polishing loop, the flue gas enters the absorber and flows counter-current to the
carbon capture solvent. The treated flue gas, with a reduced CO2 concentration, exits from the top
of the absorber. The absorber is equipped with two levels of solvent spray such that the upper level
distributes freshly regenerated KOH while the lower level recirculates the solvent. A portion of
the circulating solvent is directed to the electrochemical regenerator for regeneration before being
returned to the system.
 Exhibits 3.1.2 and 3.1.3 show the details of the CO2 absorber and electrochemical solvent
regenerator for the electrochemical polishing loop. The absorber, constructed from 316 stainless
steel, measured 72 inches in length and 8 inches in width. It was equipped with two levels of spray
nozzles, i.e., the upper level distributed freshly regenerated KOH solution at a low rate, while the
lower level recirculated a mixture of KOH and K2CO3 to ensure continuous solvent utilization and
improved CO2 absorption efficiency. To further enhance mass transfer, the absorber incorporated

 13

two stages of random packing, which increased the gas-liquid contact surface and promoted
uniform flow distribution throughout the column. The design was optimized to minimize energy
consumption associated with KOH regeneration while maintaining high CO2 capture efficiency.
This configuration also helped stabilize solvent performance during extended operation, reducing
the need for frequent solvent replacement and contributing to the overall cost-effectiveness of the
electrochemical polishing loop. The solvent regenerator was constructed using 316 stainless steel
endplates and nickel-based electrodes, with an effective surface area of 400 cm2. The unit
employed an open flow-field design to minimize hydraulic pressure losses, thereby improving
electrolyte circulation and reducing overall system backpressure. The use of nickel-based alloy
electrodes significantly reduced energy consumption associated with solvent regeneration due to
their high catalytic activity and electrical conductivity. This configuration provided a stable and
efficient electrochemical environment for continuous KOH regeneration while maintaining low
cell voltage during operation.

Exhibit 3.1.1. The process flow diagram of the electrochemical polishing loop for project DE-FE0032134.
The polishing loop process was driven by the alkaline water electrolysis to capture the remaining CO2 from
the primary loop process. In addition, the polishing loop process can produce H2 gas that can be
commercially leveraged thereafter offsetting the process cost.

 14

Exhibit 3.1.2. (a) A 3-D CAD drawing of CO2 absorber for the electrochemical loop. (b) A photo of the
absorber, taken during the fabrication stage. The absorber, constructed from 316 stainless steel, measured
72 inches in length and 8 inches in width. It featured two levels of spray nozzles, i.e., the upper level delivers
freshly regenerated KOH, while the lower level recirculates a mixture of KOH and K2CO3. In addition, the
absorber had two stages of random packing.

 15

Exhibit 3.1.3. (a) A 3-D CAD drawing of solvent regenerator for the electrochemical loop. (a) A photo of
the solvent regenerator. (c) A photo of the flow field. The solvent regenerator, constructed from 316
stainless steel endplates and nickel-based electrodes, measured 400 cm2 surface area. It featured the open
flow field design to reduce the hydraulic pressure and the use of nickel-based alloy electrodes to reduce the
energy consumption for solvent regeneration.

Exhibit 3.1.4. The photo of the electrochemical polishing loop process, which was built based upon the
process flow diagram depicted in Exhibit 3.1.1. The process was mainly configured with carbon capture
and solvent regeneration to use KOH to capture the remaining CO2 from a thermochemical primary loop
process. Usually, the operation was performed at 10 A as the applied current and 1 L min-1 as the electrolyte
circulation rate. The discharge rate of the carbon rich solvent was indicated by the pH of anolyte.

 16

 Once the fabrication of the CO2 absorber and solvent regenerator was completed, the
electrochemical polishing loop process was assembled with additional peripherals like solvent
tanks for catholyte and anolyte, pH electrode, power supply, etc., as shown in Exhibit 3.1.4.
During the commissioning stage, the carbon-rich solvent was initially discharged from the
absorber sump into the anolyte tank, while freshly regenerated KOH was simultaneously pumped
into the absorber to retain the highly efficient carbon capture process. Solvent regeneration
occurred via water electrolysis within the electrochemical solvent regenerator, where OH- ions
were regenerated at the cathode. The progress of the regeneration process was monitored by
tracking the pH of the anolyte solution, which decreased from a high to a lower value at pH 7 as
regeneration proceeded. Upon completion of a regeneration cycle, the catholyte was transferred to
a holding tank, followed by transferring the anolyte with its reduced alkalinity to the catholyte

 Membrane
tank. This liquid transfer sequence was repeatedly performed to maintain continuous and stable
system operation.

 Nickel-based corrosion product
 clogged flow channel

Exhibit 3.1.5. The photo of the corrosion products from the electrochemical solvent regenerator. The
nickel-based positive electrode (or anode) was found to be corroded after testing the solvent regenerator in
a CO32- based solvent. The corrosion products blocked the flow channel, leading to solvent leakage.

 From the initial tests, it was found that nickel-based alloy electrodes were unsuitable for
use as the anode in CO32--containing solutions. As shown in Exhibit 3.1.5, the nickel-based anode
exhibited significant corrosion, and the resulting corrosion products accumulated within the flow
channels, leading to partial blockages. This buildup ultimately caused an increase in pressure drop
across the cell and resulted in solvent leakage. The observation made the project team replace the
nickel-based electrode with a titanium electrode. As shown in Exhibit 3.1.6, after using the anode
made of titanium, the operating voltage for solvent regeneration stabilized with the average voltage
at 5.5 V. In addition, once a current was applied to the solvent regenerator, the voltage was
increased from the open-circuit voltage to the operating voltage, and co-currently, pH of the

 17

anolyte was reduced suggesting that CO32- equilibrium was pushed toward CO2 released from the
anolyte.

Exhibit 3.1.6. The characterization results of the electrochemical solvent regenerator configured with a
titanium anode. At 10 A as the applied current for solvent regeneration, the plot shows the operating voltage
was reproducible with the average value at approximately 5.5 V.

 18

Exhibit 3.1.7. (a) A photo showing the assembly of the stacked solvent regenerator. The regenerator was
modified by replacing the original separator with a cation exchange membrane and incorporating a catalytic
titanium mesh within the electrolyzer chamber. The titanium mesh was coated with a thin layer of iridium
and platinum oxides to enhance electrochemical activity and durability. (b) A photo of the electrochemical
solvent regeneration process using the stacked solvent regenerator during operation.

 To further enhance the solvent regeneration process, specifically aiming to scale up the
electrolyzer size to meet KOH supply for the carbon capture process and reduce cell voltage to
regenerate KOH, a commercially available stacked electrolyzer was modified. The modifications
involved replacing the original membrane materials and embedding a catalytic titanium mesh
spring within the electrolyzer chamber. As shown in Exhibit 3.1.7, the stacking solvent
regenerator (or electrolyzer) was configured with 10 pairs of electrodes with a total surface area
of approximately 840 cm2, more than 50% increases in the surface area in comparison with the
electrolyzer in Exhibit 3.1.3. The titanium mesh spring was thermally coated with iridium and
platinum oxides to reduce the voltage for the gas evolution reactions.

Exhibit 3.1.8. (a) The residual CO2 concentration profile when the CO2 absorber employed 4 L of 3-5 M
KOH solvent to capture 800 ppm CO2. In the initial tests (or during process commissioning), the solvent
was circulated via the upper-level nozzle only at 2 L min-1. The CO2 concentration values were averaged
between 100 and 300 min to calculate the average CO2 concentration as highlighted by yellow. (b) A plot
of average CO2 concentration as a function of KOH concentration, where the red line was yielded by linear
regression.

 After completing the improvements to the solvent regeneration performance, the carbon
capture system was tested using KOH initially to estimate the quantity of KOH supply by the
solvent regeneration process. Exhibit 3.1.8(a) presents the results of carbon capture when the CO2
absorber was commissioned by circulating 4 L of 3 M, 3.5 M, and 5 M KOH at 2 L min-1 to capture
about 800 ppm CO2 flue gas at 8 cfm. It is found that as the KOH concentration increases, the
remaining CO2 concentration decreases. Such improvement in carbon capture is primarily
attributed to the increased availability of OH- ions at the gas-liquid interface (or surface), which

 19

facilitates CO2 absorption. Further analysis was performed by plotting the average remaining CO2
concentration versus KOH concentration, as shown in Exhibit 3.1.8(b). Linear regression analysis
indicates that achieving the success criterion of capturing more than 99.8% of CO2 from a 4 vol%
CO2 stream requires approximately 6 M KOH, which must be continuously regenerated through
the electrochemistry-based solvent regeneration process. However, given the operating
specifications, e.g., max current that can be loaded onto the stacking flow electrolyzer or
electrochemical solvent regenerator, and electrode material specs of the solvent regenerator,
continuously achieving such a high KOH concentration is a challenge at the present stage based
upon the project’s budget and timing. Therefore, the project team modified the absorber by
replacing the random packing with stainless-steel wool to increase the liquid-gas interface area (or
packing density) up to about 1,430 m2/m3.

3.2 Thermochemical Primary Loop
 In comparison with the electrochemical polishing loop, the structure of the thermochemical
primary loop closely resembled that of a conventional carbon capture system. It comprised a CO2
absorber packed with 250Y-type structured packing and a thermally driven CO2 stripper for
solvent regeneration. The absorber and stripper were interconnected through a heat exchanger
designed to optimize both CO2 absorption and desorption performance simultaneously. The flow
diagram of thermochemical primary loop process is depicted in Exhibit 3.2.1. Please note that for
this project, the operational methods of improving carbon capture performance were not
performed, e.g., pump around at the CO2 absorber and splitting carbon rich solvent returning to
the stripper.
 Unlike the conventional thermochemical process utilizing aqueous amine solvents, a type
of water lean solvent was used for this project. The water lean solvent was made of 38 wt% A2P,
and 25 wt% water balanced with tetraglyme. From the initial tests, it is found that this solvent at
an elevated temperature, i.e., ~60oC, could harm the PVC plastics and rubber gaskets, leading to
the solvent leak from the primary loop CO2 absorber, as shown in Exhibit 3.2.2(a) and (b). To
resolve such a problem, the project team replaced nearly all the PVC and rubber materials with
stainless steel and Teflon for the following long-term parametric tests, as depicted in Exhibit
3.2.2(c).

 20

Exhibit 3.2.1. The process flow diagram of the thermochemical primary loop for project DE-FE0032134.
The structure of the primary loop is the same as the conventional thermal-MEA process. In this project, to
align the objective according to the FOA requirement, a type of water lean solvent was used. Furthermore,
the treated flue gas from the absorber was further processed to remove the remaining CO2 in the polishing
loop shown in Exhibit 3.1.1.

Exhibit 3.2.2. (a)-(b) When using PVC and rubber materials for a CO2 absorber column, tetraglyme from
the water lean solvent (WLS) could harm the column’s materials at an elevated temperature, resulting in

 21

the solvent leak. (c) After lessons learned from the initial tests, the project team used stainless steel and
Teflon to refabricate the column. This column was used to test the WLS through the long-term parametric
tests.

3.3 Polishing Loop Process Simulation and Optimization

Exhibit 3.3.1. The process flow diagram of the polishing loop absorber, which was used for an equilibrium
simulation by Aspen.

 The Aspen Plus® rate-based equilibrium model was developed to optimize the operation
of the KOH-based polishing absorber illustrated in Exhibit 3.3.1. The simulation evaluated the
trade-offs between chemical consumption (fresh KOH feed) and hydraulic operation (internal
circulation/pumparound) to achieve a deep CO₂ removal target of <100 ppm. Analysis of the
equilibrium curves in Exhibit 3.3.2 reveals two distinct operational mechanisms driving
performance. First, along any single performance curve (chemical driving force) where the internal
circulation (pumparound) rate is held constant, the system's performance is driven by the fresh
KOH feed rate. For example, when moving left (better performance), the rate of fresh KOH
addition is increased to achieve lower CO₂ outlet concentrations (e.g., <100 ppm). This raises the
overall alkalinity of the solvent (higher rich pH), providing a stronger chemical driving force to
absorb the final traces of CO₂. While moving right (lower performance), as the fresh KOH feed
rate is reduced, the solvent becomes more saturated with CO₂ (lower rich pH). This reduces the
mass transfer driving force, resulting in a higher concentration of CO₂ slipping through to the outlet.
Thus, each individual curve traces the trade-off between chemical consumption (fresh KOH feed)
and capture efficiency. Second, comparing the family of curves reveals the impact of hydraulic
design. The modeling results identify a critical optimization strategy: minimizing the internal
circulation rate improves performance for this specific polishing application. Here, the Liquid-to-
Gas ratio (L/G) is determined by the total liquid flux, i.e., the sum of the fresh KOH feed and the
internal pumparound flow. The simulation confirms that for deep polishing, a low circulation
strategy (low total L/G) is superior. By keeping the pumparound rate low (e.g., 184 kg/hr or 5%
of gas flow rate), the system prevents the dilution of the potent fresh feed. This maintains a sharp
concentration gradient with a high-alkalinity zone at the column top—where liquid flux is
dominated by fresh feed—allowing the system to reduce effluent CO₂ to <100 ppm even when the
bulk rich solvent pH drops to ~11.3. In contrast, increasing the pumparound rate (high total L/G)

 22

induces back-mixing. This dilutes the fresh KOH with the bulk carbonated solvent, forcing the
system to operate at a much higher average alkalinity (e.g., pH >13.5) to achieve the same 100
ppm target.

Scale-Up Implications and Hydraulic Limits
 While the Aspen Plus® model indicates that a total liquid-to-gas (L/G) ratio of 0.1 is
chemically sufficient for polishing (based on a total liquid flow of ~370 kg/hr), practical
implementation at the pilot scale is constrained by the minimum wetting rate of the packing. At
the modeled commercial scale (3,684 kg/hr gas flow), a total liquid load of ~370 kg/hr is
hydraulically marginal but computationally valid for equilibrium checks. Scaling this mass balance
down to the pilot gas flow (~20 kg/hr, i.e. 10 CFM) yields a target total liquid rate of only ~2 kg/hr.
Experimental observations confirm that a flow of ~2 kg/hr is physically insufficient to wet the
packing surface in the pilot absorber. Consequently, the pilot unit must operate at a significantly
higher internal circulation rate (high pumparound) not for chemical benefit, but to satisfy the
physical requirement of wetting the packing surface area.

Recommendations for Commercial-scale Design
 Based on the discrepancy between the chemical optimum and hydraulic constraints, the
following design basis is recommended for commercial scale-up: the commercial polishing loop
should be designed for a total L/G of 0.1 to minimize chemical usage and pump energy while
maximizing the "polishing" effect of the fresh feed. To enable this low-flow operation, the absorber
must utilize wire gauze structured packing (e.g., Sulzer BX or equivalent). Unlike standard sheet
metal packing which relies on bulk liquid volume to prevent dry spots, wire gauze utilizes capillary
action to ensure 100% surface wetting at liquid loads as low as 0.1 m³/m²h. This specification
resolves the hydraulic constraint, allowing the system to operate at the model-predicted optimum
without the channeling observed at the pilot scale. It is noted that operating at such low liquid
fluxes (0.1 m³/m²h) requires high-precision liquid distributors (e.g., drip-point or capillary pipe
distributors) rather than standard spray nozzles to ensure uniform initial coverage across the
packing bed.

 23

Exhibit 3.3.2. Rich solvent pH and L/G effect on outlet CO2 by an equilibrium simulation via Aspen.

3.4     2,000-Hour Test
 The finalized electrochemical polishing loop system used during BP3 for long-term
parametric testing consisted of a stacking solvent regenerator equipped with 10 pairs of catalytic
titanium electrodes and a CO2 absorber packed with stainless steel wool. In addition, the
thermochemical primary loop employed a type of water lean solvent. The water lean solvent was
made of 38 wt% A2P, and 25 wt% water balanced with tetraglyme. This combined process or dual
loop process was tested in 3 campaigns with a total testing time of 2,000 hours, starting from
January 2025 and ending in August 2025. Each campaign posed different focuses on process
research and development. These focuses will be addressed in the following sections.

3.4.1. 1,000-Hour Steady-State Operation
 1,000-hour steady-state experiment was the first long-term process operation out of the 3
campaigns. The focus of this experiment was to (1) investigate the stability of the electrochemical
solvent regenerator with smaller than 10% reduction in electrical conductivity, and (2) achieve
greater than 99.8% carbon capture efficiency from a 24-hour per day for 5-day operation.

 24

Exhibit 3.4.1.1 A panel of data obtained from a 24 hours per day 5 days operation. (a) CO2 concentration
of the influent for the thermochemical primary loop or (starting CO2 concentration), (b) gas flowrate, (c)
CO2 concentration of the effluent for the primary loop, and (d) CO2 concentration of the effluent for the
electrochemical polishing loop. The average values are reported in the corresponding context.

 Exhibit 3.4.1.1 (a) and (b) depict the raw data collected from a 24 hours per day for 5 days
test. The test started from 2025-1-27 and ended on 2025-2-1. In this non-stop test, the influent CO2
concentration of the thermochemical primary loop was controlled at approximately 41,203.0 ppm,
and the flowrate of the influent was controlled at about 8.0 cfm. (Please note that the flue gas was
produced from a natural gas boiler installed near the testing site.) It is found that the remaining
CO2 concentration after the thermochemical primary loop is 756.3 ppm, and the emitted CO2
concentration after the combined process is 44.5 ppm. Furthermore, as shown in Exhibit 3.4.1.2,
the overall carbon capture efficiency for the combined loop process is about 99.9% with the
polishing loop carbon capture efficiency of 93.7%, which is greater than the success criterion
points for the project in terms of carbon capture performance. In addition, over the course of this
test, as shown in Exhibit 3.4.1.3, the operating voltage of the electrochemical solvent regenerator
was about 3.86 V; and the anolyte pH exhibited periodic fluctuations, where increases in pH

 25

indicated the introduction of carbon-rich solvent into the anolyte tank, while decreases in pH
suggested a shift in carbonate equilibrium toward CO2 release from the anolyte. Exhibit 3.4.1.1
(c) demonstrated stable capture performance over the 1000-hour operation. On average, the
capture efficiency is about 99.7%. (Please note that all the numerical values about CO2
concentration, efficiency, voltage, and flowrate reported in this report were calculated by
averaging the raw data displayed in the relevant exhibits, unless otherwise stated.)
 In addition to exhibiting the ability to achieve the great CO2 reduction for NGCC carbon
capture application, the solvent regeneration stability was another focus for this 1,000-hour
experiment. As depicted in Exhibit 3.4.1.4, the cell operating voltage of the stacking solvent
regenerator hovered around 3.9 V, calculated by averaging the raw voltage data. Moreover, the
anolyte pH swing was controlled between 13 and 5. These observations indicate stable solvent
regeneration operation, with no evidence of electrode corrosion or electrolyte cross-mixing. To
further approve the excellence in stability for solvent regeneration, the electrochemical solvent
regenerator was tested separately every 300 hours using 5 M KOH as both the catholyte and
anolyte at 1 L min-1 to construct polarization curves. As shown in Exhibit 3.4.1.5, a typical
polarization curve is established, featuring increases in the voltage caused by increasing the
applied current. Through the linear regression model, the slope for every polarization curve was
used to calculate the electrical conductivity of the electrochemical solvent regenerator. It is learned
that the conductivity before the 1,000-hour test was 3.47 S m-1, while the conductivity after the
test was 3.14 S m-1. This 9.5% degradation over 1,000-hour test reflects the robust solvent
regeneration process shown in Exhibit 3.4.1.4.

 26

Exhibit 3.4.1.2. Plots of carbon capture efficiency analysis calculated based upon the data depicted in
Exhibit 3.4.1.1. (a) capture efficiency for the electrochemical polishing loop. (b) capture efficiency for the
combined loop. (c) capture performance for the 1000 hours test.

3.4.2. 500-Hour Dynamic Operation
 The objective of this 500-hour dynamic operation is to evaluate the integrated process
compatibility and system response under varying operating conditions, including deliberate
changes in flue gas flow rate, CO2 concentration, and stripper pressure. The data shown in this
section were collected during several continuous 24-hour-per-day, 5-day operation periods.
 Exhibit 3.4.2.1 presents one-week of data, during which the gas flowrate was adjusted
from 8 to 10 cfm by varying the N2 supply, while maintaining constant solvent circulation rate,
stripper temperature, and stripper pressure. It is learned from Exhibit 3.4.2.1(a) that as the gas
flowrate increased, the influent CO2 concentration rose to as high as 50,000 ppm, which was
caused by using N2 supply to control not only the flowrate but also the CO2 concentration. These
variations affected the effluent CO2 concentrations in both the primary and polishing loops, as
shown in Exhibit 3.4.2.1(b). Overall, for the bench-scale process, an increase in gas flowrate by
1 cfm likely leads to increasing the effluent CO2 concentrations by 4-10 fold.
 Exhibit 3.4.2.2 depicts another one-week of data regarding effect of stripper pressure on
carbon capture process, during which the stripper pressure was modified, varying from 17 psi to
23 psi. Given the nature of vapor-liquid equilibrium associated with aqueous carbon solution,
increases in pressure generally requires an increased temperature, and decreases in pressure
reduces temperature, which can be seen in the plot of the temperature profile. By studying the plot
for carbon capture performance, it is learned that reducing the stripper pressure could improve the
carbon capture performance, as shown in the lower concentration of the remaining CO2 from the

 29

primary loop process. Based upon this observation, it is believed that the phase change of water
for this case was more sensitive than that for the carbon rich water lean solvent, resulting in a
leaner lean solvent exiting the stripper. Lastly, on May 30 as indicated in the plot for the stripper
temperature profile, the process was interrupted by cutting off the power to the heater for about 15
min according to the testing plan, after which it was found that the carbon capture performance
had not been dramatically modified.

Exhibit 3.4.2.1 Effect of the influent flowrate on CO2 capture performance for the combined process. From
the top to bottom in plot (a), the plots are: influent flowrate, solvent circulation rate, stripper bottom
temperature, and stripper pressure. From the top to bottom in plot (b), the plots are: influent CO2
concentration, effluent CO2 concentration of the primary loop, and effluent CO2 concentration of the
polishing loop.

 30

Exhibit 3.4.2.2 The plots of one-week data for the dual loop process, in which the stripper pressure was
changed by regulating the pressure release valve. From the top to bottom in plot (a), the plots are: stripper
pressure, stripper bottom temperature, solvent circulation rate, and gas flowrate. The other plots in plot (b)
are: influent CO2 concentration for the primary loop, and effluent CO2 concentration for the primary loop.

 31

Exhibit 3.4.2.3 The process demonstrated a response time of less than 30 minutes following a change in
gas flow rate.

 Exhibit 3.4.2.3 presents evidence of overall process response to a change in gas flowrate.
As shown in the exhibit, the gas flowrate increased at approximately 11:54 pm. 6 minutes later, at
12:00 am, the effluent CO2 concentration from the primary loop began to rise. An additional ten
minutes later, at 12:10 am, an increase in the effluent CO2 concentration from the polishing loop
was observed. The 16-minute response time after step-change being set shown in Exhibit 3.4.2.3
was representative of the dynamic behavior observed during repeated dynamic testing. Across
multiple cycles, most measured process response times fell in the range of approximately 10 to 16
minutes, depending on the specific operating condition, parameters and the magnitude of the
imposed change. These results confirm that the integrated dual-loop process consistently
responded within the project success criterion of less than 30 minutes.

3.4.3. 500-Hour Stress Condition Operation
 Under stress conditions, shown in Exhibit 3.4.3.1, involving both an elevated stripper
bottom temperature close to 140oC and an increased solvent circulation rate up to 135 lb hour-1, a
500-hour operation was conducted to primarily assess the degradation behavior and thermal
stability of the water-lean solvent. The data was collected during several continuous 24-hour-per-
day operation over full 5-day work weeks. The two-day gaps in the plots generally correspond to
weekends, while longer gaps of approximately three days correspond to holiday-related shutdown
periods or other planned downtime between operating blocks. The average values reported in the

 32

figure were calculated from the steady-state portions of each operating period. In parallel, the effect
of introducing the A2P solvent into the polishing loop was evaluated to determine its impact on
the performance, efficiency, and long-term stability of the solvent regenerator.
 Ammonia (NH3) is a common oxidative degradation product formed during the operation
of amine-based carbon capture systems. Its formation primarily results from the dissolved
oxygen/its radicals reacting with amine solvents, particularly under elevated temperatures and in
the presence of oxygen and metal catalysts. In this project, NH3 was initially measured after the
water wash column by an in-situ FTIR instrument. Exhibit 3.4.3.2 shows the results of NH3
emission when (a) the solvent circulation rate was changed and (b) the stripper bottom temperature
was varied. It is found that increases in both the solvent circulation rate and stripper bottom
temperature lead to increases in the NH3 emission. By utilizing the linear regression model to
analyze the data shown in the previous 2 plots, from Exhibit 3.4.3.3, it is learned that (1) an
increase in the solvent circulation rate by 1 lb h- resulted in approximately a 0.11 ppm increase in
NH3 emissions; and (2) an increase in the stripper bottom temperature by 1 oC resulted in
approximately a 1.43 ppm increase in NH3 emissions.

 33

Exhibit 3.4.3.1 Data collected from a 500-hour operation under stress-testing conditions, Throughout the
experiment, the CO2 concentration in the flue gas was controlled at approximately 4 vol% and the gas
flowrate was maintained at 8 cfm while the reboiler temperature and solvent circulation rate for the primary
loop were varied to examine the degradation behavior of the water lean solvent. (a) Measured stripper
bottom temperature, during which the setup temperature for the reboiler was more than 140 oC for some
cases. (b) Measured solvent circulation rate, during which the circulation rate varied from 35.6 to 135.0 lb
hour-1. The blue-colored values in the plot represent averages calculated from steady-state data.

Exhibit 3.4.3.2 FTIR study of the ammonia emitted after the water-wash column during the 500-hour
operation. (a) Effect of the solvent circulation rate on NH3 emission and (b) Effect of the stripper bottom
temperature on NH3 emission. The values highlighted in dark teal represent averages calculated from the
emission data collected during the final two days of testing for each condition. The blue-colored values in
the plot represent averages calculated from steady-state data.

 34

Exhibit 3.4.3.3 Linear regression of the NH3 emissions as a function of (a) solvent circulation rate and (b)
stripper bottom temperature. The slope of the equation may state the rate of solvent degradation affected
by the solvent circulation rate and stripper bottom temperature. The data for this analysis were taken from
Exhibits 3.4.3.1 and 3.4.3.2.

 35

Exhibit 3.4.3.4 Thermal degradation products from water lean solvent for the 500-hour operation at the
evaluated reboiler temperature or stripper bottom temperature (from steady state test).

 In addition to looking at the solvent degradation from the gaseous product, further analysis
was conducted to determine the thermal degradation products from the carbon lean solvent liquid
samples collected every 200 hours. As shown in Exhibit 3.4.3.4, AMEA2P, MeOZD, and urea
have been identified. It is found that urea was the major degradation product from the water lean
solvent while MeOZD was the lowest concentration degradation product. As shown in the plot for
the stress-testing conditions, the concentration of AMEA2P increased dramatically by almost 5-
fold with rising stripper bottom temperature from 116 oC to 137 oC, followed by MeOZD. In
contrast, urea remained nearly unchanged, indicating it was less sensitive to temperature
variations. Future analysis is to apply a regression model to look for the empirical correlation
between these thermal degradation products as a function of temperature. As depicted in Exhibit
3.4.3.5, urea formation was likely independent of temperature. In contrast, significant formation
of AMEA2P and MEOZD was observed once the temperature exceeded 130 °C. Finally, there
were no additional degradation products found during this campaign.
 To understand the potential effect of A2P entrained from the primary loop in the polishing
loop solvent on the electrochemical regeneration performance, the project team rebuilt the
electrochemical solvent regenerator, which had exhibited performance degradation in terms of the
increased cell voltage, as shown in Exhibit 3.4.3.6. Such a performance degradation was mainly
due to the losses of catalytic layer coated around the titanium mesh electrodes. Once the rebuilt
solvent regenerator was tested, approximately 100 ppm A2P was dosed into the polishing loop
solvent every day. As shown in the plot, the cell operating voltage gradually increased from 4.0 V
to 4.9 V during solvent regeneration. This behavior is likely attributed to the rising impedance at
the electrode surface caused by the adsorption of organic molecules onto the electrode.

 36

Exhibit 3.4.3.5 Regression model used to yield the thermal degradation products as a function of the
stripper bottom temperature. From the analysis, urea formation was likely independent of temperature. In
contrast, AMEA2P and MEOZD were substantially produced once the temperature was above 130 oC.

 37

Exhibit 3.4.3.6 Results showing cell voltage of the electrochemical solvent regenerator. The solvent
regenerator has been charged at about 10 A through the entire 2,000-hour testing campaign. After
approximately 1,600 hours of testing, the cell voltage showed a sustained increase, which was mainly due
to the loss of the catalytic layer coated around the titanium mesh electrodes. To investigate the effect of
A2P leakage into the polishing loop on solvent regeneration performance, the solvent regenerator was
rebuilt (indicated in the plot), and 100 ppm of A2P was intentionally added daily to the polishing loop
solution. The cell voltage gradually increased during the last 3 weeks of operation.

3.5 Specific Data Analysis
 Additional data analyses were conducted to characterize specific sections of the combined
loop process, providing further insights to support process improvements in the next phase of
work.

3.5.1. Energy Consumption and Mass Transfer of Electrochemical Polishing Loop
 To show the data analysis of calculating energy consumption and mass transfer coefficient
for the electrochemical polishing loop, the raw data of the 24-hour per day for 5-day operation was
utilized. The raw data are displayed in Exhibit 3.5.2, including the operating voltage, influent and
effluent CO2 concentration, gas flowrate, and carbon capture efficiency. First, the energy
consumption of operating the solvent regenerator, E, was calculated via

 A Python script was developed to implement all equations and streamline data analysis.
Typical results are shown in Exhibit 3.5.1.1. As indicated, using equations (1)-(3) for a 24-hour
for 5-day continuous run, the energy consumption per mole of CO2 captured from the remaining
CO2 in the thermochemical primary loop was calculated as 1,597 kJ mol-1. Typically, increases in
the operating voltage and decreases in the influent CO2 concentration of the polishing loop result
in the energy consumption of carbon capture being increased. For example, as shown in Exhibit
3.5.1.2, when the influent CO2 concentration of the polishing increased to about 1,435 ppm and
the effluent CO2 was relaxed to 89 ppm, energy consumption was reduced by more than 50%.
Using equations (4)-(6) calculates the mass transfer coefficient of capturing CO2. Results in
Exhibit 3.5.1.3 show that the mass transfer coefficient is about 1.3 mmol m-2 h-1 Pa-1. This mass
transfer coefficient could be enhanced once the influent CO2 concentration of the polishing loop
increased.

3.5.2. Energy and NH3 Emissions for Thermochemical Primary Loop
 A similar Python-based approach was used to analyze the thermochemical primary loop
process by calculating both energy consumption and carbon capture efficiency. As shown in
Exhibit 3.5.2.1, the screenshot shows the script of calculating the energy consumption per mole
of CO2 captured in the thermochemical primary loop. For the C/N analysis, solvent samples were
collected daily, and alkalinity was determined using an alkalinity titrator. Carbon loading was then

 40

calculated based on experimental equilibrium data, coupled with the pH values corresponding to
the equilibrium points obtained from the alkalinity titration curves. Periodically, the calculated
carbon loading value was checked through the carbon loading measurement.

Exhibit 3.5.2.1 A screenshot of calculating energy consumption for the primary loop CO2 capture via
Python.

 Once the initial sorting, average, and reorganization of the raw data were completed,
(please note that averaged data at 11 and 12 am were used, corresponding to the time window
during which solvent samples were collected for alkalinity measurements.), all calculated values
were compiled into a spreadsheet for visualization and statistical analysis. This analysis was
conducted to identify correlations among the operating and measured parameters and to determine
the most influential parameter affecting the process. Exhibit 3.5.2.2 visualizes the impact of the
solvent property and process operating parameters on the carbon capture energy requirement. It is
found that the energy value can increase if the capture efficiency, stripper bottom temperature, and
solvent circulation rate are enhanced. On the contrary, increases in the C/N at the stripper-side, gas
inlet temperature, and influent CO2 concentration correlate to energy value decreases.

Exhibit 3.5.2.2 The results were visualized to evaluate the impact of solvent properties and process
operating parameters on carbon capture and energy consumption.

 41

Exhibit 3.5.2.3 The results were visualized to evaluate the impact of solvent properties and process
operating parameters on emitted NH3.

 By applying similar analysis, NH3 emission for the thermochemical primary loop was
studied, in which NH3 emission was measured after the water wash column by FTIR. From
Exhibit 3.5.2.3, it is learned that stripper pressure, carbon capture efficiency, stripper temperature,
and solvent circulation rate are positively correlated with the NH3 emissions.

3.6 Techno-Economic Analysis
 The Techno-Economic Analysis (TEA) was conducted by EPRI to evaluate the economic
feasibility of the UKy dual-loop CO₂ capture technology applied to an F-class Natural Gas
Combined Cycle (NGCC) power plant [1,4]. The design basis assumes a 99.8% overall CO₂ capture
rate, achieved by a 95% capture rate in the primary water-lean solvent loop and 96% capture of
the remaining CO₂ in the secondary electrochemical loop, as illustrated in the process flow diagram
in Exhibit 3.6.1.

 42

Exhibit 3.6.1 – Detailed Block Flow Diagram of Dual Loop Carbon Capture Process

 Exhibit 3.6.2 – Summary of LCOE and Cost of Capture Analysis

 43

 Regarding capital and operating expenditures, the total Bare Erect Cost (BEC) for the
primary capture and compression systems is estimated at $249.3 million, while the secondary loop
capture and hydrogen processing systems add $72.4 million. The total plant Levelized Cost of
Electricity (LCOE) is estimated at 71.7 $/MWh, inclusive of CO₂ transport and storage costs, as
shown in the cost summary in Exhibit 3.6.2. Based on the base case analysis, the cost of CO₂
capture is $59.3/tonne and the cost of CO₂ avoided is $71.5/tonne. These figures reflect the direct
economic costs of the capture system relative to the non-capture baseline, without applying credits
for the environmental benefit of the hydrogen co-product.
 The economic outlook improves when accounting for the value of the co-product. The
secondary loop co-produces high-purity hydrogen, and when this hydrogen is valued as a
displacement for conventional hydrogen production via Steam Methane Reforming (SMR), it
offsets costs significantly. As illustrated in Exhibit 3.6.3, the revenue or cost offset provided by
the H₂ byproduct (estimated to offset ~25 MWe of equivalent power consumption) helps reduce
the effective net capture cost, although the primary cost driver remains the capital expenditure of
the primary loop.
 Finally, a sensitivity study varying the primary loop capture rate between 95% and 99%
(while maintaining 99.8% overall capture) revealed that the total plant cost is minimized when the
primary loop captures 97% of the CO₂. As shown in Exhibit 3.6.4, at this optimum, the overall
cost of capture drops to a minimum of $69.2/tonne, suggesting that pushing the primary thermal
loop slightly harder is more economical than relying more heavily on the secondary
electrochemical loop.

 Exhibit 3.6.3 – Sensitivity of LCOE and Cost of Capture to Hydrogen Price

 44

 Exhibit 3.6.4 – Cost of Capture at Varying Primary Loop Capture Rates

 Although the long-term objective of this technology is to reduce capital cost substantially
relative to conventional high-capture rate NGCC carbon capture systems, the present project
should be viewed primarily as a bench-scale technical validation and system-integration effort
rather than a final commercial design exercise. Accordingly, the 50% capital-cost reduction target
was not fully demonstrated in a direct, plantwide commercial sense in the present report due to the
lack of realistic reference case that has capability to maintain the capture efficiency 99.8% over a
1000-hour operation. However, the project established a credible basis for meaningful capital-cost
reduction through the dual-loop architecture, which separates bulk CO2 removal from deep
polishing and thereby avoids forcing a single thermochemical loop to achieve the full 99.8%
capture duty.
 The techno-economic analysis indicates that the dual-loop concept is already competitive
at very high capture levels. For the base case with 99.8% overall capture, the cost of capture is
estimated at $59.3/tonne and the cost of CO2 avoided is $71.5/tonne. The analysis further shows
that cost is sensitive to how the CO2-removal duty is distributed between the primary and
secondary loops, with the minimum cost identified when the primary loop captures approximately
97% of the CO2 while maintaining 99.8% overall capture. This result indicates that future capital-
cost reduction will depend not simply on making each subsystem larger or smaller, but on
optimizing the integration between the two loops so that the polishing loop is used only for the
portion of the duty where it creates the greatest process value.
 Several design changes offer a practical pathway toward achieving larger capital-cost
reductions in future scale-up. First, the primary loop should be further optimized to operate in the
95–97% capture range, where it can leverage the lower-cost thermochemical separation for the
majority of the capture duty while avoiding overdesign associated with pushing the thermal loop
alone to ultradeep capture. Second, the secondary loop should be redesigned at larger scale around
the lessons learned from this project, including the use of corrosion-resistant titanium-based
electrodes, catalytic IrO2/PtO2-coated mesh to reduce overpotential, and improved stack sealing to

 45

eliminate catholyte/anolyte cross-mixing. Third, future absorber and solvent-distribution design
should reduce the amount of equipment and internal circulation required in the polishing loop
while preserving the high local mass-transfer rate needed for deep polishing. Fourth, additional
process integration, including tighter thermal integration and commercial-scale equipment layout
optimization, should reduce both balance-of-plant cost and auxiliary load. Finally, the hydrogen
co-product provides an additional economic lever that can offset part of the effective cost burden
of the secondary loop. Collectively, these modifications define the most credible future pathway
toward approaching the long-term capital-cost target.
 The TEA indicates that the thermochemical primary loop provides the lower-cost bulk CO₂
removal, while the electrochemical polishing loop is most valuable when used for the final deep-
removal needed to reach high capture rate or net-negative carbon emission. The sensitivity analysis
further shows that the overall cost is minimized by balancing these two loops rather than
maximizing the duty of either loop alone.
3.7 Life Cycle Assessment
 The Life Cycle Assessment (LCA) was performed in accordance with ISO 14040/14044
requirements to assess the cradle-to-gate environmental impacts of the dual-loop CCS system. The
functional unit for the study was 1 MWh of electricity delivered. As illustrated in the system
boundary diagram in Exhibit 3.7.1, the study boundaries included upstream material extraction,
construction and plant operation.

Exhibit 3.7.1 – LCA System Boundary Diagram
 The dual-loop system demonstrates the capability to achieve significant reductions in
carbon intensity. The primary results, summarized in Exhibit 3.7.2, indicate that without hydrogen
displacement, the total Global Warming Potential (GWP) is 52 kg CO₂-eq/MWh. In this scenario,
upstream natural gas leakage (assumed at 0.9%) is the single largest contributor to the carbon
footprint, outweighing infrastructure impacts by a factor of ten. However, when the system is
credited for displacing hydrogen produced via Steam Methane Reforming (SMR), the GWP is
further reduced to 42 kg CO₂-eq/MWh.

 46

Exhibit 3.7.2 – Contribution Analysis of GWP Drivers, accounting for impact from built
environment, 0.4% natural gas leakage, CO2 removed from inlet air, and H2 product displacement
 The analysis confirms that the upstream methane emissions from the natural gas supply
chain dominate the overall GWP of the facility, while operational emissions are negligible due to
the high capture rate (99.8%). Consequently, the environmental superiority of this technology is
robust, but the final "net negative" magnitude is sensitive to the assumed leakage rate of the natural
gas infrastructure. As shown in Exhibit 3.7.2, the hydrogen co-product provides significant
environmental credit, further lowering the carbon intensity of the electricity produced.
 The LCA indicates that the remaining life-cycle carbon emissions are driven mainly by
upstream natural-gas-related emissions, especially methane leakage, rather than by construction
materials or capture-system infrastructure. Therefore, further reductions in the carbon intensity of
the developed dual-loop system would depend not only on capture performance, but also on
upstream fuel supply conditions, methane management, process integration, and the useful
displacement credit from hydrogen co-production.

3.8 EH&S
 An Environmental, Health, and Safety (EH&S) assessment was conducted to evaluate risks
associated with the solvent, electrode materials, and long-term operation. The assessment did not
identify any barriers to commercial deployment; however, it highlighted specific operational
considerations.
• Ammonia (NH₃): Gas-side analysis using FTIR confirmed that NH₃ emissions increase linearly
 with both solvent circulation rate (~0.11 ppm per lb/hr increase) and stripper bottom
 temperature (~1.43 ppm per °C increase). Control of these parameters is critical for managing
 emissions.
• Solvent Degradation: Liquid-side analysis identified Urea, AMEA2P, and MeOZD as
 degradation products. While Urea formation was temperature-independent, the formation of
 AMEA2P and MeOZD increased significantly when stripper temperatures exceeded 130°C.

 47

 Regular solvent reclaiming and temperature monitoring are recommended mitigation
 strategies.
• The use of titanium electrodes in the electrochemical regenerator was validated as a safe and
 durable alternative to nickel-based alloys, which were prone to corrosion and flow-channel
 clogging. No safety incidents regarding electrode failure or leakage were recorded during the
 2,000-hour testing campaign with the optimized materials.
 Additional details of the environmental, health, and safety assessment are provided in
Appendix A.
4. CONCLUSION
 The project successfully advanced the technical maturity of the dual-loop CO₂ capture system,
confirming its capability to meet DOE goals for deep decarbonization (>99%) with high reliability.
The key conclusions from experimental and economic validation are:
1. Experimental Validation via Long-Term Testing: The design, build, and commissioning of the
 pilot facility were successfully completed, supporting a robust 2,000-hour testing campaign.
 This included 1,000 hours of steady-state operation where the system achieved 99.9% capture
 efficiency and demonstrated negligible solvent degradation.
2. Emission Control & System Stability: The pilot unit demonstrated superior operational
 stability compared to single-loop systems. The secondary electrochemical loop acted as a
 robust "polishing buffer," effectively managing transient fluctuations to ensure compliant
 emission levels (<100 ppm) were maintained continuously. This stability was further
 evidenced by the electrochemical cell's durability, which showed a conductivity decrease of
 less than 10% over 1,000 hours of operation. Ammonia and aerosol emissions were effectively
 controlled, with the water wash system reducing aerosol carryover to <1 ppm.
3. Economic Competitiveness: The economic analysis identified a distinct optimal operating
 window. While the base case was validated at $59.3/tonne, sensitivity studies show that
 increasing the primary loop capture rate to 97% further reduces the cost of capture to
 $69.2/tonne, making deep decarbonization economically comparable to standard 90% capture
 baselines.
4. Net-Negative Environmental Potential: The LCA confirmed that the technology dramatically
 reduces the carbon intensity of power generation. The cradle-to-gate analysis yielded a GWP
 of 52 kg CO₂-eq/MWh, and the co-production of hydrogen provides a significant
 environmental credit, lowering the net impact to 42 kg CO₂-eq/MWh.

5. AREAS OF FUTURE INTEREST
 To further advance this technology toward commercial deployment (TRL 7+), the following
areas are recommended for future research and development:
1. Optimization of Bicarbonate Formation via Load Balancing: The current polishing loop
 operation favors carbonate formation (K2CO3) due to high alkalinity. Future research should
 investigate shifting the primary/secondary load split to increase the CO₂ concentration entering
 the polishing loop. Operating in a regime that favors bicarbonate (KHCO3) formation would
 theoretically reduce the electrochemical regeneration demand from 2 moles of KOH to 1 mole
 of KOH per mole of CO₂. Pilot data has already validated this pathway: when the polishing
 loop influent concentration was increased to ~1,435 ppm, the calculated energy consumption
 dropped significantly to ~650 kJ mol⁻¹.

 48

2. Hydraulic Optimization for Low-Flow Polishing: Modeling indicates that the chemical
 optimum for the polishing loop is a "Low Circulation" regime (L/G ≈ 0.1). Future work should
 focus on validating Wire Gauze Structured Packing and high-precision liquid distributors (e.g.,
 drip-point designs) to physically enable this low-flow hydraulic regime at commercial scale.
 This design choice is critical to ensuring 100% surface wetting and preventing the channeling
 observed in smaller pilot columns.
3. Electrochemical Cell Durability & Impurity Management: Long-term durability testing of the
 electrochemical regeneration cell is recommended to characterize membrane lifespan under
 continuous industrial conditions. Future research should specifically evaluate the cell's
 tolerance to trace impurities (e.g., amine aerosols or degradation products) that may carry over
 from the primary loop and impact cell voltage.
4. Dynamic Load Following: As grid demands evolve, future pilot campaigns should quantify
 the transient response of the dual-loop system. Specifically, research should characterize the
 secondary loop's capacity to ramp hydrogen production up or down in response to renewable
 energy availability while maintaining strict CO₂ compliance (>99% capture).
5. Hydrogen Subsystem Integration: Engineering studies are needed to optimize the balance-of-
 plant systems for hydrogen purification, compression, and storage, ensuring that the co-product
 generates maximum economic value (offsetting ~25 MWe of equivalent power consumption)
 in a commercial utility setting.

 ABSTRACT

An Environmental, Health, and Safety (EH&S) Assessment was completed for an advanced carbon dioxide
(CO2) capture technology under the support of the United States Department of Energy (U.S. DOE) for a
dual-loop carbon capture system (CCS) developed by the University of Kentucky Institute for
Decarbonization and Energy Advancement (UKy IDEA) for the post-combustion carbon dioxide (CO 2)
capture from natural gas combined cycle (NGCC) power plant flue gas.

The objective of this EH&S Assessment was to determine if there are any unacceptable EH&S risks that
may prevent the requisite environmental permitting and commercial deployment of a full-scale dual-loop
CCS Facility at a greenfield NGCC power plant located in the Midwestern United States. If significant
concerns were identified, the EH&S Assessment was to include possible mitigation measures for UKy IDEA
to incorporate into future CCS designs and operating practices.

This CCS Technology EH&S Risk Assessment evaluated EH&S issues relating to the solvent, electrodes, and
their degradation during long-term operation of the UKy IDEA bench-scale CCS plus further extrapolated
to commercial-scale application. The purpose of the EH&S Assessment was to assess the environmental
friendliness and safety of any future process based on the materials and process studied at the
bench-scale and to determine whether there are any unanticipated or uncontrolled EH&S risks which
would impede commercialization of the dual-loop CCS technology. The evaluation included identification
of risks related to CCS process materials, air quality management considerations, water management
considerations, employee hazards, impact mitigation strategies, and precautions/accidental release
measures for process-generated materials.

The EH&S Assessment did not identify any obvious concerns or apparent barriers for the commercial
deployment of a UKy IDEA full-scale dual-loop CCS (CCS Facility). Commercial operations of a CCS Facility
would involve more extensive safety precautions in equipment design, operation, and storage facilities;
however, these aspects are not unique and can be managed in a safe and efficient manner following
established and emerging standards. Additional testing on a large-pilot scale is recommended to better
qualify the EH&S impacts of a commercial-scale system. Air, water, wastewater, and waste requirements
should be refined when a complete engineering design package is available for a site-specific commercial
CCS Facility. Environmental permits and authorization will need to be obtained prior to commencing
construction of a greenfield power plant in the Midwestern United States equipped with a CCS Facility.
