 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

This EH&S Assessment was prepared by ALL4 LLC (ALL4) for the UKy IDEA Project “Dual-loop
Solution-based CCS for Net Negative CO2 Emissions with Lower Costs” (Project) funded by a grant from
the U.S. DOE National Energy Technology Laboratory (U.S. DOE/NETL). The EH&S Assessment evaluated
the environmental friendliness and safety of future CCS processes for NGCC power plant flue gas using
the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Proprietary Amine Solvent (UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent) with two solvent
absorption/regeneration loops (dual-loop).

The objective of this EH&S Assessment was to determine if there are any unacceptable EH&S risks that
may prevent the requisite environmental permitting and commercial deployment of a full-scale dual-loop
CCS Facility at a greenfield NGCC power plant located in the Midwestern United States. The scope of the
EH&S assessment was limited to a desktop review evaluating process design plans, process simulation
data, testing information provided by UKy IDEA, and a literature review. Information provided by UKy IDEA
included: 1) process flow diagrams (PFDs); 2) CCS design and operating parameters; 3) raw material
storage and consumption rates; 4) air emissions testing from bench and pilot-scale CCS; 5) solvent
analytical testing; 6) quantification and characterization data of waste potentially generated; and
7) estimated wastewater discharges. At the time of this assessment, a complete engineering design
package for commercial scale deployment was not available. Therefore, this EH&S Assessment did not
consider detailed equipment sizing; utility flow diagrams; piping and instrumentation diagrams (P&IDs);
final layout drawings; complete engineered process and utility equipment lists; single line electrical
diagrams; resourcing and workforce plans; or a constructability review.

To evaluate potential health risks associated with the UKy IDEA CCS technology, an assessment of the
toxicity and potential health risks related to the process equipment materials was conducted. The results
of the assessment demonstrate that the UKy IDEA CCS technology poses little human toxicological or
ecological risk. There is potential for acute and chronic hazards from the amine-based solvent used in the
CCS process; however, these pose little human health or ecological risk when proper safety, handling, and
industrial hygiene procedures are followed. Additional testing on large-pilot scale projects would be
beneficial to refine estimates for emissions and EH&S impacts prior to full-scale deployment.

Offsite transportation of product gases captured by the CCS Facility were evaluated for rail, barge, and
pipeline. Construction and operating authorization considerations were provided for each mode under

66

 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

consideration. Evaluation of additional EH&S impacts for CO2 offsite transport via pipeline will be required
based on site-specific conditions that could not be evaluated under this assessment.

Air emissions from solvent and solvent degradation products are expected from the CCS Facility.
Calculation methods used in this assessment produced emissions estimates for order-of-magnitude
impacts; however, further evaluation and testing at a large-scale CCS is recommended to quantify air
emissions more accurately. Validation of process data for gas flow rates, solvent liquid flow rates, stack
parameters (height, diameter, gas velocity), flue gas composition, and abnormal operating conditions (i.e.,
startup, shutdown, and varying load demands) for the CCS Facility is recommended following completion
of an engineering design package to accurately quantify risks/impacts to human health or the
environment for a commercial-scale CCS Facility. Installation and operation of the full-scale CCS Facility
will be subject to Clean Air Act (CAA) New Source Review (NSR) permitting requirements including
preconstruction NSR applicability tests prior to commencement of the project. The CCS Facility is
projected to control CO2 emissions to levels below major NSR emissions thresholds while meeting the
stringent CO2 emissions limits for new combustion turbines (CTs) effective beginning January 2032 under
United States Environmental Protection Agency (U.S. EPA) Clean Air Act (CAA) regulations found at 40 CFR
Part 63.

Water usage and wastewater discharges are expected to increase with the operation of the CCS Facility
compared to an equivalent NGCC power plant without a CCS Facility. The increase in water consumption
will trigger additional permit requirements for water withdrawal and for wastewater discharge through a
National Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (NPDES) permit; however, site-specific permitting
requirements were not considered under this evaluation because geographic siting of the CCS Facility, on
a watershed basis, is particularly relevant and may have significant implications on the ability of a
particular discharge to be permitted. Future characterization of discharges at bench-scale is
recommended to further identify and characterize potential degradation products that may impede
commercialization. The analysis of water-lean carbon capture solvent after a 2,000-hour long-term testing
campaign on the UKy IDEA bench-scale CCS did not identify a degradation product that is anticipated to
impede commercialization. However, due to the novelty of the technology at commercial scale, its
implementation at an existing facility may result in scrutiny from regulators that are evaluating the
proposed technology under the current federal Effluent Limitation Guidelines (ELG) for NGCC power
plants. Additionally, the potential for the implementation of a site-specific Best
Professional
67

 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

Judgement (BPJ) limit introduces the risk of inconsistent implementation of CCS Facility discharge limits
across geographies, including on a state-to-state basis within the Midwest. However, this risk does not
represent an insurmountable obstacle that would impede commercialization of the technology evaluated.
The indirect discharge of as many CCS Facility wastewater streams as practicable to a Publicly Owned
Treatment Works (POTW) is anticipated to provide the most favorable regulatory conditions for operating
the proposed technology on a commercial scale.

Wastes from raw material storage would primarily consist of out of specification, spilled, or excess
materials. Wastes from chemicals used in the CCS Facility will consist primarily of reclaimer sludge, spent
filters, spent GAC cartridges, and miscellaneous maintenance lubricants and fluids. Some materials may
be corrosive or contain toxic chemicals at concentrations warranting disposal as a hazardous waste that
should be readily managed and disposed of or recycled according to applicable state and Resource
Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) regulations. Anticipated waste types and volumes estimated for
commercial deployment are not atypical for a power generating station and do not represent a unique
environmental concern. These wastes can be minimized by using a well-managed material handling
protocols and procurement practices to avoid spills, contamination, and excessive inventory requiring
disposal.

The UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent has been determined to pose little human toxicological or ecological
risk in its raw material form. A preliminary risk assessment of degradation products identified from bench
and pilot scale studies of analogous amin-based carbon capture solvents suggested no substantial
potential health risk. Ammonia presents a non-cancer health risk and was detected in air emissions from
prior bench-scale research projects. However, this EH&S assessment did not quantify CCS Facility
ammonia emissions in air or wastewater streams because the previous order-of-magnitude emissions
estimates completed in past EH&S assessments for UKy IDEA CCS technologies indicated that offsite
exposures to ammonia did not present an uncontrolled risk that could impede commercialization of this
CCS technology. Appropriate employee health and safety precautions should be employed to minimize
exposure and protective equipment should be used when exposure to ammonia emissions may occur.

Generation of significant quantities of nitrosamines was deemed unlikely when using the UKy IDEA
Water-Lean Solvent at bench-scale. Prior studies identified no detected nitrosamines in air emissions;
however, the level of detection was higher than risk levels identified by the U.S. EPA for worker
exposure

68

 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

over a 25-year lifetime. Prior preliminary calculations using half of the level of detection for several
potential nitrosamine degradation compounds suggested it was unlikely exposures for the bench scale
operation would exceed U.S. EPA recognized acceptable risk levels. Similarly, preliminary calculations
suggested longer term worker exposure at a commercial scale facility may be within acceptable risk levels;
however, confirmation of actual emissions at commercial scale needs to be further evaluated and proper
use of appropriate personal protective equipment (PPE) is still recommended.

This evaluation did not identify any unusual or significant environmental, health or safety concerns for the
bench-scale study or extrapolated to a commercial scale dual-loop CCS Facility using assumptions based
upon currently available information.

69

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

1. INTRODUCTION

1.1 PROJECT FUNDING AND DESCRIPTION

This EH&S Assessment was funded by a grant from the U.S. DOE-NETL Project Number DE-FE0032134. The
project consists of the development and bench-scale testing of a dual-loop CO 2 capture process for NGCC
power plant flue gas with 99.8+% CO2 capture efficiency, a 50% reduction in capital cost (as compared to
the U.S. DOE/NETL B31B Case on the primary loop), and hydrogen (H2) production with negative CO2
emissions that offset the operating cost for the technology’s commercial deployment.

1.2 PROJECT PURPOSE AND OBJECTIVES

The specific Project objectives include: 1) design, retrofit, and research a dual-loop CCS on the existing
University of Kentucky (UK) 0.1 megawatt-thermal (MWth) bench-scale facility using natural gas-derived
flue gas, augmented to match NGCC CO2 and oxygen (O2) concentrations; 2) to conduct a techno-economic
analysis (TEA) to document the benefits of the proposed technology and identify technology gaps for the
next scale of development; 3) assess EH&S issues relating to the solvent, electrodes, and their degradation
during long-term operation, and further extrapolate to commercial-scale application; 4) conduct a life
cycle analysis (LCA) to document the process practical lifetime; and 5) develop a technology maturation
plan (TMP) for future technology development.

1.3 EH&S ASSESSMENT PURPOSE, PROCESS, AND OBJECTIVES

The purpose of the EH&S Assessment was to assess the environmental and safety implications of any
future process based on the materials and processes in this project and to determine whether there are
any unanticipated or uncontrolled EH&S risks which would impede commercialization of the CCS. The
process for assessing EH&S risks associated with the dual-loop CSS included:

  A literature review to identify potential EH&S hazards of equipment and materials used in the
 process, as well as a review of information available for similar operations.

  Analysis of historic and on-going research data from CCS studies performed by UKy IDEA.

 1-1
70

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

  Analysis and extrapolation of test data from UKy IDEA bench-scale and small-pilot evaluations to
 assess potential health risks at the power plant configuration used as the design basis for the UKy
 IDEA dual-loop CCS.

  Desktop review of design-basis documents for the research study that included a process
 description, commercial-scale process flow diagram, and anticipated quantities of materials used
 in the process or managed onsite. Specifically, this EH&S Assessment is based upon the following
 documents provided by UKy IDEA:

 o “Dual-Loop Solution-Based CCS for Net Negative CO2 Emissions with Lower Costs –
 Techno-Economic Analysis” (Draft Report, September, 2025) (TEA Report);

 o “Monthly Technical Report August 2025” (received via email September 4, 2025);

 o “Quarterly Research Performance Progress Report Q3FY25” (dated July 25, 2025);

 o “Quarterly Research Performance Progress Report Q2FY25” (dated April 30, 2025);

 o “Quarterly Research Performance Progress Report Q1FY25” (dated January 29, 2025);

 o “Quarterly Research Performance Progress Report Q4FY24” (dated October 24, 2024);

 o “Technology Maturation Plan – Dual-loop Solvent-based CCS for Net Negative CO 2
 Emissions with Lower Costs” (dated May 24, 2024); and

 o “Dual-Loop Solution-Based CCS for Net Negative CO2 Emissions with Lower Costs –
 Process Design Package” (dated September 30, 2022).

The objective of this EH&S Assessment was to determine if there are any unacceptable EH&S risks that
may prevent the requisite environmental permitting and commercial deployment of a full-scale dual-loop
CCS Facility at a greenfield NGCC power plant located in the Midwestern United States. If significant
concerns were identified, the EH&S Assessment was to include possible mitigation measures for UKy IDEA
to incorporate into future CCS designs and operating practices.

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

2. SITING CONSIDERATIONS FOR COMMERCIALIZATION

The commercial deployment of a UKy IDEA full-scale dual-loop CCS (CCS Facility) at a NGCC Electric
Generating Unit (EGU) located at a greenfield power plant in the Midwestern United States (Site) is the
design basis for this report. The Site characteristics are based upon information provided in the TEA Report
prepared by the Electric Power Research Institute, Inc. (EPRI) [1]. The CCS Facility would be integrated
with NGCC EGU operations at the Site described in Table 2-1. The gross and net power of the hypothetical
NGCC plant is 697 and 597 MWe, respectively [1]. The NGCC EGU consists of two 2017 F-class Combustion
Turbine Generators (CTGs), Heat Recovery Steam Generators (HSRGs) and one Steam Turbine Generator
(STG) in a multi-shaft 2×2×1 configuration. EH&S siting considerations for commercial deployment at the
hypothetical greenfield site are discussed in the following subsections.

2.1 LOCATION AND LAND USE CONSIDERATIONS

The site selection process should identify property parcel(s) isolated and distant from cultural,
recreational, and environmentally sensitive features using an established due diligence process in general
conformance with the American Society for Testing and Materials Standard Practice for Environmental

 2-1
72

Site Assessments: Phase I Environmental Site Assessment Process E1527-21 (ASTM E1527-21) and the “All
Appropriate Inquiries” regulations found at 40 CFR Part 312. This process, commonly referred to as a
“Phase I ESA” is conducted to identify “recognized environmental conditions” that could prevent
development of the Site for power generation with carbon capture. Conducting a Phase I ESA will allow
for selection of a site that does not have existing obstacles to development. Using a proper due diligence
process will result in selection of a property where no significant impacts to cultural, recreational, or
environmentally sensitive features are anticipated.

2.2 PRODUCT TRANSPORT

The CCS Facility will produce three product gases for offsite sale and transport that include carbon dioxide
(CO2), hydrogen (H2), and oxygen (O2). For captured CO2, this EH&S Assessment considers pipeline
transport of CO2 to an offsite saline formation for long-term, permanent storage of CO 2 captured from the
Site. Hydrogen produced in the secondary electrochemical capture loop of the CCS Facility can offset 25
MWe power if used for electricity generation [1]. For H2 produced by the CCS Facility and not utilized
onsite, the Site will transport saleable pipeline-grade hydrogen by rail or truck. For oxygen (O 2) produced
by the CCS Facility and not utilized onsite, the Site will transport saleable pipeline-grade O 2 by rail or truck.
Onsite storage and offsite transport of H2, O2, and CO2 were not evaluated using a detailed engineering
analysis in the TEA Report; therefore, this EH&S Assessment considers general EH&S considerations for
product transport of pressurized gases (see Section 5.5.1).

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

3. PROCESS DESCRIPTION

The proposed technology includes two solvent absorption/regeneration loops. The dual-loop CCS Facility
will include the installation of a series of process and storage vessels, pumps, heat exchangers, plumbing,
ductwork, and support features.

The proposed technology addresses the inherent technical challenges associated with CO 2 capture from
low CO2 (approximately 4%, by volume) and high O2 (approximately 12%, by volume) concentrations in
NGCC flue gas using a dual-loop process that yields very high CO2 capture efficiency (greater than 99%)
and a 20% cost reduction from U.S. DOE NETL Reference Case B31B [2], while also lowering capital cost
on the primary loop and offsetting operating costs with negative CO 2 emissions and H2 production.

The major system components of the primary UKy IDEA Water-Lean Proprietary Amine Solvent (UKy IDEA
Water-Lean Solvent) capture loop (Primary Loop) include: 1) direct contact coolers (DCCs); 2) primary
absorber, water wash, and stripper columns with reboilers and reclaimers; and 3) CO 2 processing train.

Major system components for the secondary electrochemical capture loop (Secondary Loop) include:
1) a potassium hydroxide absorber (KOH absorber); 2) an electrochemical regenerator; 3) O 2/CO2
processing train; and 4) H2 processing train.

A brief description of the dual-loop CCS process flow is provided in the following subsections. A simplified
block flow diagram is provided in Figure 3-1.

 3-1

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

3.1 PRIMARY WATER-LEAN AMINE SOLVENT CAPTURE LOOP

The following subsections describe the major system components of the Primary Loop utilizing the
UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent. A PFD for the Primary Loop is provided as Figure 3-2.

3.1.1 Direct Contact Coolers

Cooling of the flue gas entering the Primary Loop will be accomplished with an in-duct and a short packing
cooler directly attached to the flue gas source. Cooling water will cycle through the DCC associated with
the absorber. The CTGs and HRSGs will be operated with back pressure of approximately 30 inches of
water column, driving the flue gas through the CCS Facility. Taking advantage of the NGCC positive
pressure operational nature, the flue gas will enter the Primary Loop at approximately 90°C and 1.05 bar,
thus eliminating the need for a blower and reducing the cooling tower duty. This configuration is
advantageous to minimize maintenance and downtime while the potential vacuum pressure applied to
the HRSGs is avoided. The flue gas will then be cooled by downward in-duct direct contact to 45°C before
entering the primary absorber.

3.1.2 Primary Absorber/Water Wash/Stripper Columns

After exiting the DCC, the flue gas will enter the primary absorber column from the bottom, move through
the primary absorber filled with UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent, exit the top of the primary absorber
column, move to the water wash column, and then ultimately is directed to the secondary loop for further
CO2 removal after another gas entrainment clean up. The primary absorber can capture greater than 95%
of the CO2 with the use of staged UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent feeds, which will inject UKy IDEA Water-
Lean Solvent into the primary absorber and stripper to achieve CO 2 capture while lowering UKy IDEA
Water-Lean Solvent degradation and the energy requirement. The UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent will be
delivered by rail or truck and stored in an Aboveground Storage Tank(s) (AST). The UKy IDEA Water-Lean
Solvent will then be pumped directly to the absorber.

In the primary absorber, 80% of the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent will be fed to the middle, capturing
approximately 80% of the CO2, while 20% of UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent will be fed to the top as a
polishing step, capturing the remaining approximately 15% of the CO2. The primary absorber temperature

 3-3
76

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

profile will be controlled through the feed stream temperatures, as in the case of intercooling, to enhance
the absorption driving force, which results in an effective, short column. The primary absorber is kept
short (less than approximately 12 meters packing) by maximizing the liquid/gas contact area and utilizing
the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent and one-stage bubble tray or short structure packing as the
top stage-feed for approximately 15% of total CO2 captured, and by controlling the temperature profile
(30-50°C) with the second stage-feed, approximately 80% of UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent flow, and
structure packing. Treated flue gas will exit the top of the primary absorber, flow through water wash,
achieving less than 1 part per million (ppm) amine entrainment and aerosols, and be directed to a
Secondary Loop for further CO2 removal. Plant water will be sent directly to the water wash.

Rich UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent will leave the primary absorber and be sent through heat exchangers
to the primary stripper. The rich UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent will be split into two streams prior to
entering the primary stripper: a portion will be introduced at the top of the primary stripper at a lower
temperature, and the remaining will be introduced in the middle of the column at higher temperature to
minimize the overhead stream water CO2 content and reduce the required regeneration energy. UKy IDEA
Water-Lean Solvent will be fed to the primary stripper at two points over structured packing to minimize
the H2O/CO2 vapor ratio at the primary stripper exhaust (the top feed stream) and to reduce the reboiler
specific duty by introducing a second vapor stream (middle feed stream). It is a similar arrangement to
other advanced warm bypass configurations, but the Primary Loop produces a two-phase flow at the high
temperature end of the lean/rich heat exchanger. In the primary stripper, approximately 80% of UKy IDEA
Water-Lean Solvent will be regenerated to a higher lean loading (0.22-0.28 C/N) and a minimum amount
of UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent, approximately 20%, will be regenerated to the extra lean loading
required for the high capture efficiency, thus minimizing degradation and the energy consumption. A
typical rich UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent loading (2.3-2.5 mol C/kg solution) will be maintained to keep
the regeneration energy low under the UKy IDEA rich split arrangement [1,150 British thermal units per
pound (Btu/lb) CO2]. UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent will exit the primary stripper and cycle back to the
primary absorber through the same set of heat exchangers.

Heat will be recovered from two sources: 1) the primary stripper overhead condenser heat is used to heat
the rich UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent, reducing solvent regeneration energy; and 2) reboiler condensate
sensible heat is used to preheat a portion of the rich UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent to be fed to the primary
stripper. Each will further reduce the reboiler specific duty, cooling tower duty, and improve overall plant

 3-4
77

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

efficiency. This will further lower the reboiler specific duty, to 1,075 Btu/lb CO 2 in design condition. The
last feature of the Primary Loop technology is that hybrid degas and activated carbon beds will be installed
on the primary stripper overhead condensation line to remove volatile organic compounds (VOC) and
impurities in the stripper condensate. The steam supply to the Primary Loop is a combination of low
pressure (LP) drum saturated steam, intermediate pressure (IP) superheat steam, and hot reheat steam
after a laydown turbine or a steam-driving CO2 compressor.

To increase Primary Loop’s operational flexibility, the steam extraction used in the process is from the
HRSGs directly rather than from IP/LP cross-over. The steam supplied to the reboiler will come from three
locations: 1) LP drum with setpoint of pressure at 50 pounds per square inch absolute (psia); 2) IP steam
after IP superheater; and 3) hot reheat (HRH).

3.1.3 Primary Loop CO2 Processing Train

The CO2 removed from the rich UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent in the primary stripper will be sent to the
overhead condenser, where it will be cooled and then sent to the CO 2 compressor. The CO2 product
stream exiting the Primary Loop will be cooled to a temperature of approximately 40°C and will be at a
low pressure of approximately 30 psia. The cooled CO2 product stream from the Primary Loop will then
be compressed to increase the pressure of the CO2 to its critical point or liquification pressure, making it
suitable for transport via pipeline.

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

3.2 SECONDARY ELECTROCHEMICAL CAPTURE LOOP

The following subsections describe the major system components of the secondary electrochemical
capture loop (Secondary Loop). A simplified PFD for the Secondary Loop is provided as Figure 3-3.

3.2.1 Potassium Hydroxide Absorber

Flue gas exiting the Primary Loop from the water wash after demister goes to the KOH absorber. The KOH
absorber consists of a top-structured packing section as engagement zone and two sieve trays on the
bottom capable of removing the remaining CO2 to less than or equal to 100 parts per million (ppm). This
stage takes advantage of fog-formation using spray nozzles to achieve a liquid-to-gas contact surface area,
two to three times higher than that achieved with structured packing, and the fast reaction rate and
zero-CO2 partial pressure of KOH with CO2. A one-stage tray with spray will satisfy the need to capture the
remaining CO2 in the flue gas in the form of potassium carbonate (K2CO3). The K2CO3 rich solution is then
heated before being fed to the electrochemical regenerator.

Potassium for the Secondary Loop will be stored onsite as 99.5% KOH pellets and 99.5% potassium
bicarbonate (KHCO3) powder that will be mixed with reverse osmosis (RO) water to produce 3 Molar (M)
KOH and 1.5 M KHCO3 solutions [3]. These solutions are used to charge the electrochemical regenerator
during startup and for chemical make-up.

3.2.2 Electrochemical Regenerator

The K2CO3 rich solution is fed to the electrochemical regenerator at 15 bar and 70-80°C to produce H 2
(99.9% purity), O2/CO2 gas, KHCO3, and regenerated KOH. The electrochemical regenerator is a device
consisting of a stack of bipolar membrane cells designed to facilitate the separation of ionic species
through electrochemical reactions. The K 2CO3 rich solution, along with the KHCO3 recycle stream, is
introduced into the anode side of each cell, and electricity is applied to enable the dissociation of
carbonate ions (CO32+) into CO2. CO2 is then released in gas phase, while O2 gas is coproduced. Potassium
ions (K+) and water move through a cation selective membrane to the cathode side of the electrochemical
regenerator. On the cathode side, water splits into hydroxide (OH-) and hydrogen ions (H+), the former
combining with K+ to regenerate the KOH solution, while H+ ions form H2 gas to maintain chemical
equilibrium. Depending on external grid load demand, the KHCO 3 solution leaving the electrochemical cell

 3-7
80

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

anode is flashed at 7-10 bar while the gas stream containing CO 2 (70%) and O2 (30%) is processed further
to produce pipeline quality CO2 for off-site storage/sequestration and greater than 99% purity O 2 gas for
sale. The regenerated KOH from the cathode side of the electrochemical regenerator is returned to the
KOH absorber.

3.2.3 Secondary Loop O2/CO2 Processing Train

The Secondary Loop has its own O2/CO2 and H2 processing trains for purification. The O2/CO2 processing
train involves separating the CO2 and the O2, CO2 drying, cryogenic distillation, and pressurization of the
product CO2 to the CO2 product pressure in the Primary Loop then compressed to pipeline specifications
using same CO2 compressor. Oxygen will be stored in pressurized vessels onsite and transported via truck
or rail for industrial use/sale if preferred. Long-term storage of CO 2 will occur in an offsite saline formation.
While not specifically contemplated in the TEA Report, offsite transport of CO 2 is most likely to occur via
pipeline if implemented at a greenfield site in the Midwestern U.S. for transport to a saline formation.

3.2.4 Secondary Loop H2 Processing Train

For H2 produced by the CCS Facility and not utilized onsite, the Site will transport saleable pipeline-grade
hydrogen gas by rail or truck. The H2 gas cogenerated in the secondary loop is cooled, dried, and
compressed to transportation specifications. Detailed engineering and design specifications for this
component of the CCS Facility were not completed at the date of this assessment; therefore, general EH&S
considerations will be considered for representative equipment used to compress, store, and transport
H2 gas.

3.2.5 Secondary Loop Start-Up

The secondary Loop start-up procedure can be accomplished in two ways, which consume different
feedstocks. The two start-up procedures are as follows [3]:

Procedure 1
In this procedure, the Secondary Loop is batched with 1.5 M KHCO 3 and 3 M KOH. The cathode side of the
electrochemical regenerator and the KOH absorber are filled with KOH, and the anode side of the
electrochemical regenerator is filled with KHCO3. The KOH is then recirculated through the KOH absorber
in a closed loop, with a single pass of air, to build up the concentration of K 2CO3 in the system by absorbing

 3-8
81
ALL4 Project No. 001242-0004.00                                                                    November 2025

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

CO2, until the K/C ratio is approximately 2:1. The solution is then sent to the electrochemical regenerator,
where electricity will be applied to the cells and normal operation will begin [3].

Procedure 2
In this procedure, a K2CO3 solution, rather than KOH, is batched to the KOH absorber. This allows the initial
recirculation of KOH in the KOH absorber (as in Procedure 1) to be skipped, since the concentration of
K2CO3 will not need to be built up. In this start-up process, electricity can be applied immediately to the
ER and generation of O2, CO2, and H2 can begin [3].

82

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

4. LITERATURE REVIEW

A literature review was conducted to examine potential human health effects and ecotoxicity for
chemicals proposed for use in the primary and secondary loops of the CCS Facility as provided in
Subsections 4.1 and 4.2.

4.1 PRIMARY WATER-LEAN AMINE SOLVENT CAPTURE LOOP

Evaluations of the qualitative and quantitative health risks related to CO 2 capture and storage using amine
solvents to remove CO2 from post-combustion flue gas [4] [5] [6] were conducted under previous EH&S
assessment for similar carbon capture technologies. These evaluations were performed to support initial
CO2 capture pilot testing program and subsequent additional large-pilot scale testing scenarios for
solvent-based CCS units, as well as testing of various amine-based CO2 capture solvents. The evaluations
concluded that the amine/tetraglyme water lean solvent poses little human health or ecological risk when
proper safety, handling, and industrial hygiene procedures were followed. The solvents may cause
irritation to the eyes, skin, or respiratory system if direct contact occurs. It was also concluded that if
nitrosamines are formed as degradation products of the CO2 capture solvents, they may pose significant
human health and ecological risks if prolonged exposures are allowed to occur. Monitored results
identified no detectable quantities of nitrosamines; however, detection limits were higher than
potentially harmful concentrations of nitrosamines.

A literature review identified several reports that discussed potential health risks associated with amines
used in CO2 capture. A report by the Norwegian Institute of Public Health (NIPH) [7] presented health
effects information for monoethanolamine (MEA), piperazine, aminomethyl propanol (AMP), and
methyldiethanolamine (MDEA). The report concluded that all these amines appear to cause irritation, but
only piperazine was reported to be sensitizing (i.e., causing an allergic reaction). There were also
indications that amines may be reproductive and developmental toxins. There were no indications that
these amines are carcinogenic.

A report from the Norwegian Institute for Air Research (NILU) [8] also studied MEA, AMP, MDEA, and
piperazine. The study concluded that the amines themselves likely pose negligible risk to human health
but, if emitted, may contribute to nitrogen loading, which may cause eutrophication in sensitive

 4-1
84

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

ecosystems. Oxidative degradation products of the amines may be formed that include nitrosamines,
nitramines, aldehydes, and amides. Nitrosamines are a human health concern as they can be toxic and
carcinogenic at extremely low levels of exposure.

A report from the Bellona Foundation [9], also studied amine solvents used for CO2 capture. The study
distinguished between potential health risks related to the use of MEA compared to potential health risks
associated with AMP, MDEA, and piperazine. The report concluded that MEA has a higher biodegradability
than the other amines and would not be expected to have a direct adverse effect on human health or
ecological receptors. It may, however, cause eutrophication when emitted in high concentrations. The
other amines (AMP, MDEA, and piperazine) were thought to have a higher ecotoxicity, lower
biodegradability, and higher environmental impact when compared to MEA. The report also concluded
that most of the degradation products of the amines will not have environmental impacts. However,
nitrosamines are likely to be degradation products with the most adverse potential risks.

CCS have been studied for fossil fuel-fired boilers, and degradation of the CO 2 capture UKy IDEA Water-
Lean Solvent is anticipated to occur through irreversible side reactions with CO 2 and other flue gas
components [10]. The NILU study [8] also determined the potential human health and environmental
impacts of air emissions for several amines, including MEA, from carbon capture and storage operations.
The study concluded that the risk of exposure to MEA was low; however, the study acknowledged that
additional research was warranted to evaluate photooxidation of the amines in the atmosphere with NO X,
which may produce compounds like nitrosamines, nitramines, and amides, which are of higher concern
and may pose some risk to human health and the environment.

Additional research on solvent degradation and nitrosamine/nitramine formation in CCS was published in
Environmental Science & Technology [11]. In their paper, the authors describe the formation of
nitrosamines/nitramines derived from amine-based solvents in both the absorber and desorber/stripper
sections of a typical CCS. From their research, it was reported that amine order correlated with
nitrosamine formation, with secondary amines having the highest rate of nitrosamine formation, followed
by tertiary amines. Primary amines (as used in the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent) had the lowest
nitrosamine formation rate. This trend was apparent at both the absorber and desorber/stripper.
Nitrosamines generated in the absorber and desorber/stripper can accumulate in the wash water control
system due to mass transfer and form from reactions with residual NO x in the absorber exhaust.

 4-2
85

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Nitrosamines can enter the environment through air emissions and the disposal of spent solvents. If wash
water is disposed, it may also be an avenue for environmental introduction and potential impact. Potential
air emissions control measures evaluated were: 1) destroying nitrosamines in the desorber at higher
temperatures; 2) enhancing water wash performance with various in-line treatment systems to remove
accumulated nitrosamines/nitramines; and 3) preventing formation of nitrosamines through NO x removal
prior to CO2 capture. Each strategy imposes an additional cost or system burden. It was suggested in the
Environmental Science & Technology [11] study that the most promising strategy would be to remove
NOx prior to CO2 capture and to develop effective CO2 capture solvents with low nitrosamine/nitramine
formation potential.

In 2011, the environmental risks for carbon capture and storage were evaluated in the United Kingdom,
including amine solvent use. The conclusions by the Environment Agency were that “environmental risks
of CCS technologies can be controlled so that they are no higher than risks posed by the existing power
stations and industrial processes.” The staged implementation approach from laboratory-scale to
pilot-scale to demonstration-scale was identified as a key risk control measure [12].

Additional studies [13] [14] have evaluated nitrosamine emissions from CCS facilities. Based upon earlier
studies published by the Commonwealth Scientific and Industrial Research Organization (CSIRO) and the
NILU that reported nitrosamine emissions rates of 0.996 grams (g) of N-nitrosodimethlyamine
(NDMA)/tonne CO2 captured and 0.724 g of N-nitrosodiethylamine (NDEA)/tonne of CO2 captured for
post-combustion carbon capture systems (PCCCS), nitrosamine air emissions dispersion rates were
modeled in China [13] for different capacity CCS and various exhaust stack heights. Modeled results
indicated relatively low or no risk to onsite workers or surrounding communities for PCCCS scenarios at
or below 146,000 tonnes CO2 captured annually. However, this capture rate is substantially less than the
U.S. DOE/NETL reference Case B12B [2] that specifies removal of 3,282,103 tonnes/yr, an increase of
approximately 22 times from the modeled results. The target capture rate for the Site considered in this
report is 595,000 tonne/yr [1].

After additional evaluation of the toxicity of potential amines and derivatives associated with CO 2 capture,
the NIPH [15] recommended:

 “For compounds released from the CO2 capture plant, NIPH recommends that the risk
 estimate calculated for NDMA should be used for the total concentration of nitrosamines

 4-3
86

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

 and nitramines in air and water. We recommend maximum levels ensuring minimal or
 negligible risk of cancer for the public from exposure to these substances. NIPH therefore
 concludes that the total amount of nitrosamines and nitramines should not exceed 0.3
 ng/m3 (nanogram/cubic meter) in air.”

An article in Energy Procedia [16] summarizes environmental and health impact data gathered during an
extensive study of amine-based PCCCS through the Norwegian CLIMIT-program and the CO 2 Technology
Center Mongstad (TCM). Additionally, the report presents studies, models, and procedures which can be
used to qualify amines and related risks for any CO2 capture project. Experiment results indicated that
nitrosamines decompose more easily than nitramines in the environment. The authors concluded that
the secondary and tertiary amines had higher potency to form nitrosamines and nitramines than primary
amines (note: the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent is formulated with primary amines). The study reported
risk level comparisons of anticipated nitrosamines and nitramine concentrations based upon the TCM
process and updated dispersion model assumptions. The study identified reaction pathways of amines,
nitrosamines, and nitramines in both the gaseous and aqueous phase for formation and destruction
during day and night. They concluded that there are no important aqueous pathways, but they identified
formation and destruction reaction constituents in the gas phase. They cite studies that predict “the
maximum ambient air concentration of sum nitrosamines/nitramines can be reduced by a factor of 10
relative to the assumption of instant formation at the outlet of the stack,” thus concluding that
simultaneous dispersion and chemical transformation significantly limited the formation rates of
nitrosamines/nitramines.

An article in Energy Procedia [17] provides additional data regarding the Norwegian full-scale CO 2 capture
project TCM and describes processes to qualify amine-based technologies with respect to health and
environmental properties. The authors indicate that nitrosamines and nitramines are not likely to be
detectable in the environment. Thus, dispersion modeling is recommended to qualify impacts. Several
conclusions from the authors’ prior 2013 publication [16] are reiterated, and a description of a deposition
runoff model are provided to account for nitrosamines and nitramines transported to surface water. The
report refers to the Gassnova website collection of reports as the Amine Qualification Toolbox because it
offers specific processes and evaluation methodology to assess various amine CCS.

A review of literature and experimental data compiled by the Chemical Engineering Journal [18] included
a discussion of the formation, emissions, and degradation of N-nitrosamines and N-nitramines in

 4-4
87

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

amine-based PCCCS processes and potential methods for removal of these compounds from PCCCS waste.
This report stated N-nitrosamines and N-nitramines mainly accumulate in the absorber, stripper, and
water wash unit [19], and some fraction will be released to the environment through exhaust gas
emissions, wastewater discharge, and solid waste disposal [20]. Because many of these compounds are
probable human carcinogens, several strict wastewater discharge or drinking water limits have been
developed and were identified: 1) California Department of Public Health drinking water notification level
of 10 nanograms per liter (ng/L) for N-NDEA and NDMA; 2) Ministry of Environment of Ontario drinking
water limit of 1 ng/L for NDMA; and 3) Norwegian Climate and Pollution Agency suggested a wastewater
discharge permit limit for total N-nitrosamines and N-nitramines of 0.3 ng/m3 in downwind airsheds, and
4 ng/L in downwind water supplies for CO2 TCM.

A 2013 report published in Environmental Science & Technology [19] measured 59 micromoles (μmol) of
total N-nitrosamine and 0.23 μmol of 1,4-dinitropiperazine from pilot wash water samples from a system
operated with a 25% AMP/15% Piperazine (PZ) solvent, but only 0.73 μmol of total N-nitrosamine and
0.24 μmol of N-nitromonoethanolamine for a 35% MEA solvent [21]. With a 0.73 μmol total N-nitrosamine
concentration in the water wash unit [21] stated, an almost 25,000 times reduction is needed to meet the
proposed permit limits for downwind drinking water supplies for the permit in this study.

Potential control measures for N-nitrosamine and PZ solvent wash water included reference to a Stiftelsen
for industriell og teknisk forskning (SINTEF) report from Norway which identified ultraviolet (UV)
treatment as a feasible option for the treatment of wash water or emitted gas from PCCCS [22]. Such UV
treatment may avoid the accumulation of N-nitrosamines and N-nitramines in wash water. Application of
UV can destroy more than 90% of N-nitrosamines and N-nitramines in the wash water [23] [24].

A 2018 report [25] concluded that air emissions would not be anticipated to be harmful for the
environment, based upon studies regarding carbon capture applied in the refinery and petrochemical
industries yielding amine concentrations in air emissions of less than 0.2 ppm.

Increasingly, power plants must operate during periods of variable load. These flexible operating
conditions would require an integrated CCS Facility to also adjust its operational parameters accordingly,
which could result in intermittent or transient operations. In a 2021 study [26] evaluating air emissions
from a CCS operating during varying power loads at the Niederaussem 1,000 megawatts-electric (MWe)
lignite-fired power plant, it was observed that amine emissions were significantly higher under transient

 4-5
88

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

operation [AMP: 30 – 100 milligrams per cubic meter (mg/m 3); PZ: 2 – 30 mg/m3; and MEA: less than 8
mg/m3, all at standard temperature and pressure]. The CCS consisted of a conventional amine absorber
with packed beds, an intercooling system, and a water wash section. It was concluded that emissions
mitigation tools in addition to a single water wash section were necessary to reduce solvent emissions to
meet expected future emissions regulations. Results suggested monitoring as well as managing lean
solvent and water wash temperatures and flow rates to better control emissions.

Evaluations were conducted of the qualitative and quantitative health risks, air emissions, wastewater,
waste, employee hazards and safety, and permitting related to a compact absorber for post-combustion
CO2 capture in fossil-fuel power plants [27]. This preliminary analysis was based on a bench-scale analysis
scaling up to a commercial-scale of a 672 MWe PCCCS, little to no major environmental or safety issues
were found in this bench-scale assessment. This evaluation is a representative basis for material in this
current assessment for the CCS Facility.

A review of the tetraglyme safety data sheet (SDS) [28] found that exposure to tetraglyme present in the
solvent may impair fertility or cause harm to unborn children; however, the product does not contain any
hazardous materials with occupational exposure limits established by the U.S. regulatory bodies. While
this occupational exposure warning should be considered for Site personnel, the risk of exposure from
tetraglyme (and its degradation products) in ambient air under a lifetime inhalation exposure pathway is
not anticipated to exceed U.S. EPA benchmark risk thresholds (i.e., 1x10 -6 to 1x10-4).

4.2 SECONDARY ELECTROCHEMICAL CAPTURE LOOP

Several studies were identified that examined the potential human health effects and ecotoxicity related
to the materials present in the Secondary Loop (KOH, K 2CO3, KHCO3), as well as potential degradation
products of electrochemical regenerator electrodes. The literature review found that a KOH-based
polishing capture loop is a promising carbon-negative technology for hydrogen production [29], is
effective for KOH recovery [30], and can reduce operational costs of the carbon capture technology [31].
Nickel electrodes were found to have the potential to form nickel oxides and hydroxides, which are widely
used as electroactive materials in industrial applications [32].

KOH has a moderate acute oral toxicity due to its corrosivity; case reports on human accidents or
intentional exposure confirm that the risk posed by KOH for human health originates from its corrosive

 4-6
89

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

properties [33]. KOH was also found to have moderate acute toxicity based on results from three animal
studies in rats following oral exposure [34]. The median lethal dose (LD50) in rats is reported as 273–1230
mg/kg body weight (bw); In contrast, the LD50 value in rats for potassium chloride, 3000 mg/kg bw, is
much higher than that of KOH, indicating low toxicity of the potassium ion [35]. Under normal handling
and use conditions (non-irritating) neither the concentration of potassium in the blood nor the pH of the
blood will be increased above normal limits and therefore absorbed KOH is not expected to cause
systemically toxic levels in the blood of humans [33]. A systemic (non-acute) oral intoxication by KOH is
not expected [33].

Atmospheric emissions as KOH aerosols should be rapidly neutralized by carbon dioxide, as occurs with
sodium hydroxide (NaOH) or other acids and the salts (e.g. potassium carbonate) will be washed out by
rain.; For this reason potential atmospheric emissions of KOH are considered of no concern [33].
Significant emissions to the terrestrial environment are not expected during normal handling and use of
KOH; small terrestrial emissions will be neutralized by the buffer capacity of the soil [33]. For this reason,
the environmental assessment can be limited to the aquatic compartment [35].

With the sum of information of the studies on KOH, NaOH, KCl and K 2CO3, apart from the artefacts due to
high pH with KOH or NaOH, and high osmotic pressure with KCl, there is no evidence for a mutagenic
activity [33]. The major human health hazard (and the mode of action) of KOH is local irritation and/or
corrosion. Exposure via the environment is negligible, as the product is diluted in rivers and neutralized
by the bicarbonate buffering capacity or acids [33]. There is no evidence KOH to be carcinogenic in
exposure situations that are relevant for man [35].

 4-7
90

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

5. EH&S EVALUATION

An EH&S evaluation assessing the environmental friendliness and safety of a future commercial-scale CCS
Facility was performed considering air emissions, water management, waste management, employee
safety, accidental releases, and other regulatory considerations as described in Subsections 5.1 through 0.
An assessment of toxicological effects from commercial-scale CCS Facility operations is provided in
Subsection 5.6.

5.1 AIR QUALITY MANAGEMENT

The direct emissions associated with a commercial scale CCS Facility as part of a Greenfield NGCC EGU
plant (Greenfield Plant) are required be addressed in an initial preconstruction permit application for the
entire Site. The Greenfield Plant, referenced in the TEA Report, is described as “…the baseline F-class case
from DOE/NETL’s cost and performance baseline report rev4a (Case B31A and B31B)” [36]. The power
plant consists of two 2017 F-class CTGs, HSRGs and one STG in a multi-shaft 2×2×1 configuration located
in the Midwestern United States [36]. The air emissions associated with the Greenfield Plant, as presented
in DOE/NETL’s cost and performance baseline report rev4a (Baseline Report), reflect no sulfur dioxide
(SO2) emissions, no particulate matter (PM) emissions, 57 tons per year (tpy) for oxides of nitrogen (NO X),
and 204,176 tonnes per year of CO2 [36]. Based on ALL4’s experience with air permitting Greenfield NGCC
EGUs, the emissions performance data referenced in the Baseline Report does not reflect the potential to
emit (PTE) of a complete Greenfield NGCC EGU plant as it would be reflected in an application for a
preconstruction air quality permit. The potential emissions of regulated New Source Review (NSR)
pollutants associated with the Greenfield Plant, inclusive of the CCS Plant potential emissions, will dictate
the air permitting and regulatory requirements for the Project.

The direct emissions associated with a commercial scale CCS Facility under a retrofit at an existing NGCC
EGU plant (Retrofit Plant) will result in direct emissions associated with the CCS Facility itself (i.e., potential
solvent and electrolytic solution and decomposition products) in addition to emissions increases of
regulated new source review (NSR) pollutants from the “affected” or “modified” Retrofit Plant. The
increase in regulated NSR pollutants from a Retrofit Plant is due to the parasitic burden of the commercial
CCS Facility on the Retrofit Plant, because of the steam required to regenerate the solvent in the primary

 5-1
91
ALL4 Project No. 001242-0004.00                                                                     November 2025

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

and secondary loops. In other words, the NGCC will need to produce approximately 14.3% more energy
in the form of steam and power to maintain baseline electricity production levels. The generation of
additional steam and power requires additional fuel combustion, which will result in emissions increases
of regulated NSR pollutants above “baseline” emissions levels. The projected emissions increases from
the new commercial CCS Facility and the potential emissions increases from the retrofitted NGCC unit at
the Retrofit Plant will dictate the air permitting and regulatory requirements.

5.1.1 Summary of CCS Facility Potential Emissions at Commercial-Scale

The potential emissions associated with the CCS Facility will factor into the air permitting requirements
associated with a Greenfield Plant or a Retrofit Plant. Because a Greenfield Plant project will likely trigger
major NSR permitting based on the PTE of one or more regulated NSR pollutants being greater that 100
tpy1, the potential emissions associated solely with the CCS Facility must be quantified and included in a
major source preconstruction permit application. In a retrofit plant project, the PTE of the CCS Facility will
need to be quantified and included, along with the projected actual increases of regulated NSR pollutants
associated with the affected or modified NGCC EGU that occur because of the parasitic load burden of the
CCS Facility on the NGCC EGU. The PTE calculations for the CCS Facility are described below.

5.1.1.1 Potential Emissions

Potential emissions of regulated air pollutants (RAP) from the CCS Facility were estimated using process
simulation stream data provided by UKy IDEA, testing data for DL-1-Amino-2-propanol (A2P) from the 0.7
MW equivalent E.W. Brown (EWB) Generating Station pilot plant [37] [38], process parameters provided
by UKy IDEA, and chemical usage rates in the TEA Report. RAP emitted from the CCS Facility includes VOC
and Hazardous Air Pollutants (HAP). VOC, formaldehyde (HAP), and acetaldehyde (HAP) are emitted from
CCS as solvent slip and degradation products.

Preliminary emissions estimates are provided in Appendix A. A summary of the preliminary emissions
calculations is provided in Table 5-1. Potential emissions calculations presume that the CCS Facility will
operate 8,760 hours per year at a “worst case” emissions rate. Note, these emissions rates only quantify

5.1.1.2 Greenfield Plant Potential Emissions

The Site is expected to consist of two 2017 F-class CTGs and HRSGs, and one STG in a multi-shaft 2×2×1
combined-cycle configuration. The HRSGs will include selective catalytic reduction (SCR) to minimize
emissions of NOX, and an oxidation catalyst to minimize carbon monoxide (CO) and VOC emissions from
the CTGs. Potential emissions of NOX from a NGCC EGU facility of this size are typically based on
conservative, maximum operating conditions (i.e., 100% load at low ambient temperatures) and are
expected to exceed 100 tpy. Utilizing a NOX emissions rate of 0.004 pounds per million British thermal
units (lb/MMBtu) obtained from operating case B31B, and assuming a CT natural gas heat input rate of
2,300 MMBtu/hr, each CTG has an estimated NOX PTE greater than 100 tpy. Additionally, the facility must
include emissions related to startup (SU) and shutdown (SD) events in a preconstruction permit
application that are assumed to be uncontrolled, higher emissions rates for the duration of the event,
which will increase the PTE. Should the HRSGs be equipped with duct burners (DB) to increase the plant
output, emissions estimates would increase. It is also assumed that a Greenfield Plant will include
additional NOX emitting sources to support plant operations including emergency generators, fuel gas
heaters, and an auxiliary boiler thereby increasing plant-wide PTE. The potential emissions from the CCS
Facility would be included in the site-wide PTE for a Greenfield Plant and its inclusion will significantly
reduce the PTE of CO2 from the facility.

 5-4
94
ALL4 Project No. 001242-0004.00                                                                      November 2025

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

It is assumed that the Greenfield Plant will be in an area that is classified as in attainment for all regulated
NSR pollutants with respect to the National Ambient Air Quality Standards (NAAQS). Therefore, all NSR
regulated pollutants are regulated under the Prevention of Significant Deterioration (PSD) permitting
program. As a fossil-fuel fired electric generating plant, the Greenfield Plant would be subject to the PSD
major source applicability threshold of 100 tpy in accordance with 40 CFR 52.21(b)(i)(A) and would trigger
major NSR because preliminary emissions estimates indicate that the PTE rate of NO X will exceed 100 tpy
and that other regulated NSR pollutants will exceed applicable PSD significant emission rates (SER).

5.1.1.3 Retrofit Plant Projected Emissions Increases

The retrofit of a commercial scale CCS Facility to an existing NGCC EGU plant (Retrofit Project) is estimated
to result in an approximate 14.3% parasitic load on the heat duty of an existing NGCC EGU generating unit
because of the additional steam and power needed for the CCS Facility [1]. The NGCC EGU at a Retrofit
Plant will need to operate at a rate that is approximately 14.3% higher than its normal baseline operating
rate to maintain its baseline electricity output (assuming that the excess capacity exists). The combustion
of additional fuel in the CTGs and HRSG DB, as applicable, results in emissions increases of regulated NSR
pollutants. The size of the Retrofit Plant NGCC EGU components, their configuration, their vintage, and
the level of NOX, CO, and VOC control will dictate the magnitude of emissions increases. Because no site
has been selected for a Retrofit Project, a complete NSR emissions analysis for emissions for a Retrofit
Project is not included in this document. However, based on practical experience, an emissions analysis
associated with a retrofitted plant reflecting a 14.3% parasitic load over baseline operating conditions
would show net significant emissions increase of at least one regulated NSR pollutant and therefore
trigger PSD permitting requirements.

5.1.2 Preconstruction Permitting Considerations

The direct emissions associated with the Site would be addressed as part of an initial preconstruction
permit application for the entire Greenfield Plant. The potential emissions rate for regulated NSR
pollutants associated with the EGU, inclusive of the CCS Facility potential emissions associated with
solvent slip or degradation products, will dictate the air permitting and regulatory requirements for the
Project. Construction of the Site or a Retrofit Project would likely trigger major NSR permitting
requirements under the PSD program, the Nonattainment New Source Review (NNSR) program, or both

 5-5
95

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

programs as a new major stationary source or major modification at an existing major stationary source
in the Midwestern United States.

Federal, state, or regional permitting regulations and emissions standards are triggered when changes
(i.e., modifications) are made at existing facilities that result in emissions increases of regulated NSR
pollutants. The various preconstruction air quality permitting programs and emissions standards that
likely apply to the Greenfield Plant Site or a Retrofit Project are described in the following subsections.

5.1.2.1 New Source Review

A preconstruction air quality permit will be required prior to beginning actual construction of the Project.
Preconstruction permits are issued under state and local NSR regulatory programs, which typically include
a jurisdiction-specific minor NSR program and two distinct major NSR permitting programs: PSD and
NNSR. For preconstruction permitting, the definition of “major stationary source” is important and
depends on an area’s attainment status with the NAAQS and the type of facility being modified. Citizen
participation is required for all PSD and NNSR permits, which include required public notice and comment
periods, public hearings, and citizens’ ability to appeal permit decisions made by CAA administrators.

The CAA requires U.S. EPA to set NAAQS for six criteria pollutants: PM, CO, nitrogen dioxide (NO 2), SO2,
ozone (O3), and lead (Pb). NAAQS have also been established for particulate matter of less than 10 microns
(PM10) and particulate matter of less than 2.5 microns (PM2.5). Because ozone is not emitted directly but
is formed by complex photochemical atmospheric reactions, VOC and NO X emissions are regulated as
precursors to the formation of ground level ozone. U.S. EPA establishes whether areas are attaining the
NAAQS and the degree of NAAQS nonattainment primarily through regional ambient air quality
monitoring stations. State CAA administrators are required to develop and administer State
Implementation Plans (SIPs) to ensure compliance with the NAAQS. The PSD and NNSR preconstruction
permitting programs are required components of SIPs. As described below, the preconstruction
permitting programs differ between areas that are attaining the NAAQS and those that are not attaining
the NAAQS.

The PSD regulations apply to the construction or major modification of a major stationary source and are
designed to ensure that the air quality in areas attaining the NAAQS does not significantly deteriorate.
The PSD program regulates emissions of criteria pollutants and certain other RAP. Constructing a new

 5-6
96

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

major stationary source or initiating a major modification at an existing major stationary source in a
NAAQS attainment area triggers the PSD permitting regulations. The major components of the PSD permit
program are described in subsequent sections.

The NNSR regulations apply to the construction or modification of major stationary sources in areas that
are designated as nonattainment with one or more NAAQS. The NAAQS attainment status of criteria
pollutants for a given region or county are found under 40 CFR Part 81 Subpart B. The NNSR review and
permitting process is intended to allow the construction of new or modified sources of air pollution in
nonattainment areas while still making reasonable further progress towards attaining NAAQS compliance.
The NNSR rules are also designed to ensure that emissions decreases of nonattainment criteria pollutants
or precursors occur as a result of a new major stationary source or major modification at an existing major
stationary source located in a nonattainment area.

For the purposes of this analysis, the Retro Fit Project scenario is presumed to be a major modification at
an existing major stationary source. Fossil fuel-fired steam electric plants of more than 250 million British
thermal units heat input per hour (MMBtu/hr) are regulated under 40 CFR §52.21(b)(1)(i)(A) as 100 ton
per year major PSD stationary sources. Therefore, the Project will occur at an existing major stationary
source under the PSD regulations for the pollutants shown in Table 5-2 and an existing major stationary
source for NOX, an ozone precursor under NNSR requirements. As an existing major stationary source, the
facility must evaluate the Retrofit Project’s emissions increases of RAP, including ozone precursors (i.e.,
NOX and VOC) to determine if the Retrofit Project is a major modification2 under the PSD and NNSR
preconstruction air permitting programs. That evaluation is completed through an NSR applicability
determination. If the project-related emissions increases exceed the significant emissions rates (SER)
values in Table 5-2, the project will be subject to major NSR permitting requirements under one or both
NSR permit programs.

 that would result in a significant emissions increase… of a regulated NSR pollutant…; and a significant net
 emissions increase of that pollutant from the major stationary source. [40 CFR 52.21(b)(2)(i)]
