5.1.2.1.1 New Source Review Determinations

State and local air permitting authorities operate under the authorization of U.S. EPA. PSD requirements
are typically adopted by reference into SIPs or reflect U.S. EPA-approved state or local-specific PSD rules
that cannot be less stringent than the federal PSD rule. The PSD applicability determination procedures
are specified under 40 CFR §52.21(a)(2) and 40 CFR §51.166. NNSR requirements are typically state or
local-specific regulations that are codified under an implementation plan and that reflect the applicable
provisions of 40 CFR §51.165(a)(2). The NNSR applicability determination procedures under the PSD and
NNSR programs are largely the same. To be considered a major modification under each program, the
Project must result in a significant emissions increase (Step 1) and a significant “net” emissions increase
(Step 2).

Each NSR program includes the following applicability “tests” to determine if a significant emissions
increase occurs in Step 1:

 5-8
98
ALL4 Project No. 001242-0004.00                                                                        November 2025

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

  “Actual-to-projected-actual test” for projects that involve existing emissions units – In this test
 the baseline actual emissions (BAE) associated with a modified or affected emissions unit is
 compared to the projected actual emissions (PAE) of that unit following the change. The PAE can
 exclude emissions that the unit could have accommodated (CHA) during the 24-month baseline
 period used to establish the PAE that are unrelated to the Project.

  “Actual-to-potential test” for projects that involve new emissions units - In this test the PTE for a
 new emissions unit is compared to a BAE rate of zero.

  “Hybrid test” for projects that involve multiple types of emissions units - In this test, the emission
 rate changes determined through the actual-to-projected-actual test and the actual -to-
 potential test for new, modified, and affected emissions units associated a project are summed.

 Several terms used in the preceding tests clarify how the rules are applied to certain emissions units
 and define the information that is needed to determine if the Project-related emissions increase is
 significant under Step 1. State and local permitting authorities often establish jurisdiction-specific PSD
 and NNSR regulations as part of their approved implementation plans, which may include variations
 of the defined terms under the PSD and NNSR programs. In such instances, the terms below should
 be deferred to those jurisdiction-specific regulatory definitions. Each such term is described below:

  Major Modification – is defined under 40 CFR 52.21(b)(2)(i) as “…any physical change in or change
 in the method of operation of a major stationary source that would result in: a significant
 emissions increase… of a regulated NSR pollutant…; and a significant net emissions increase of
 that pollutant from the major stationary source.” The NNSR regulations include a similar definition
 of major modification under 40 CFR 51.165(a)(1)(v)(A) as “…any physical change in or change in
 the method of operation of a major stationary source that would result in: (1) [a] significant
 emissions increase of a regulated NSR pollutant…; and (2) [a] significant net emissions increase of
 that pollutant from the major stationary source.” The definitions include the key phrase “any
 physical change or change in the method of operation...” An NGCC may be a modified unit
 because the changes proposed (i.e., retrofit of a CCS Facility, which is a control device) could be
 considered as a “physical change or change in the method of operation” of the NGCC because
 post-project emissions are expected to change as a result of the CCS Facility retrofit.

  Affected emissions unit – is not specifically defined but is used to describe existing emissions units
 whose emissions could change as a result of a project. Affected emissions units may experience
 an emissions increase as a result of the installation of a new emissions unit or a modification to
 an emissions unit located upstream or downstream (e.g., additional uptime, de-bottlenecking).
 The NGCC may be an affected unit because the post-project emissions are expected to change as
 a result of the installation and operations of a CCS Facility under a Retrofit Project.

  Baseline actual emissions – for an existing electric utility steam generating unit, BAE is “…the
 average rate, in tons per year, at which the unit actually emitted the pollutant during any
 consecutive 24-month period selected by the owner or operator within the 5-year period

 5-9
99

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

 immediately preceding when the owner or operator begins actual construction of the project.”
          [40 CFR 52.21(b)(48)(i), 40 CFR 51.166(b)(47)(i), and 40 CFR 51.165(a)(1)(xxxv)] The selection of a
 baseline period may vary on a pollutant-by-pollutant basis. However, the baseline periods must
 be consistent for all modified and affected emissions units. Because the NGCC EGU will be either
 modified or affected under a Retrofit Project, the BAE for all regulated NSR pollutants from the
 NGCC EGU must be established.

  Projected actual emissions – is “… the maximum annual rate, in tons per year, at which an existing
 emissions unit is projected to emit a regulated NSR pollutant in any one of the 5 years (12-month
 period) following the date the unit resumes regular operation after the project...” [40 CFR
          52.21(b)(41)(i), 40 CFR 51.166(b)(40)(i), and 40 CFR 51.165(a)(1)(xxviii)(A)] Because the NGCC EGU
 will be modified or affected under a Retrofit Project, the PAE of all regulated NSR pollutants from
 the NGCC EGU must be established.

  Could have accommodated emissions – is not specifically defined, but is used within the definition
 of PAE, and reflects emissions from an affected or modified emissions unit that could have
 occurred during the 24-month baseline period that are “…also unrelated to the particular project,
 including any increased utilization due to product demand growth.” [40 CFR 52.21(b)(41)(ii)(C),
          40 CFR 51.166(b)(40)(ii)(C), and 40 CFR 51.165(a)(1)(xxviii)(B)(3)] CHA emissions are those
 emissions that could have legally occurred during the 24-month baseline period in response to an
 increased demand for power. CHA emissions are used to adjust (i.e., lower) PAE in an NSR
 applicability determination. However, PAE can only be adjusted for CHA emissions to the extent
 that they are unrelated to the Project. Because the NGCC EGU will be modified or affected under
 a Retrofit Project, the CHA emissions for all regulated NSR pollutants from the NGCC must be
 established.

  Potential to emit – is “… the maximum capacity of a stationary source to emit a pollutant under
          its physical and operational design…” [40 CFR 52.21(b)(4), 40 CFR 51.166(b)(4), and 40 CFR
 51.165(a)(1)(xxxv)(C)] Because the CCS Facility is a new emissions unit and will emit regulated
 NSR pollutants independent of the NGCC EGU, the PTE of all regulated NSR pollutants must be
 established for the CCS Facility.

Under a Retrofit Project, the project includes new emissions units (i.e., the CCS Facility) and modified or
affected emissions units (i.e., the NGCC EGU). Therefore, the Hybrid applicability test for projects that
involve multiple types of emissions units (i.e., new and modified/affected) should be used to evaluate PSD
and NNSR applicability to the project for all regulated NSR pollutants, as applicable.

5.1.2.1.2 New Source Review Applicability Determination Calculation Procedures

The Step 1 emissions increases of regulated NSR pollutants for the CCS Facility are established using the
actual-to-potential applicability test and the following equation for each regulated NSR pollutant:

 CCS Emissions increase (tpy) = PTE (tpy) – BAE (tpy)

 5-10
100

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Because the CCS Facility is new, its BAE for regulated NSR pollutants is 0 tpy.

The Step 1 emissions increases of regulated NSR pollutants for a modified or affected NGCC EGU under a
Retrofit Project are established using the actual-to-projected-actual (ATPA) applicability test and the
following equation for each regulated NSR pollutant:

 NGCC EGU Emissions increase (tpy) = [PAE (tpy) – CHA (tpy)] – BAE (tpy)

The Step 1 emissions increases of regulated NSR pollutants for the Retrofit Project are established under
the hybrid test by summing the emissions increases (and decreases) associated with the applicability tests
used for the CCS Facility retrofitted to an existing NGCC EGU for each regulated NSR pollutant:

 Project emissions increase (tpy) = CCS Emissions increase (tpy) + NGCC Emissions increase (tpy)

The Project emissions increase of each individual regulated NSR pollutant is compared to its
corresponding SER to determine if the project-related emissions increase for each regulated NSR pollutant
is significant under Step 1. If there is a significant emissions increase for a regulated NSR pollutant under
Step 1, then a Step 2 “netting” evaluation is required to determine if there is also a “net” significant
emissions increase of that pollutant.

The Step 2 netting evaluation must consider other “contemporaneous” increases and decreases of
regulated NSR pollutant emissions across the entire facility during the contemporaneous period, which is
typically five years prior to the change, but can vary. Major PSD and NNSR requirements can be avoided
if a project can “net out” of NSR applicability. The Step 2 netting analysis to determine if there is a
significant net emissions increase is determined using the following equation for regulated NSR pollutants
that had a significant emissions increase under Step 1:

 Net emissions increase (tpy) = Project emissions increase (tpy) + Site-wide creditable emission
 increases (tpy) – Site-wide creditable emissions decreases (tpy)

If the net emissions increase, established using the techniques described above, is greater than the SER
for a given regulated NSR pollutant, then the project is a major modification for that regulated NSR
pollutant and the project is subject to PSD and/or NNSR requirements, depending on the regulated NSR
pollutant and its attainment status for the area at which the stationary source is located.

 5-11
101

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Under a Retrofit Project, it is recommended that the existing facility develop the necessary emissions
information for all regulated NSR pollutants (i.e., BAE, CHA, and PAE for the NGCC plus PTE for the CCS
Facility) to complete the necessary preconstruction PSD and NNSR applicability determinations described.

5.1.2.2 Prevention of Significant Deterioration Permit Requirements

If the Project results in a significant net emissions increase of PM 10, PM2.5, NO2, SO2, CO, VOC or Pb, the
major PSD permit requirements are triggered assuming that the Project occurs in a region that is in
attainment with NAAQS for these pollutants. PSD review and permitting is intended to allow construction
of new or modified sources of air pollution in attainment areas while protecting ambient air quality.

If the project is a major modification under PSD, the preconstruction permit application for the project
will need to address requirements of PSD in accordance with regional (i.e., state or local) PSD provisions
in an approved implementation plan. Most state and local implementation plans incorporate the
provisions of 40 CFR 52.21 by reference. However, it is important to review and understand how state or
local jurisdiction implement the PSD regulations because they can (and often do) develop and operate
under U.S. EPA approved jurisdiction-specific PSD rules, which generally reflect the substantive federal
PSD requirement but may include variations that could influence a PSD applicability analysis. Air permit
applications that address the major PSD requirements are more technical than a typical construction
permit application because they must address the required components of a PSD application in addition
to the standard construction permit application requirements for the jurisdiction. The additional PSD
application requirements include best available control technology (BACT), Source Impact Analysis,
Additional Impact Analysis, and Class I Impact Analysis. The substantive requirements under the PSD
permitting program are summarized in the following subsections.

5.1.2.2.1 Best Available Control Technology

The applicant is required to apply BACT “… for each regulated NSR pollutant for which it would result in a
significant net emissions increase at the source.” The BACT requirement applies to each emissions unit
for which a significant net emissions increase in the pollutant would occur as a result of a physical change
or change in the method of operation in the unit (i.e., modification) [40 CFR §52.21(j)(3)]. BACT is defined
as an “emissions limitation… based on the maximum degree of reduction for each pollutant subject to
regulation under the Act which would be emitted from any proposed major stationary source or major

 5-12
102

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

modification which the Administrator… determines is achievable for such source or modification through
application of production processes or available methods, systems, and techniques…” [40 CFR
§52.21(b)(12)]. BACT analyses are determined on a case-by-case basis and take energy, environmental,
and economic impacts and other costs into account. Generally speaking, determining and implementing
BACT for a new emissions unit is more favorable because control requirements can be considered in the
engineering design phase of the emissions unit. BACT determinations and control technology
implementation for existing emissions units with a net significant emissions increase under a major
modification project at existing major stationary source is more challenging because the control
technology must be designed around the existing emissions unit considering more physical limitations and
restrictions (e.g., an existing nearby building prohibits ideal placement of equipment and/or ducting).

5.1.2.2.2 Source Impact Analysis

The applicant is required to demonstrate, through a source impact analysis, that significant net increases
in criteria pollutant emissions will not cause or contribute to a violation of a NAAQS or PSD increment.
PSD increments reflect the maximum allowable increase in ambient air concentrations above a baseline
concentration for an area [40 CFR §52.21(k)].

5.1.2.2.3 Additional Impact Analysis

The applicant is required to conduct an additional impacts analysis to assess a project’s impact on soil,
vegetation, and visibility related to its emissions increases and an evaluation of secondary emissions that
may occur as a result of the Project due to growth [40 CFR §52.21(o)].

5.1.2.2.4 Class I Impact Analysis

The applicant is required to conduct a Class I area impacts analysis, as applicable. Class I areas include
national parks and national wilderness areas, typically managed by the United States National Park Service
(U.S. NPS), the United States Forest Service (U.S. FS), or the United States Fish and Wildlife Service
(U.S. FWS). The Class I area impacts analysis can include air quality modeling for reduced Class I PSD
increments, visibility, and deposition, and a demonstration of protection of air quality related values
(AQRV) such as flora and fauna, water, visibility, cultural and paleontological, and odor. A Class I impacts
analysis includes participation by the Federal Land Manger (FLM) for federal lands potentially impacted

 5-13
103

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

by emissions from the project site [40 CFR §52.21(p)]. Based on historical U.S. EPA guidance, a 100
kilometer (km) range is a generally acceptable modeling domain for Class I impact analyses but analyses
have been required for distances beyond 100 km.

5.1.2.3 Nonattainment New Source Review Permit Requirements

The NNSR permit requirements may apply if the Project occurs at an NGCC EGU facility that is located in
area designated as nonattainment with a NAAQS. While there are pockets of nonattainment areas
associated with several NAAQS, the most likely nonattainment scenario in the midwestern United States
is associated with the 2015 ozone NAAQS or with a site located in an ozone transport region (OTR). Major
source thresholds for NOX and VOC and the associated SER for each vary based on the degree of
nonattainment of a given area, as presented in Table 5-3.

If the project is a major modification under NNSR, the preconstruction permit application for the Project
will need to address the substantive requirements of major NNSR in accordance with 40 CFR 51.165. Air
permit applications that address the major NNSR requirements are more technical than a typical
preconstruction permit application because they must address the required conditions for approval under
40 CFR §51.165 which include lowest achievable emission rate (LAER), existing source compliance,
emissions offsets, and alternative siting analysis. Each of the applicable requirements for approval is
described in the following subsections.

5.1.2.3.1 Lowest Achievable Emission Rate

For the pollutant(s) under NNSR that result in a significant net emission increase, the project would be
considered a major modification and would be required to meet an emission limitation which specifies
the LAER for such source [40 CFR Appendix-S-to-Part-51 IV.A. “Condition 1”]. LAER is defined under 40
CFR §51.165(a)(1)(xiii) as:

 “The most stringent emissions limitation which is contained in the implementation plan of any
 State for such class or category of stationary source, unless the owner or operator of the proposed
 stationary source demonstrates that such limitations are not achievable; or

 The most stringent emissions limitation which is achieved in practice by such class or category of
 stationary sources. This limitation, when applied to a modification, means the lowest achievable
 emissions rate for the new or modified emissions units within or stationary source. In no event
 shall the application of the term permit a proposed new or modified stationary source to emit any
 pollutant in excess of the amount allowable under an applicable new source standard of
 performance.”

5.1.2.3.2 Existing Source Compliance

The applicant will need to demonstrate that all existing major sources that are owned or operated by the
applicant and located in the same state are in compliance with all applicable emissions limits and
standards or are in compliance with an expeditious state and federally enforceable compliance schedule
or a court decree establishing a compliance schedule [40 CFR Appendix-S-to-Part-51 IV.A. “Condition 2”].

5.1.2.3.3 Emissions Offsets

The applicant will need to “offset” the emissions increases of ozone precursors (NO X and/or VOC) for the
Project with emissions decreases from existing sources from within the nonattainment area or OTR to
ensure that there is reasonable further progress towards attainment of the NAAQS [40 CFR Appendix-S-
to-Part-51 IV.A. “Condition 3” and “Condition 4”]. The offset ratio is dependent on the degree of
nonattainment and range from 1.1:1 (marginal nonattainment) to 1.5:1 (extreme nonattainment) [40 CFR
§51.165(a)(9)(ii)]. Emissions offsets must be for the same pollutant [40 CFR Appendix-S-to-Part-51 IV.G.5]
unless interpollutant offsetting is allowed. Emissions offsets must reflect reductions in actual emissions
and can be generated through the permanent shutdown of existing emissions units, curtailing operations
of existing emissions units, or through overcontrol of emissions. Any emissions reductions that are used

 5-15
105

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

for offsets must be surplus, permanent, quantifiable, and federally enforceable [40 CFR Appendix-S-to-
Part-51 IV.C.34].

5.1.2.3.4 Alternative Siting Analysis

The applicant will need to prepare “… an analysis of the alternative sites, sizes, production processes, and
environmental control techniques for such proposed source, which demonstrates that benefits of the
proposed source significantly outweigh the environmental and social costs imposed as a result of its
location, construction, or modification” [40 CFR Appendix-S-to-Part-51 IV.A. “Condition 5”].

5.1.2.3.5 Potential Major NSR Implications

The specific PSD and NNSR requirements cannot be determined without first determining whether and
how each will apply to the Project. There is also uncertainty regarding how regulatory agencies will
interpret the status of the NGCC CTG at a Retrofit Plant as a modified or as an affected emissions unit. An
NGCC CT’s status as a modified or affected emissions unit can substantially affect the technical effort
associated with the preparation of a preconstruction permit application because as a modified emissions
unit, the NGCC CTG would be subject to BACT analyses for pollutants exhibiting a significant net emissions
increase under PSD and a LAER analysis for a significant net emissions increase of NO X emissions (and
possibly VOC emissions) under NNSR. Because there is a NAAQS for NO2 and because NOX is an ozone
precursor, a significant net emissions increase of NOX would trigger both major PSD and major NNSR
permit requirements for a project located in an ozone nonattainment area. Potential PSD and NNSR
permitting scenarios for a Greenfield Plan and a Retrofit Plant are summarized below.

Greenfield Plant

Scenario 1: New NGCC CT in Attainment Area

  A new major stationary source of regulated NSR pollutants [i.e., > 100 tpy PTE] will trigger major
 PSD permitting requirements.
  The PTE for emissions associated directly with the CCS Facility (i.e., solvent slip, minor degradation
 products, trace HAP, etc.) would be incorporated into the overall PTE for the project.
  The construction permit application will need to include case-by-case BACT analyses for all
 pollutants that exhibit a PTE of greater than 100 tpy (major stationary source threshold under PSD
 for fossil fuel fired steam generating units) or a significant net emissions increase. Both the CO 2
 major stationary source threshold and the CO2 significant emission rate are unlikely to be

106
ALL4 Project No. 001242-0004.00                                                                   November 2025

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

 exceeded because the CCS Facility will capture and control CO2 emissions, negating the need for
 a CO2 BACT analysis.
  The construction permit application will need to include a source impacts analysis, an additional
 impacts analysis, and a Class I impacts analysis for regulated NSR pollutants exhibiting greater
 than 100 tpy PTE or a significant emissions increase.

Scenario 2: New NGCC CT in Ozone Nonattainment Attainment Area

  A new major stationary source of PM10, PM2.5, NO2, SO2, CO or lead [i.e., > 100 tpy PTE] will trigger
 major PSD permitting requirements.
  The PTE for emissions associated directly with the CCS Facility (i.e., solvent slip, minor degradation
 products, trace HAP, etc.) would be incorporated into the overall PTE for the project.
  The preconstruction permit application will need to include case-by-case BACT analyses for all
 attainment pollutants that exhibit a PTE of greater than 100 tpy (major stationary source
 threshold under PSD for fossil fuel fired steam generating units) or a significant emissions
 increase. Neither the CO2 major stationary source threshold nor the CO2 significant emission rate
 is likely to be exceeded because the CCS Facility will capture and control CO 2 emissions, negating
 the need for a CO2 BACT analysis.
  The preconstruction permit application will need to include a source impacts analysis, an
 additional impacts analysis, and a Class I impacts analysis for criteria attainment pollutants
 exhibiting greater than 100 tpy PTE or a significant emissions increase.
  As a new major stationary source of NOX, the Project will trigger major NNSR permit requirements.
 A new NGCC EGU facility will likely be a minor source of VOC emissions (i.e., PTE < 100 tpy) in a
 marginal or moderate nonattainment area but may be a major source of VOC emissions
 (i.e., > 50 tpy) in a serious nonattainment area. The major NNSR permit requirements will be

 or 50 tpy.
  The application will need to include a LAER analysis for the NGCC for NO X emissions (and possibly

Retrofit Plant

  A significant net emissions increase for PM10, PM2.5, NO2, SO2, VOC, CO or Pb for the Project will

  The preconstruction permit application will need to include a case-by-case BACT analyses for the

5.1.3 General Permitting Process

For the first step in permitting a CCS Facility at a Greenfield Plant or under a Retrofit Plant, the Site will
need to determine the NSR pollutant emissions increases from the Project as described in previous
sections, including all of the affected sources. Because the CCS Facility will use load from the NGCC CTGs
(i.e., steam and power), the emissions increases associated with load at a Retrofit Plant need to be
quantified. Once the projected emissions increases are determined, the site will need to obtain regulatory
determination from the state or local regulatory agency for classifying any existing NGCC CT with a retrofit
CCS as either a modified or affected source under the project.

After the Site evaluates the permitting strategy and applicability, it will need to submit a preconstruction
permit application in accordance the state or local air permitting regulations. In general, an application
will need to include the following:

  Detailed project description;
  An analysis of character characteristics, properties, and quantity of the air contaminants under
 maximum operating conditions;
  An analysis of applicable regulations;
  Emissions calculations;
  Complete preconstruction permit application forms;
  A certification that the statements made in the application are true and complete, signed by a
 responsible official; and
  Any additional analyses associated with the PSD and NNSR permit programs, as applicable and as
 discussed in previous sections (i.e., BACT or LAER).

 5-19
109
ALL4 Project No. 001242-0004.00                                                                   November 2025

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Once the application is submitted, the state or local CAA administrator will review and issue a draft permit.
The draft permit will go through public comment which can include public hearings, and the ability to
appeal permit decisions made by state or local regulatory agency. If the preconstruction permit
application must address the major modification provisions under the PSD/NNSR programs, a final
permitting action can take up to 18 months or more to complete after submission of the application.

5.1.4 Federal Air Quality Regulations

Federal regulations applicable to NGCC EGU under Sections 111 and 112 of the CAA are discussed in
Subsections 5.1.4.1 and 5.1.4.2.

5.1.4.1 National Emissions Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants

National Emissions Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP) promulgated under 40 CFR Part 63
regulate the HAP listed in Section 112 of the CAA using technology-based standards that reflect maximum
available control technology (MACT). Standards promulgated under 40 CFR Part 63, commonly referred
to as MACT standards, apply to source categories that are area or major sources of HAP. Select NESHAP
standards related to NGCC EGU facilities are discussed in the following subsections.

5.1.4.1.1         40 CFR Part 63 Subpart YYYY

The provisions of 40 CFR Part 63, Subpart YYYY (National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants
for Stationary Combustion Turbines) apply to all owners or operators of a stationary CT located at a major
source of HAP. 40 CFR 63, Subpart YYYY regulated formaldehyde emissions and specifies a limitation of 91
parts per billion volume dry (ppbvd) as listed in Table 1 to 40 CFR Part 63, Subpart YYYY. Because the
Greenfield Plant and the Retrofit Plant would likely be area sources of HAP, 40 CFR Part 63, Subpart YYYY
would not apply to either. The inclusion of a CCS Facility would not likely change the source classification
of the Greenfield Plant or the Retrofit Plant from an area source of HAP to a major source of HAP.

5.1.4.1.2         40 CFR Part 63, Subpart DDDDD

40 CFR Part 63, Subpart DDDDD (National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants for Major
Sources: Industrial, Commercial, and Institutional Boilers and Process Heaters ) regulates HAP emissions
from boilers and process heaters located at major sources of HAP. Because the Greenfield Plant and the

 5-20
110

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Retrofit Plant would likely be area sources of HAP, 40 CFR Part 63, Subpart DDDDD would not apply to
either. Also, because the HRSGs associated with the Greenfield Plant or the Retrofit Plant meet the
definition of “waste heat boiler” under 40 CFR §63.7575, they are excluded from the definition of “boiler”
under 40 CFR §63.7575. Because the HSRGs do not meet the definition of “…industrial, commercial, and
institutional boiler…,” they would not be subject to 40 CFR Part 63, Subpart DDDDD. U.S. EPA has further
confirmed that waste heat boilers equipped with duct burners are not subject to 40 CFR Part 63, Subpart
DDDDD [39]

5.1.4.2 Standards of Performance for New Stationary Sources

U.S. EPA has promulgated the Standards of Performance for New Stationary Sources, which implement
emissions requirements for specific new, reconstructed, and modified sources, otherwise known as
“NSPS” under 40 CFR Part 60. Select NSPS regulations related to NGCC EGU facilities are discussed in
Subsections 5.1.4.2.1 through 5.1.4.2.3.

5.1.4.2.1         40 CFR Part 60 Subpart KKKK

40 CFR Part 60, Subpart KKKK (Standards of Performance for Stationary Combustion Turbines) applies to
owners or operators of a stationary CT with a heat input at peak load equal to or greater than 10
MMBtu/hr based on the higher heating value (HHV) and that commenced construction, modification, or
reconstruction after February 18, 2005. In accordance with 40 CFR §60.4305(a), only the heat input rate
of the CT is considered when determining 40 CFR Part 60, Subpart KKKK applicability. The proposed natural
gas-fired CT and HRSG Duct Burner will be subject to the Subpart KKKK emissions standards. In a combined
cycle system, The CT and HRSG Duct Burner must comply with the Subpart KKKK emissions standards for
NOX and SO2 for a new turbine firing natural gas.

In accordance with 40 CFR §60.4320(a) and Table 1 of Subpart KKKK, NO X emissions for a CT with a heat
input of greater than 850 MMBtu/hr, when operating in cogeneration or simple cycle operating modes,
must be less than 15 ppm at 15% O2, or 0.43 pounds per megawatt hour (lb/MWh) of useful output. In
accordance with Table 1 of Subpart KKKK, if operating the HRSG Duct Burner independently, it will be
subject to an emissions limit of 54 ppm at 15% O2. CTs are also subject to the SO2 emissions standards of
40 CFR §60.4330(a) of either 0.90 lb/MWh gross output or 0.060 lb/MMBtu heat input.

 5-21
111

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Greenfield Plant

Because the construction of the Greenfield Plant CTs would commence after February 18, 2005 and the
CTs would have a heat input at peak load equal to or greater than 10 MMBtu/hr based on the HHV of
natural gas, the applicable NOX and SO2 standards and associated monitoring, recordkeeping, and
reporting requirements under 40 CFR Part 60, Subpart KKKK requirements would apply to the Greenfield
Plant. The inclusion of a CCS Facility would not impact the applicability of or compliance with Subpart
KKKK.

Retrofit Plant

If the construction of the CCS Facility to Retrofit Plant CTs commenced after February 18, 2005, the
applicable NOX and SO2 standards and associated monitoring, recordkeeping, and reporting requirements
under 40 CFR Part 60, Subpart KKKK requirements would apply to the CTs. The Retrofit Project to install a
CCS Facility would not impact the applicability of or compliance with 40 CFR Part 60, Subpart KKKK.

If the construction of the CTs commenced before February 18, 2005, the applicability of Subpart KKKK to
the CTs under the Retrofit Project would need to be evaluated as either a reconstruction or modification
under 40 CFR §60.15 and §60.14, respectively.

Under 40 CFR 60.15(b), Reconstruction means “the replacement of components of an existing facility to
such an extent that:

 (1) The fixed capital cost of the new components exceeds 50 percent of the fixed capital cost that
 would be required to construct a comparable entirely new facility, and
 (2) It is technologically and economically feasible to meet the applicable standards set forth in this
 part.”

If the fixed capital cost of retrofitting the CCS Facility to the CTs is greater than 50% of the fixed capital
cost of a comparable entirely new affected facility (i.e., Facility CTs), the affected facility meets the
reconstruction criteria and 40 CFR 60, Subpart KKKK would apply. Based on cost estimates provided in the
TEA Report for the CCS Facility and in the National Energy Technology Laboratory (NETL) baseline report
rev 4a for the Case B31B.90 NGCC EGU, the retrofit of the CCS Facility would not meet the reconstruction
criteria and Subpart KKKK would not apply as reconstruction.

 5-22
112

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Under 40 CFR §60.14(a), Modification means “…any physical or operational change to an existing facility
which results in an increase in the emission rate to the atmosphere of any pollutant to which a standard
applies…” The modification evaluation provisions are specified under 40 CFR §60.14(b). Because the
retrofit of a CCS Facility does not change the operating capacity of the affected facilities and the CTs have
likely operated at firing rates reflecting the post-CCS Facility retrofit, the CCS Facility retrofit would not
likely meet the modification criteria. The CCS Facility retrofit would also likely meet the modification
exemption criteria under 40 CFR §60.14(e)(5), which exempts “the addition or use of any system or device
whose primary function is the reduction of air pollutants.”

5.1.4.2.2         40 CFR Part 60 Subpart TTTT

40 CFR Part 60, Subpart TTTT (Standards of Performance for Greenhouse Gas Emissions from New
Stationary Sources for Electric Utility Generating Units) applies, with certain exceptions, to owners or
operators of any steam generating unit, integrated gasification combined cycle (IGCC), or stationary CT
that commenced construction after January 8, 2014 or commenced reconstruction after June 18, 2014
and that have a base load rating greater than 250 MMBtu/hr and serves a generator capable of selling
greater than 25 MW of electricity to a utility power distribution system. While Subpart TTTT could apply
to the Retrofit Plant, it could not apply to the Greenfield plant based on the applicability date of the rule.
The following emissions standards could apply to affected combusting only natural gas:

  When the CTG supplies more than its design efficiency or 50%, whichever is less, times its
 potential electric output as net-electric sales on both a 12-operating month and a 3-year rolling
 average basis, either of the following standards must be met:
 o 1,000 lb CO2/MWh of gross energy output, or
 o 1,030 lb CO2/MWh of net energy output
  When the CT supplies its design efficiency or 50%, whichever is less, times its potential electric
 output or less as net-electric sales on either a 12-operating month or a 3-year rolling average basis
 the following standard must be met:
 o 120 lb of CO2 per MMBtu

Retrofit Plant Applicability

The “modification” provisions under 40 CFR Part 60, Subpart A (General Provisions) do not apply to
combustion turbines in accordance with 40 CFR §60.5508. 40 CFR Part 60, Subpart TTTT will apply to the
Retrofit Plant based on the date that construction or reconstruction was commenced on the plant. If

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

construction commenced construction after January 8, 2014 or reconstruction commended after June 18,
2014, the Retrofit Plant would already be subject to the CO2 Standards under Subpart TTTT. Regardless of
applicability, the CO2 emissions reductions as a result of the CCS Facility retrofit would enable the Retrofit
Plant to easily comply with CO2 emissions standards under Subpart TTTT.

5.1.4.2.3         40 CFR Part 60 Subpart TTTTa

40 CFR Part 60, Subpart TTTTa (Standards of Performance for Greenhouse Gas Emissions for Modified
Coal-Fired Steam Electric Generating Units and New Construction and Reconstruction Stationary
Combustion Turbine Electric Generating Units) establishes emissions standards and compliance schedules
for owners or operators of any steam generating unit, IGCC, or stationary CT that commenced
construction, modification, or reconstruction after May 23, 2023, has a base load rating greater than 250
MMBtu/hr, and serves a generator capable of selling greater than 25 MW of electricity to a utility power
distribution system. Under 40 CFR §60.5580a, Subpart TTTTa defines affected CT EGUs as either “base
load,” “intermediate load,” or “low load” units. Base load combustion turbines supply more than 40
percent of their potential electric output as net-electric sales on both a 12-operating month and a 3-year
rolling average basis. Intermediate load combustion turbines supply more than 20 percent but less than
or equal to 40 percent of their potential electric output as net-electric sales on both a 12-operating month
and a 3-year rolling average basis. Low load combustion turbines supply 20 percent or less of their
potential electric output as net-electric sales on both a 12-operating month and a 3-year rolling average
basis. “Net electric sales” means the gross electric sales to the utility power distribution system minus
purchased power. The CTs would be characterized as base load CTs.

Greenfield Plant Applicability

The Greenfield Plant would be subject to Subpart TTTTa as a new source in accordance with 40 CFR
60.5509a(a) because it would be constructed after May 23, 2023 in accordance with 40 CFR 60.5509a(a).
In accordance with 40 CFR §60.5520a(a), the CTs would need to meet the applicable emissions standards
in Table 1 of 40 CFR Part 60, Subpart TTTTa. For a base load CT (i.e., supplies more than 40% of its potential
electric output as net-electric sales on both a 12-operating month and a 3-year rolling average basis), the
CO2 limitations are either 800 to 1,250 lb CO2/MWh of gross energy output or 820 to 1,280 lb CO2/MWh
of net energy output for 12-month operating averages beginning before January 2032. The standards
decrease for 12-month operating averages beginning after December 31, 2031 to 100 to 150 lb CO 2/MWh

 5-24
114

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

on a gross energy output basis and 97 to 139 lb CO2/MWh on a net energy output basis. Based on
information provided in the TEA Report, the Greenfield Plant would meet the CO 2 emissions standards
under Subpart TTTTa.

Retrofit Plant Applicability

The “modification” provisions under 40 CFR Part 60, Subpart A do not apply to combustion turbines in
accordance with 40 CFR §60.5509a(a). However, a Retrofit Plant could trigger applicability to Subpart
TTTTa as “reconstruction” because it would occur after May 23, 2023 under 40 CFR §60.5509a(a) and 40
CFR §60.15. Under 40 CFR 60.15(b), Reconstruction means “the replacement of components of an existing
facility to such an extent that the fixed capital cost of the new components exceeds 50 percent of the
fixed capital cost that would be required to construct a comparable entirely new facility, and It is
technologically and economically feasible to meet the applicable standards set forth in this part.”

If the fixed capital cost of the CCS Facility is greater than 50% of the fixed capital cost of a comparable
entirely new facility, the affected facility meets the reconstruction criteria and Subpart TTTTa would apply.
Based on cost estimates provided in the TEA Report for the CCS Facility and in the NETL baseline report
rev 4a for the Case B31B.90 NGCC EGU, the retrofit of the CCS Facility would not meet the reconstruction
criteria and Subpart TTTTa would not apply. Regardless of applicability, the CO 2 emissions reductions as a
result of the CCS Facility retrofit would enable the Retrofit Plant to comply with CO 2 emissions standards
under Subpart TTTTa.

5.1.5 State and Local Regulatory Considerations

In addition to federal preconstruction permitting programs and air quality regulations, state and local air
quality considerations for permitting, control technology reviews, minor NSR modeling, air toxics
programs, and environmental justice must be considered for a Greenfield Plant or Retrofit Project as
described in Subsections 5.1.5.1 through 5.1.5.5.

5.1.5.1 Permitting

Permitting requirements, including major NSR permitting and general state and local regulatory authority
permitting are discussed above. However, air permitting programs can and do vary significantly across
various jurisdictions and implementation plans can be more stringent than federal requirements. All

 5-25
115

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

implementation plans include required emissions control components and reasonably available control
technology (RACT) requirements for certain sources. While it is generally recognized that the major NSR
regulations can be challenging and technology forcing, various implementation plans approved by U.S.
EPA and implemented by a delegated CAA administrator include similarly stringent requirements that
must be addressed even when the NSR regulations are not triggered. Several common requirements of
state and local implementation plans are discussed in the following subsections.

5.1.5.2 Control Technology Reviews

Many state and local implementation plans include requirements for control technology reviews [i.e.,
BACT, best available technology (BAT), toxics BACT, etc.] associated with all construction permit
applications that require similar analyses (i.e., top-down review) that if not completed in accordance with
expectations of the delegated CAA administrator, can impact project schedule.

5.1.5.3 Minor NSR modeling

Many state and local implementation plans include requirements for minor NSR source modeling of
criteria pollutant increases to ensure that NAAQS standards not adversely impacted. Certain state
programs identify emissions increase screening levels that exempt minor emissions increase for the
modeling requirements. Other states require comparison of modeling emissions increases against
pollutant specific significant increase levels (SILs) and full blown attainment demonstrations if SIL
concentrations are exceeded.

5.1.5.4 Air Toxics Programs

State and local air toxics programs are initiatives put into place by residing agencies with the purpose of
protecting public health and welfare and designed to reduce health risks from toxic air pollutants. The
programs utilize a combination of monitoring, risk assessment, and regulatory action to identify high-risk
areas, inform monitoring programs, and prioritize emissions reductions. Regulated air toxics could differ
significantly between jurisdictions, with certain programs considering any compound with a health based
exposure threshold to be toxic. Compliance with state and local air toxics programs is generally
accomplished through an air quality modeling demonstration, although certain states have air toxic
specific BACT requirements as well. State and local air toxics programs are in addition to the Federal 40

 5-26
116

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

CFR Part 63 NESHAP program, which regulates CAA Section 112 HAP, although some programs exempt
Part 63 NESHAP affected sources from air toxic program applicability.

The thresholds established for air toxics, in the form of reference concentrations, can also vary from state
to state or local agency and may or may not involve risk assessments based on each individual state or
local air toxics program requirement. State or local agencies that do not have an established program or
define air toxics synonymous to NAAQS thresholds may still require certain high-profile projects to
conduct a case-by-case air toxics analysis. If no reference concentration is established by a state or local
agency to evaluate impacts against, it is up to the project to propose and justify reference concentrations
to use for an air toxics analysis. In addition, some state and local air toxics programs are beginning to
require an analysis of background air toxics data to be included for a cumulative air toxics analysis by
either running U.S. EPA’s AirToxScreen program or onsite ambient monitoring and analysis.

5.1.5.5 Environmental Justice

The U.S. EPA has rescinded major environmental justice (EJ) and climate change initiatives in 2025 through
executive orders which terminated federal EJ offices and programs plus cut or impounded previously
approved funding under the Inflation Reduction Act for such initiatives. This change federal in federal
policy and action shifts the EJ considerations from the federal to the state and local levels. Because existing
state and local EJ permitting and policy requirements vary widely and are expected to remain unchanged
or even become more prevalent, it creates a critical need before and during the project development
process to closely monitor state and local EJ policy and permitting processes to proactively address
program requirements (i.e., public outreach and engagement) that could delay a project.

5.2 WATER MANAGEMENT

Water quality and regulatory considerations for a commercial-scale CCS Facility were identified based on
analysis of potential impacts from carbon capture solvent degradation products as described in
Subsections 5.2.1 and 5.2.2.

5.2.1 Potential Solvent Degradation Products

During bench-scale testing, evidence of corrosion was observed in the Secondary Loop when using a nickel
alloy as the anode in the electrochemical regeneration cell. This type of alloy and other similar materials
with the potential to corrode during operation should be avoided. The observation was also made that
amines in the electrochemical regeneration cell were quickly being oxidized to ammonia, which is believed
to predominately exist within the treated flue gas; however, data supporting this observation were not
provided and entrained ammonia in the electrolyte is considered a potential degradation product. In
addition to these observations, analytical samples were taken from the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent
from the primary loop after 2000 hours of operation, the constituents and associated concentrations from
which are provided in Table 5-4.

5.2.2 Regulatory Considerations for Commercial-Scale CCS Facility

Clean Water Act (CWA) regulatory requirements, including water withdrawal, wastewater discharges,
stormwater discharges, federal Effluent Limitation Guidelines (ELG), as well as state and local water
quality considerations for a commercial-scale CCS Facility are described in Subsections 5.2.2.1 through
5.2.2.5.

5.2.2.1 Water Withdrawal

The CCS Facility will require water for washing, cooling, and makeup water for various units; however, the
bench-scale data reviewed under this assessment did not include flow rates for water consumed during
the bench-scale testing campaigns. Based on the TEA Report, 3894 gallons per minute (gpm) of raw water
will be sourced in equal parts from municipal supply and groundwater [1]. While municipally supplied
water will in almost all conceivable situations meet the National Primary Drinking Water Standards,
codified at 40 CFR Part 141, groundwater quality is highly variable by geography. Depending upon the
quality of groundwater available, raw water treatment will likely be required for the proposed
technologies but this type of treatment is anticipated to be employed at the Site. The hypothetical
greenfield facility is located in the Midwest; therefore, water rights are not a significant consideration
compared to areas that are facing water scarcity or drought. For these reasons, the potential for an
increase in water usage required by the CCS Facility is not anticipated to impede commercialization.
Timeline to obtain water withdrawal authorization from a delegated CWA administrator can be up to 12
months for significant withdrawal rates over 10,000 gallons per day (gpd).

5.2.2.2 Wastewater Discharges

Discharges of industrial wastewater directly to surface waters are regulated by the National Pollutant
Discharge Elimination System (NPDES) under 40 CFR Part 122, which is administered by U.S. EPA and state
agencies with delegated authority. Wastewater streams at existing power generation facilities that are
directly discharged and covered by this type of permit include non-contact cooling water, once-through

 5-29
119

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

cooling water, cooling tower and boiler blowdowns, demineralization or RO concentrates, process
wastewaters, and facility floor drains or domestic sewers. While there may be state-specific nuances in
the permitting process, discussed further in Section 5.2.2.5, implementation of this technology at existing
facilities with coverage under the NPDES for wastewater will require notification of the permitting agency
of the proposed change. The Primary Loop and Secondary Loop at scale are likely going to generate
wastewater streams from the water washing loop, cooling water loop, and electrochemical regenerator
in order to prevent scaling, corrosion, and maintain an overall efficient operation.

The TEA Report outlines that the cooling tower blowdown is the largest waste stream by volume at
approximately 874 gpm, followed by the Primary Loop at 353 gpm. The total wastewater discharge
estimated based on this design basis is 1,257 gpm or 1.8 million gallons per day (mgd). 40 CFR §122.41(l)(1)
requires facilities to notify the permitting agency when an alteration or addition to the facility could
introduce a new source, could change the nature or increase the quantity of pollutants in the discharge,
or change the sludge use or disposal practices. The addition of the proposed activity will require a major
modification of an existing NPDES wastewater discharge permit or, if a facility does not have an existing
NPDES permit, the project requires that the facility submit a permit application at least 180 days prior to
the discharge beginning. Any new discharge will be subject to new source performance standards,
antidegradation standards, and, because of the novel nature of the technology, could reasonably expect
a protracted permitting process as the permitting agency with jurisdiction evaluates the potential for the
proposed industrial process wastewater to cause or contribute to violations of water quality standards.

The potential degradation products and pass-through constituents from water supplied to the process
may result in a change in the nature of the wastewater discharged from the Site, which would have
implications for routine NPDES permitting, specifically on the application requirements to analyze or
estimate discharge characteristics during the permit renewal or modification process. If there are new or
increased pollutants identified in this process, then the permitting agency may reevaluate existing or
implement new limits or conditions in the permit. A significant factor that will determine whether the CCS
Facility can meet water quality standards or if additional wastewater treatment will be required prior to
discharge is the quality of the water sourced for the processes. Many processes at an existing NGCC facility
as well as the CCS Facility will increase the concentration of a pollutant in cycles, such as cooling tower
blowdown, and the water sourced as makeup will play a significant role in what the potential discharge
characteristics from the process will be. Constituents which have the potential to be present in

 5-30
120

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

municipally-supplied water (i.e., those with federal drinking water standards) include microorganisms,
disinfection byproducts (i.e., bromate, chlorite, haloacetic acids, trihalomethanes), disinfectants (i.e.,
chloramines, chlorine, chlorine dioxide), inorganic chemicals (i.e., metals, fluoride, cyanide, nitrates and
nitrites), organic chemicals (i.e., benzene, styrene, vinyl chloride, etc.), per- and poly-fluoroalkyl
substances (PFAS), and radionucleotides.

In addition to constituents introduced in water supplied to the CCS Facility, any byproducts generated by
the CCS Facility through degradation or electrochemical reactions will have to be removed via wastewater
treatment processes or they will be present in the discharge. These constituents would experience cycles
of concentration at various stages within the CCS Facility, which may concentrate them above effluent
limits and/or, in the electrochemical regenerator, lead to degradation of the cationic exchange membrane
or other components of the cell. To minimize wear and tear, existing NGCC power plants utilize
demineralization and/or RO pretreatment of supply water to the process and may require, for operational
reasons, even further treatment. Blowdown from boilers, heat exchangers, and cooling towers are
commonplace at existing power plants and the increase in these types of discharges is not anticipated to
impede commercialization. However, discharges associated with both the Primary Loop and Secondary
Loop are novel and have the potential to contribute to pollutant loading in wastewater discharges in a
manner not currently contemplated by the applicable discharge regulations. While all NGCC power plants
do not have wastewater treatment capabilities on-site, the TEA Report does indicate that wastewater
treatment equipment is envisioned. Implementation of the proposed technology is recommended to be
planned for installation upstream of any major on-site wastewater treatment systems.

Indirect discharges of industrial wastewater like those to a publicly owned treatment works (POTW) are
regulated through industrial user permits authorized by the National Pretreatment Program. Discharges
at existing power generation facilities that are typically directed to a POTW, if available and permitted,
include demineralization unit regeneration or RO concentrates, boiler blowdown, and cooling tower
blowdown. Indirect discharges to a POTW that have the potential to cause pass-through or upset of the
treatment works operations may have additional permit restrictions, limitations, and/or conditions. Based
on the experimental data, both qualitative and quantitative, constituents of concern include ammonia
and those identified in Table 5-4.

 5-31
121

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

5.2.2.3 Stormwater Discharges

Stormwater discharges are also regulated by the NPDES program and there are significant differences
between permitting programs on a state-by-state basis. Industrial stormwater from facilities subject to
the ELG at 40 CFR Part 423, which includes the proposed Site, are typically required by state industrial
stormwater general permits to obtain coverage. It is assumed that the CCS Facility will not be located
within a building and would be considered exposed to stormwater. As a result, the new area and activities
would be required to be incorporated into the Site’s permit and stormwater contingency plan, as required
by their governing industrial stormwater permit.

Many jurisdictions also require additional analytical sampling, inspections, or even structural controls to
be implemented if stormwater discharges are directed to a receiving water that is impaired, meaning that
it is on the state’s CWA 303(d)/305(b) Integrated Report or has a U.S. EPA-approved Total Maximum Daily
Load (TMDL). The review and potential modification of the designation of any given receiving water body
as impaired occurs no less frequently than once per three-year period, which may result in new or
modified impairments for pollutants and should be reviewed, if not required by the permit, on an annual
basis. Industrial materials and activities, particularly those that could result in potential pollutant loading
via stormwater discharges should be evaluated by the facility implementing the new process to determine
if there is a material or activity that could impact an impaired receiving water body. It is not anticipated
that any aspect of the CCS Facility, specific to the solvent, electrodes, and their degradation products, will
pose a concern for commercialization with respect to stormwater discharges. However, once the process
is commercialized, auxiliary operations and processes to support it and its integration into an existing
facility should be evaluated wholistically from a stormwater compliance management perspective.

5.2.2.4 Federal Effluent Limitation Guidelines

ELG regulate industrial discharges, both of wastewater and stormwater, from certain industrial sectors
and develop technology-based effluent limits which are used to set numerical limits in facilities’ operating
permits. Industrial wastewater dischargers are regulated by the NPDES rules in 40 CFR Part 122, which
also provides permit writers to implement effluent limitations and permit limits on a best professional
judgment (BPJ) basis. The integration of the CCS Facility into a NGCC facility means that the Site’s
operations are regulated by 40 CFR Part 423 for Stream Electric Power Generating Point Source Category.
The regulated pollutants in this ELG are provided in Table 5-5.

The limits provided in Table 5-5 are cumulatively applied to the discharge from any given facility, which
may present a challenge for complying with the discharge limit without additional on-site treatment
processes. Depending on the type of wastewater treatment system present at the Site, additional
treatment may be required for the discharge to meet effluent limits. None of the degradation products in
Table 5-4 appear in Appendix A to Part 423, which lists the 126 priority pollutants that are prohibited from
discharge in any quantity. Furthermore, none of the constituents identified in Table 5-5 are regulated by
the ELG for NGCC power plants. However, nitrate and nitrite are regulated by Part 423 for flue gas
desulfurization discharges which, while not applicable to a combined-cycle power plant, includes a daily
maximum discharge limit of 4 mg/L and 30-day average limit of 3 mg/L that could be considered by permit
writers as relevant to the development and implementation of a BPJ limit to incorporate into a discharge
permit. The analysis of nitrate and nitrite after 2,000 hours of bench-scale operation resulted in 363 mg/L
and 1 mg/L, respectively. If a BPJ or another such limit were implemented it would not be uncommon for
an internal outfall to be established at the point of generation in the process or to implement an
end-of-pipe limitation at the outfall, which could be assessed based on the expected dilution of the

123
ALL4 Project No. 001242-0004.00                                                                             November 2025

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

process between the points of generation and discharge. Chloride was also identified as a degradation
product and is expected to be relatively stable as an aqueous anion; however, high concentrations of
chloride can impact treatability at wastewater treatment plants. If ammonia stays in aqueous form and is
not otherwise treated or removed from the CCS Facility carbon capture loops, then it may contribute to
pollutant loading in a future discharge. High concentrations of either compound in discharges to surface
waters can contribute to aquatic toxicity. Interactions between the organic and inorganic degradation
products may be possible and lead to pollutants of concern, such as halogenated organics, which further
analysis may better characterize. The wastewater discharge from a future implementation of the CCS
Facility at a NGCC power plant will require characterization and will vary significantly based on the factors
discussed, but for the potential pollutants identified during this stage of experimentation, treatment
technologies exist and the ability to permit, construct, treat, and discharge wastewater is not anticipated
to impede commercialization.

5.2.2.5 State and Local Regulations

State and local regulations may impact each of the aforementioned, water-related topics. As the federal
ELG does not impact the CCS Facility or existing Site, as described previously, the driver for much of the
water permitting that will occur will be the Site location. Certain waterbodies have special state-derived
designations, for example “Outstanding Resource Waterbody” or “Exceptional Value Waterbody,” that
prohibit new or additional pollutants from being discharged to them. Similarly, surface waters may be
impaired for certain pollutants or have an existing TMDL and, depending on the state, there are various
implications for wastewater and stormwater discharges to those surface waters. Identification of these
water bodies is typically straightforward and should be reviewed during the siting evaluation in early
project planning to avoid a surface water that may impede the ability to be discharged to it. With proper
planning and early identification during the siting evaluation process, state and local regulations with
respect to water use and discharge are not anticipated to impede commercialization.

Waste management considerations for a commercial-scale CCS Facility were identified based on analysis
of potential waste generating activities from CCS Facility operations as described Subsection 5.3.1 and

5.3.1 Regulated Wastes Generated by CCS Facility

Regulated waste generated from a CCS Facility would primarily consist of out of specification, spilled, or
excess materials. These waste types are not atypical for an industrial facility and do not represent a unique
environmental concern. Waste can be minimized by using a well-managed material handling system to
avoid spills, contamination, and excessive inventory requiring disposal.

A waste determination will be required for each solid waste generated as part of the construction and
operation of the CCS Facility. No unique or special precautions were identified for waste that will be
generated. Based upon the project process information derived from the bench-scale model, the Site is
anticipated to generate and process solid wastes listed in Table 5-6.

5.3.2 Regulatory Considerations for Commercial-Scale CCS Facility

The treatment, storage, and disposal of solid, special, and hazardous wastes would be regulated by the
state in which the CCS Facility is constructed and operated. The Site would need to comply with all
requirements for permitting, licensing, and operating facilities generating and managing hazardous waste
with the delegated Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) administrator. All solid waste will be
required to be disposed of offsite in accordance with Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA)
requirements.

 5-36
126

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Hazardous waste generators must also register with U.S. EPA. With the integration of the CCS Facility, the
Site’s generator status would likely be large quantity generator (LQG). Therefore, any waste streams
generated at the Site will need to be evaluated for applicability to the RCRA Subtitle C regulations and
impact to hazardous waste generator status.

Based on the findings of this assessment, there is no acute hazardous waste that would have more severe
restrictions or management requirements than non-acute hazardous waste. Facility operations may be
optimized to minimize generation of regulated hazardous wastes following facility startup through a site-
specific pollution prevention project.

5.4 EMPLOYEE SAFETY AND ACCIDENTAL RELEASES

Employee safety and accidental releases considerations for a commercial-scale CCS Facility were
evaluated as described Subsections 5.4.1 and 5.4.2.

5.4.1 Employee Hazards

Employee hazards include physical and chemical hazards that are not unusual for an industrial setting.
Personnel working in these areas should be informed of the potential hazards with appropriate protective
measures and trained consistent with U.S. OSHA and industry standards.

For the purpose of this EH&S Assessment, it is assumed that the Site will have an established formal and
well-documented safety program where operations are subject to audit by both internal parties and state
and federal regulators. Training requirements for personnel are established and completion of required
training is tracked and subject to audit. Any new potential hazards at a NGCC power plant site caused by
the addition of the CCS Facility will be treated the same way as existing/known hazards present at a NGCC
power plant site. Appropriate protective measures and training should be implemented and documented
within the existing safety program established at the Site.

5.4.1.1 Noise

Given the hypothetical location of the Site, a complete review of state statues, state and local regulations,
and local ordinances governing the CCS Facility construction and operation could not be evaluated.
Therefore, the Site will need to evaluate and comply with any numerical limits for noise impacts from

 5-37
127

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

construction or operation of the CCS Facility set forth by state statues, state and local regulations, and
local ordinances after the final location is selected. In the absence of numerical noise impact criteria
specified by a statute, regulation, or ordinance, U.S. EPA guidance was relied upon for the qualitative noise
assessment provided in this report.

Under U.S. EPA guidance document 550/9-74-004, a 24-hour exposure level of 70 decibels is the level of
environmental noise which will prevent any measurable hearing loss over a lifetime. Likewise, levels of 55
decibels outdoors and 45 decibels indoors are identified as preventing activity interference and
annoyance. These levels of noise are considered those which will permit spoken conversation and other
activities such as sleeping, working, and recreation. The guidance levels are not applicable to single events
or "peak" levels; instead, they represent averages of acoustic energy over specified time periods (e.g.,
8-hour, 24-hour, or annual averaging periods).

U.S. EPA has indicated annual levels are sufficient to protect public health and welfare if they do not
exceed a Day-Night Average Sound Level (Ldn) of 55 decibels on the A Scale (dBA) outdoors in sensitive
areas such as residences, schools, and hospitals. The Ldn is the 24-hour average sound level with a 10
decibel (dB) penalty applied to the nighttime sound levels (between 10:00 p.m. and 7:00 a.m.) to account
for increased sensitivity to noise during nighttime hours.

Using this U.S. EPA guidance criteria, a qualitative noise impacts assessment of baseline conditions for a
typical NGCC power plant, Project construction activities, and CCS Facility operations was performed.
Baseline site conditions derived from recent ambient noise monitoring conducted at a representative
NGCC power plant [40] indicated the primary noises at the Site boundaries (considering a Site area of 100
acres) were generated from road traffic and not Site operations.

Major construction phases will consist of mobilization, foundation construction and excavating,
equipment installation, structure erection, and Site cleanup. Noise emissions will vary with each phase of
the construction depending on the construction activity and equipment which could include backhoes,
cranes, front end loaders, scrapers, supply trucks, dump trucks, and a variety of power tools. The Site may
need to limit high-noise specialized equipment usage to primary daytime construction hours between
7 a.m. – 9 p.m. under local ordinances. Review of noise modeling data for an analogous project [40],
indicates construction activity peak and average sound levels would be below U.S. EPA guidance
thresholds.

 5-38
128

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Based on the measurements and conclusions noted in analogous site noise modeling reports [41] [42],
short-term ambient measurements at a representative NGCC power plant site [40], and the estimated
CCS Facility operational noise levels, there is no predicted increase in current operational noise levels at
the Site boundary when operating a CCS Facility co-located with a NGCC EGU.

5.4.1.2 Hazardous Materials Exposure

SDS indicate that the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent could cause acute or chronic health effects to Site
personnel. Improper material handling could result in serious inhalation, dermal, or ingestion hazards.
Storage hazards indicated in the SDS are summarized in Section 5.6.2. These hazards apply to storage and
process area work as well. Eyewash and safety showers are warranted near storage and process areas.
Worker safety procedures (including a process specific hazard assessment) as well as material handling,
line break, and spill procedures will be required to minimize any impact on the employees or the
environment in the event of a spill or release of hazardous materials.

The degree of hazard associated with CCS Facility materials is no greater than the hazards of commonly
used materials at a commercial-scale fossil fuel-fired power plant. Appropriate employee precautions and
spill prevention and response procedures should minimize potential employee hazards at the Site.

Table 5-7 provides a summary of the hazardous properties for CCS Facility chemicals related to volatility,
