5.4.1.3 Primary Loop

Potential physical hazards of the Primary Loop include falls from heights, exposure to elevated
temperatures of combustion gases and scrubber solvent, and noise. Solvent temperatures throughout the
Primary Loop process range from approximately 40°C to 140°C. Flue gas temperatures may be higher.
Personnel are not expected to have direct exposure to these materials and temperatures other than
during process sampling, system startup/shutdown, and non-routine line break procedures. Employees
will need to be trained for the health hazards of these chemicals and instructed to follow standard
operating procedures, follow chemical hygiene protocols, and wear appropriate personal protective
equipment (PPE). The appropriate PPE for sampling and line break procedures would include tight fitting
safety goggles, face shield, chemical protective clothing, boots, and impermeable gloves.

The desktop review performed under this portion of the EH&S evaluation did not suggest excessive noise
exposure; however, verification of extended duration exposure to noise levels for a commercial-scale CCS
Facility should be performed to determine if hearing conservation program requirements will be
applicable to the CCS Facility.

These hazards are not unusual for a power plant and appropriate employee safety training and practices
should minimize the potential for harm.

5.4.1.4 Secondary Loop

Potential physical hazards of the Secondary Loop also include falls from heights, exposure to elevated
temperatures from the electrochemical regenerator, and noise. The operating temperature of the
electrochemical regenerator ranges from approximately 70-80°C. Flue gas exiting the Primary Loop may
also be at higher temperatures. Personnel are not expected to have direct exposure to these materials
and temperatures other than during process sampling, system startup/shutdown, and non-routine line
break procedures. Employees will need to be trained for the health hazards of these chemicals and
instructed to follow Standard Operating Procedures, follow chemical hygiene protocols, and wear
appropriate PPE. The appropriate PPE for sampling and line break procedures would include tight fitting
safety goggles, face shield, chemical protective clothing, boots, and impermeable gloves.

 5-42
132

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

5.4.1.5 Electrical Hazards

Data from a commercial process simulation [43] was used to identify general electrical safety
requirements for the Site. Operation of the equipment of the CCS Facility involves high voltages and
currents, which can introduce several electrical hazards that must be mitigated to ensure operator safety
and system reliability. The Site owner and operators should be mindful of electrical hazards, including the
following:

  Electrical shock – workers may inadvertently make contact with an energized conductor or circuit
 part, leading to electrical shock.

  Arc flashes – this entails a sudden release of electrical energy from a fault, which can create
 intense heat, light, and pressure. Arc flashes may cause equipment damage, severe burns, hearing
 loss, eye injuries, lung damage, and complications from blast injuries.

Various mitigation strategies and protective measures can be implemented to address these hazards, with
extensive guidance provided by U.S. OSHA, the National Fire Protection Association (NFPA), and the
National Electric Code (NEC). General requirements for electrical installations, including grounding and
insulation (29 CFR §1910.303) and the use of PPE for employees working in hazardous areas (29 CFR
§1910.335) must be included in the Site’s established electrical safety program for the CCS Facility.
Workers dealing with or close to electrical components should be equipped with appropriate PPE,
including insulating gloves, flame-resistant clothing and face shields to protect against electrical shock and
arc flash incidents. The requirements of NFPA 70E would be required for staging of safe work zones with
boundaries, hazard assessments, and proper labeling of electrical equipment to prevent electrical shock
or arc flash incidents. Additional workplace electrical safety requirements include comprehensive training
programs for workers on recognizing and mitigating electrical hazards, frequent inspection and
maintenance of electrical components to prevent faults, and clear procedures for handling electrical
emergencies, including evacuation and first aid for electrical injuries. The CCS Facility electrical systems
and components would require incorporation into the Site’s established electrical safety program.

5.4.1.6 Lockout/Tagout

Energy sources including electrical, mechanical, hydraulic, pneumatic, chemical, thermal, or other sources
in machines and equipment can be hazardous to workers. During the servicing and maintenance of
machines and equipment, the unexpected startup or release of stored energy can result in serious injury

 5-43
133

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

or death to workers. Proper Lockout/Tagout (LOTO) procedures must be in place to safeguard workers
from hazardous energy releases. U.S. OSHA 29 CFR §1910.147 requires employee training, development
of LOTO procedures and prohibition against attempting to restart or reenergize machines or other
equipment that are locked or tagged out. The CCS Facility would require incorporation into the Site’s
established LOTO program.

5.4.1.7 Confined Space Safety

The CCS Facility uses process equipment, including storage vessels, which meet U.S. OSHA’s definition of
“confined spaces.” These vessels are classified as confined spaces due to their design, which restricts the
ability of employees to enter, perform work inside, or exit without difficulty. Confined spaces, by
definition, are large enough for an employee to enter fully and perform assigned work; are not designed
for continuous occupancy; and have limited or restricted means of entry or exit. Those vessels have the
potential to engulf employees who enter the space and the potential to contain hazardous atmospheres
upon entry. Entry into the vessels additionally meet U.S. OSHA’s definition of a permit-required confined
space. 29 CFR 1910.146(c) establishes the general requirements for permit-required confined spaces; the
employer will be required to inform exposed employees, by posting danger signs or by any other equally
effective means, of the existence and location of and the danger posed by the permit-required spaces.
Furthermore, this regulation stipulates that if the employer decides that its employees will enter permit
spaces, the employer must develop and implement a written permit space program. Additionally, a
confined space rescue plan will be required for confined spaces, or if applicable, a permit in accordance
with the requirements described in 29 CFR §1910.146. The written program must be available for
inspection by employees and their authorized representatives.

Incorporation of CCS Facility confined spaces into a site’s established confined space program is not an
unusual requirement for a NGCC power plant facility and does not represent an obstacle towards
commercialization of this carbon capture technology.

5.4.1.8 Impact Mitigation Strategies

Potential employee exposure to hazardous chemicals should be minimized with appropriate work
practices and employee protective equipment. Concentrated forms of the solvents were identified as
moderate acute hazards. Dilute concentrations used onsite represented a lower hazard potential. Their

 5-44
134

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

use requires particular care and caution, but it does not represent a risk that would be expected to prevent
the implementation or permitting of this carbon capture technology. With consideration of electrical
hazards and hazardous energy, it is recommended that the employer implement LOTO procedures and
require verification of a zero-energy state before performing maintenance or repairs on electrically
powered subsystems. Safety around electrical hazards also entails the establishment of preventative
maintenance procedures for electrical equipment and metal components of the CCS Facility process to
mitigate the potential for arc flashes. To adhere to the standards of confined space safety, it is suggested
that the CCS Facility unit operator conduct a hazard evaluation of confined spaces and label all permit
required confined spaces.

5.4.2 Precautions and Accidental Release Measures

General precautions, accidental release measures, and first aid considerations for the safe handling and
storage of materials associated with CCS Facility operations are discussed in Subsections 5.4.2.1 through
5.4.2.3.

5.4.2.1 Material Storage

Several materials including the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent, 99.5% KOH pellets, 99.5% KHCO 3 powder,
liquified CO2, and compressed H2 will be stored onsite as part of the CCS Facility. The UKy IDEA Water-
Lean Solvent, 99.5% KOH pellets, and 99.5% KHCO3 powder will be delivered by rail or truck and stored in
ASTs, totes, drums, or other containers. Liquified CO2 and compressed H2 may be stored in pressurized
ASTs or compressed gas cylinders.

When exposed to humid air, KOH can form a mild caustic mist or aerosol as it reacts with water vapor.
KOH dust from solid pellets may be produced during handling and transfer, and if it becomes airborne, it
will be corrosive. Protective measures and controls should be implemented for solid KOH, including
storage in dry, airtight containers to minimize moisture absorption, and ventilation in storage and
handling areas to dissipate particulate matter or mist produced by incidental moisture absorption. KHCO 3
powder is also planned for onsite storage. This compound is not expected to pose significant air quality
concerns, as it is relatively stable and does not readily form harmful aerosols or gases under typical storage
conditions. There is the potential for KHCO3 to become a fine mist or dust, in which case, the
recommended measures and controls for managing solid KOH are also applicable.

 5-45
135

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

The Site will need to comply with all local, state, and federal requirements for operating and maintaining
ASTs; however, the materials stored as part of the CCS Facility do not differ significantly from common
materials stored at other manufacturing facilities and NGCC power plants. Compressed gases will need to
be stored in accordance with the requirements identified in 29 CFR §1910.101. Based on the above, the
storage of these materials is not anticipated to impede commercialization.

5.4.2.2 Accidental Release Measures

In the event of an accidental release from the commercial-scale CCS Facility, immediate and
well-coordinated action is essential to mitigate EH&S risks. Potential accidental releases should be
addressed within the Site’s established Emergency Response Plan, in accordance with U.S. OSHA standard
29 CFR §1910.138. The most common release potentials include leaks or spills of the CO 2 capture solvents,
degraded solvent byproducts, and the unintended release of captured CO 2 gas and cogenerated H2 gas.
Each potential for release must be addressed systematically.

The UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent is corrosive, volatile at operating temperatures, and capable of
releasing vapors that have the potential to cause respiratory irritation, chemical burns, and eye damage.
If a release were to occur, the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent may vaporize quickly, particularly in high-
temperature areas, leading to potential airborne exposure risks. If the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent has
undergone thermal degradation, more hazardous byproducts such as ammonia or trace nitrosamines may
also be present, increasing the toxicological risk. Therefore, containment of the liquid and control of
vapors would be prioritized in the event of an accidental release.

KOH is a strong alkali that, in the event of a spill, can impact soil and nearby water bodies, leading to
alkaline conditions that may harm or kill vegetation and aquatic life. The major human health hazard (and
the mode of action) of KOH is local irritation and/or corrosion. Neutralization with an acid (such as acetic
acid) may be necessary.

KHCO₃ is less caustic than KOH but can still affect pH by slightly increasing alkalinity, potentially disrupting
the natural pH balance of soil and water. Elevated bicarbonate levels may impact aquatic ecosystems,
possibly leading to eutrophication and reduced oxygen levels in water bodies. While the impact is
generally less severe than that of KOH, the major human health hazard (and the mode of action) of KHCO₃
is local irritation and/or corrosion. Neutralization with an acid (such as acetic acid) may be necessary.

 5-46
136

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

In the event of an accidental release of liquified CO2, there would be a potential risk of oxygen
displacement, particularly in enclosed environments. Liquified CO 2 has the potential to cause frostbite
upon contact with skin and may create over-pressurization hazards, if rapidly expanding. Ventilation
within the area of the accidental release should be prioritized and monitoring for oxygen levels is essential.
In the event of an accidental release, the CCS Facility should be safely shut down or isolated to prevent
further releases, and incident notification protocols followed, including regulatory reporting, if required.
A post incident review would include inspection of equipment integrity, root cause analysis, and
procedural updates to reduce the likelihood of recurrence.

In the event of accidental release of compressed H2, the primary concern would be ignition of the H2 gas
and/or an explosion. Like any flammable fuel, H2 gas can combust. However, hydrogen’s buoyancy,
diffusivity and small molecular size make it difficult to contain and create a combustible situation.
Additionally, the likelihood of H2 gas exploding in open air is low, due to hydrogen’s tendency to rise
quickly. This is the opposite for heavier gases such as propane or gasoline fumes, which are denser and
stay close to the ground. As with an accidental release of CO2, in the event of an accidental release of
compressed H2, the CCS Facility should be safely shut down or isolated to prevent further releases, and
incident notification protocols followed, including regulatory reporting, as applicable. A post incident
review would include inspection of equipment integrity, root cause analysis, and procedural updates to
reduce the likelihood of recurrence. Care should be taken to eliminate any ignition source located near
the H2 release.

Common emergency spill response provisions for the types of chemicals used in the CCS Facility include:

  Avoid contact with skin, eyes, and clothing;
  Do not breathe vapors or spray mist;
  Notify a supervisor immediately;
  Evacuate personnel to safe areas;
  Keep people away from and upwind of spill/leak;
  Stop leak if safe to do so without risk;
  Ventilate the area using a fan that will not cause ignition of the material;
  Spill response team should wear appropriate PPE prior to handling spilled material. Consult the
 SDS for the substance for full details; and

 5-47
137

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

  After the response is complete, dispose of materials in accordance with applicable regulations.
  Notifications to regulatory agencies (e.g., State and Local Agencies and Fire Department)
 depending on the hazardous material and quantity released.

5.5 OTHER REGULATORY CONSIDERATIONS

EH&S considerations for hazardous materials transportation; Comprehensive Environmental Response,
Compensation, and Liability Act (CERCLA); Emergency Planning and Community Right to Know Act
(EPCRA); and the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA) are discussed in Subsections 5.5.1 through 5.5.4.

5.5.1 Hazardous Materials Transportation

For product gases produced by the CCS Facility and not utilized onsite, the Site will need to transport the
saleable gases by pipeline, rail, or truck. For this EH&S Assessment, pipeline transport of liquified CO 2 to
an offsite saline formation and truck or rail transport of compressed H2 and O2 gases were considered.
However, onsite storage and offsite transport of liquified CO2 and compressed gases were not evaluated
using a detailed engineering analysis in the TEA Report; therefore, this EH&S Assessment considers
general EH&S considerations for product transport of CO 2, H2., and O2.

5.5.1.1 Liquified CO2 Transportation

CO2 produced as a product of the CCS Facility will need to be transported offsite. Anticipated EH&S
requirements to transport CO2 offsite via pipeline were evaluated. Liquified CO2 is considered a hazardous
material and dangerous good. The class and division of liquified CO2 according to 49 CFR §172.101 is Class
2, Division 2, which encompasses all non-flammable compressed gases.

The Department of Transportation (DOT) Pipeline and Hazardous Materials Safety Administration
(PHMSA) regulations are applicable to liquified CO2 transport regardless of the method of transport. There
are no requirements to obtain a permit through PHMSA for the transportation of liquified CO 2. However,
all hazardous material handling regulations need to be followed due to liquified CO 2 being classified as a
hazardous material.

The transport of liquified CO2. offsite by pipeline would require the construction of a pipeline to transport
liquified CO2. directly from the CCS Facility or storage tanks located at the Site to an offsite saline
formation for long-term, permanent sequestration of CO2. To construct a CO2 pipeline, required
permitting would include a National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) review and state-specific NPDES
General Permits for Stormwater Discharges for any interstate pipeline construction. Additional
construction approval may be required by state specific siting boards.

 5-49
139

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

If a pipeline has a federal nexus, such as crossing federal lands, waterways, or involving federal funding,
the project will trigger a review under the NEPA and potential CWA Section 404 permitting. If the pipeline
crosses through waterways or wetlands, the lead federal agency (e.g., the Bureau of Land Management
or U.S. Army Corps of Engineers) would oversee the environmental review and determine whether the
project can proceed as planned, or if modifications are necessary.

The federal administration is currently converting NEPA regulations into agency-specific guidance or
agency-specific regulations; therefore, NEPA requirements for any CO 2 pipeline considered under this
EH&S assessment are subject to change and not addressed in additional detail. Potentially applicable
NEPA assessments to the Project require examination of impacts to air quality, soil health, groundwater,
surface water, biological and cultural resources, visual impacts, and noise.

For pipeline construction, a U.S. ACE permit would be required when discharging dredged or fill material
into Waters of the United States (WOTUS) including navigable waters and wetlands. A wetland delineation
is required to determine the boundary of wetlands on a given property. The following U.S. ACE permits
may apply to the construction of a CO2 pipeline in addition to construction permits discussed in this report.
The process to obtain a U.S. ACE permit can be lengthy and can take up to one year.

U.S. ACE Individual (Standard) Permit
  This permit applies to projects that disturb the nation’s navigable waters and have more than
 minimal individual or cumulative impacts, must be evaluated using additional environmental
 criteria, and will involve a more comprehensive public interest review.

U.S. ACE NWP 58
  This is a general permit that can be issued for projects that result in minimal adverse effects and
 fall under one of the 41 established Nationwide Permit categories.
  Applies to a “utility line” defined as any pipe or pipeline for the transportation of any gaseous,
 liquid, liquescent, or slurry substance, for any purpose that is not oil, natural gas, or
 petrochemicals. Examples of activities authorized by this NWP include utility lines that convey
 water, sewage, stormwater, wastewater, brine, irrigation water, and industrial products that are
 not petrochemicals (i.e., CO2, H2, and O2).
  The project may not result in the loss of greater than 1/2-acre of WOTUS.
  A Pre-Construction Notification (PCN) is required for discharges that result in the loss of greater
 than 1/10-acre, the construction of a new pipeline greater than 250 miles long, or a Section 10
 permit is required.

  For utility line activities crossing a single waterbody more than one time at separate and distant

5.5.1.2 Compressed Gas Transportation

Compressed H2 and O2 produced as products from the CCS Facility’s Secondary Loop will need to be
transported offsite. Anticipated EH&S requirements to transport compressed H 2 and O2 gases offsite via
rail or truck were evaluated for this EH&S Assessment.

Compressed H2 is considered a hazardous material and dangerous good. The class and division of
compressed H2 according to 49 CFR §172.101 is Class 2, Division 1, which encompasses all flammable
compressed gases.

Compressed O2 is considered a hazardous material and dangerous good. The class and division of
compressed H2 according to 49 CFR §172.101 is Class 2, Division 2, which encompasses non-flammable,
nonpoisonous compressed gases.

PHMSA has regulations applicable to the transport of these compressed gases regardless of the method
of transport. There are no requirements to obtain a permit through PHMSA for the transportation of
compressed H2 or O2. However, all hazardous material handling regulations need to be followed due to
 5-51
141

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

compressed gas classifications under PMHSA regulations. In considering the transportation of compressed
H2 and O2 via railway or truck, it is presumed that an external company would be contracted for either
transportation method. An external transportation company would be responsible for any applicable
permits. Therefore, it should be confirmed that any external companies hired for transportation of
compressed H2 and O2 by either truck or rail have the permits required for Class 2.1 materials.

5.5.1.2.1 Compressed Gas Transport Via Railway

One method of compressed gas transport offsite would be railway transport utilizing a Site railway spur.
Compressed gases would be transported from storage tanks to railcars onsite via pipeline or utilizing
trucks. If an onsite pipeline is to be constructed to transport compressed gases from storage tanks to rail
containers a NEPA review would be required. Additionally, a NPDES General Permit for Stormwater
Discharges Associated with Construction Activities would be required if land disturbing activities occur at
or above thresholds identified in state-specific NPDES permitting programs.

If a compressed gas tanker is used for transporting from storage tanks to railcars, DOT and PHMSA
hazardous material regulations must be adhered to, although no permits are required to be obtained. The
regulations pertaining to shipping compressed gases are found in 49 CFR Part 173. This includes the
registration of the tanker with PHMSA. Additionally, if the gross weight of the compressed gases exceed
1,000 pounds, placarding on the tanker is required.

The contracted railway company would be responsible for obtaining the necessary permitting. All
applicable regulations regarding transportation of compressed gases by railway are in 49 CFR Part 174.
There are no explicit permits that need to be obtained from the Federal Railroad Administration (FRA) if
a third party is hired for transporting compressed gases. However, the Site would need to register with
DOT as a shipper, obtain proper rail containers, and follow all applicable regulations outlined in 49 CFR to
maintain compliance with the FRA.

5.5.1.2.2 Compressed Gas Transport Via Truck

Another method for compressed gas transport offsite is via cryogenic liquid tanker trucks, gaseous tube
trailers, or gas cylinders. Liquified or compressed H 2 or O2 gases would be transferred from storage tanks
into a corresponding truck container or cylinder. The regulations pertaining to shipping cryogenic or

 5-52
142

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

compressed gases are found in 49 CFR Part 173. This includes the registration of the shipper with PHMSA.
Additionally, if the gross weight of the cryogenic or compressed gas exceeds 1,000 pounds, placarding on
the shipper is required.

The contracted trucking company would be responsible for obtaining the necessary permitting. All
applicable regulations regarding transportation of compressed gas by truck are in 49 CFR Part 177. The
Site would need to register with DOT as a shipper, obtain proper cylinders or truck containers, and follow
all applicable regulations outlined in 49 CFR Part 173 to maintain compliance.

5.5.2 Comprehensive Environmental Response and Liability Act

CERCLA provides a federal "Superfund" to clean up uncontrolled or abandoned hazardous-waste sites as
well as accidents, spills, and other emergency releases of pollutants and contaminants into the
environment. Through CERCLA, U.S. EPA has authority to seek out those parties responsible for any
release and assure their cooperation in the cleanup. The four major provisions under CERCLA include the
establishment of a Superfund for cleanup, a liability framework for responsible parties, a structured
response process for hazardous releases, and mandated community involvement in remediation efforts.

Because the Site is a hypothetical greenfield, CERCLA would not be applicable unless or until a regulated
release would occur. However, the Site selection process should assess contiguous property parcel(s) for
potential contamination that could migrate to the site and potentially subject the Site to mitigation and
response requirements under CERCLA. A properly conducted Phase I ESA should identify “recognized
environmental conditions” that could prevent development of the Site for power generation with carbon
capture due to its proximity to subsurface contamination or regulated CERCLA release.

5.5.3 Emergency Planning and Community Right-to-Know Act

Planning and reporting requirements under Sections 311 and 312 of EPCRA apply for total amounts of
hazardous chemicals stored onsite in excess of 10,000 pounds or in quantities greater than the threshold
planning quantity (TPQ), whichever is lower [44]. If thresholds are exceeded, the Site would be required
to submit initial notification, SDS, and Tier II reports to the State Emergency Response Commission with
jurisdiction, the Local Emergency Planning Committee (LEPC), and the local fire department. The
commercial-scale facility will need to perform the reporting under EPCRA Sections 311 and 312 for CCS

 5-53
143

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Facility raw materials and compressed gases stored onsite. These are common requirements for an
industrial facility and do not represent significant environmental concern. Onsite storage of the UKy IDEA
Water-Lean Solvent at a commercial-scale facility will exceed these reporting thresholds. Reporting under
this program is required.

Additional provisions of EPCRA apply to Extremely Hazardous Substances (EHSs) which are listed in Title
40 of the Code of Federal Regulations, Part 355, Appendices A and B. No EHSs were identified in the
materials stored for this project. Although there are no EHSs being stored, a few chemicals that are
additionally listed EHSs are ammonia, formaldehyde, and two nitrosamines. These chemicals would be in
air emissions from the process and would need to be evaluated with respect to releases above reporting
thresholds. However, the provisions of EPCRA Section 303 only apply to storage of EHSs.

Under Section 313 of EPCRA, the Site is required to report EPCRA Section 313 listed chemicals in quantities
exceeding the established thresholds for manufacture, process, or otherwise used including the amount
released to air, water, land, or transferred offsite for disposal. The Site will likely trigger reporting for
chemicals used in the CCS Facility under the U.S. EPA Toxics Release Inventory (TRI) program. Reported
releases are publicly available through various online tools and resources to inform the public about
chemical releases and waste management practices in their communities.

5.5.4 Toxic Substances Control Act

A facility planning or operating carbon capture projects must ensure that any solvent they use, including
amine solutions or blends, complies with TSCA regulations, especially concerning new chemicals and
chemicals that the U.S. EPA determines to pose an unreasonable risk. TSCA regulates chemicals in the U.S.
by giving the U.S. EPA authority to gather information, require testing, assess risk, and control chemical
exposure to protect human health and the environment. Key regulatory areas include chemical inventory
reporting, pre-manufacture notice (PMN) for new chemicals, risk evaluation and management for existing
chemicals, and reporting/record-keeping requirements for manufacturers and importers.

Manufacturers of new chemical substances, including new carbon capture solvents, are required to
submit a PMN to the U.S. EPA for review before they can be introduced into commerce. The Chemical
Data Reporting (CDR) rule requires manufacturers and importers to report data on chemical production
and use every four years. Under TSCA Section 6, the U.S. EPA conducts risk evaluations for existing

 5-54
144

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

chemical substances already in commerce if an "unreasonable risk" to health or the environment is
identified requiring the agency take action using the following risk-based prioritization and evaluation
process for chemicals ion commerce:

  Prioritization: The U.S. EPA identifies and designates existing chemicals as either high-priority for
 risk evaluation or low-priority. Factors considered include toxicity, exposure, and production
 volume.
  Risk evaluation: High-priority chemicals undergo a formal risk evaluation to determine if they
 pose an "unreasonable risk" under their conditions of use. This evaluation also considers risks to
 "potentially exposed and susceptible subpopulations," such as children.
  Risk management: If a chemical is found to present an unreasonable risk, the U.S. EPA must take
 action within two years to eliminate that risk through regulatory controls. The EPA has a range of
 options, from labeling and record-keeping requirements to use restrictions and outright bans.

Under this authority, the U.S. EPA can require manufacturers, importers, or processors to conduct testing
on chemicals if there is insufficient information to assess risks or if a potential unreasonable risk exists.
Additionally, the U.S. EPA Significant new use rule (SNUR) can trigger a review process similar to the PMN
for new chemicals if an existing chemical is used for a significant new use (e.g., carbon capture). During
the review period, the U.S. EPA assesses whether the new chemical or significant new use presents an
"unreasonable risk" to human health or the environment, without considering costs or benefits.

The risk of U.S. EPA applying the SNUR to UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent is low because it is widely known
that amine-based solvents are used in carbon capture processes and their use has not been found to
present an unacceptable risk. Review of CCS Facility raw materials did not identify any regulated chemicals
proposed for use that are likely to become subject to risk evaluations under TSCA that could preclude or
significantly delay their use in the CCS Facility.

5.6 TOXICOLOGICAL EFFECTS

Based on the literature review provided in Section 4 and EH&S evaluations of air quality management,
water management, waste management, employee safety, accidental releases, and other regulatory
considerations in the preceding subsections, toxicological effects from CCS Facility operations were
evaluated as described Subsection through 5.6.1 through 5.6.3 to characterize risk from CCS Facility
operations as presented in Subsection 5.6.4.

 5-55
145

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

5.6.1 Materials And Methods

To evaluate potential health risks from the CCS Facility, ALL4 relied upon an assessment of a 0.7 MWe
pilot plant CCS which was presented in a prior EH&S report evaluating an extrapolation to
commercial-scale (650 MWe) of a similar PCCCS technology developed by UKy IDEA [6].

The methodology used to conduct the risk evaluation in 2020 was consistent with the Risk Assessment
Guidance for Superfund (RAGS) developed by U.S. EPA and generally accepted toxicological and risk
assessment practices. The evaluation focused on potential health risks related to possible direct exposures
to a UKy IDEA Proprietary Solvent, ammonia, and nitrosamines analyzed using limit of detection (LoD)
methodology developed from air emissions testing at EWB Generating Station pilot plant CCS.

Calculations were completed based on theoretical potential exposures during testing sessions based on
run times provided by UKy IDEA. Because specific data and information (e.g., measured ambient air
concentrations) for actual personnel exposures were not available, extrapolations were made from
available information and previous analyses.

Specific quantitative toxicity values and risk-based screening level (RSL) concentrations for ammonia and
nitrosamines were available from U.S. EPA [45]. There are no published RSLs for the alkanolamine
components of the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent.

Under this project, UKy IDEA operated its bench-scale dual-loop CCS for almost 2,000 hours; however,
UKy IDEA did not produce new testing data for nitrosamine concentrations from the bench-scale primary
loop exhaust steam under this project. Instead, UKy IDEA stated that nitrosamines were not detected at
the same detection levels established in the 2019 campaign in its technical responses to the authors of
this report. Therefore, the total run time of 512 hours during the 2019 testing campaign was relied upon
to characterize risk from the CCS Facility assessed under this report. Additional testing on a larger scale
for a longer duration would be warranted to confirm the observations and conclusions presented in this
EH&S Assessment.

The primary and immediate health concern identified for the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent is the
corrosive nature of the material. Direct exposure to the solvent may cause severe skin irritation, damage
to the eyes, mouth, throat, and respiratory system. Some of the compounds may also cause allergic
reactions and a few have been identified as reproductive toxins. If these exposures are prevented through

 5-56
146

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

proper process and sampling technique, the use of PPE, and with proper engineering and administrative
controls, then the corrosive nature and other toxicities of the solvents will be mitigated.

A health concern for nitrosamines is the potential cancer risk from exposure to used solvents. The U.S.
EPA Integrated Risk Information System (IRIS) [46] identifies many nitrosamines as Group B2 (probable
human carcinogens). Nitrosamines are considered to pose a potential cancer risk even at very low levels
of exposure.

The formation of nitrosamines from the solvents during the CO2 capture process and the presence of
nitrosamines in the bulk solvent may be problematic. Inhalation exposures to nitrosamines in ambient air
or may pose a cancer risk to Site personnel at very low levels of exposure [i.e., parts-per-trillion (ppt)].

A complete exposure assessment quantifies the potential level(s) of exposure to specific compounds and
their environmental concentration(s). Due to the improbability of significant exposure to CCS Facility
chemicals via ingestion or dermal exposure, inhalation was the primary route of potential exposure
evaluated in the assessment.

The 2019 testing protocol included analysis of ammonia, amine, and seven nitrosamines:
N-nitro alkanolamine iperidine (NPIP), NDMA, N-nitrosomethylethylamine (NMEA), NDEA, nitrosodi-
n-propylamine (NDPA), N-nitrosopyrrolidine (NPY), and N-nitroso-n-butylamine (NDBA). Because the
UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent is similar to carbon capture solvents evaluated in other referenced U.S. DOE
sponsored projects with similar emissions, similar non-detectable levels of amine and nitrosamine
compounds were presumed for air emissions consistent with the findings reported in 2019.

5.6.2 Toxicity Assessment

The 2019 toxicity assessment evaluated acute, chronic, non-cancer, and cancer endpoints for a
proprietary solvent, solvent degradation products, and process reaction products. The assessment was
conducted using information obtained from U.S. EPA IRIS, scientific literature, and SDS. The
chemical-specific toxicity profiles present the human health RSLs and ecological screening levels (ESL),
when available. For many substances, no specific toxicity information or screening values could be
identified. A summary of information available for the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent components and the
KOH Solvent is provided herein.

5.6.2.3 Potential Degradation Products Evaluated

Under this Project, samples of UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent (containing A2P, Tetraglyme, and water) from
the bench-scale CCS were analyzed using High Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) and mass
spectrometry (MS) using a semiquantitative analysis method to identify potential thermal degradation
compounds. The analysis identified N,N′-bis(2-hydroxypropyl)urea, 5-methyl-2-oxazolidinone (MeOZD),
and 1-[(2-amino-1-methylethyl)amino]-2-propanol (AMEA2P), which contains both primary and
secondary amine groups. After 2,000 hours of operation in the Bench Scale CCS, N,N′-bis(2-
hydroxypropyl)urea reached approximately 424 ppm as shown in Figure 5-1 [47]. While this N,N′-bis(2-
hydroxypropyl)urea is stable, MeOZD is an unstable intermediate at 195 ppm and can convert to AMEA2P
by reacting with another free 1A2P molecule, which reached approximately 444 ppm during the Bench
Scale CCS long-term testing campaign. Total thermal degradation products, including A2P Urea, MeOZD,
and AMEA2P reached 1,063 mg/L after 2,000 hours of operation in the Bench Scale CCS [47].

5.6.3 Exposure Assessment

As stated previously, the 2019 exposure assessment focused solely upon inhalation risks. If incidental
ingestion of process solvent occurs during a spill or accidental release, then an immediate health and
safety response is warranted due to the corrosive/irritant nature of these materials.

Exposure to the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent may occur in the workplace during handling/use of the
materials. Exposure to the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent and related degradation and reaction products
may occur away from the Facility (offsite) through air emissions and wastewater discharges. The offsite
exposure scenarios include both industrial and residential receptors.

5.6.3.1 Workplace Exposures

Exposures in the workplace may occur during storage, use, and handling of CCS Facility chemicals.
Workplace ambient air concentrations of the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent or other chemicals used in the
CCS Facility should be monitored and controlled to concentrations less than the U.S. OSHA PEL/ACGIH
TLV. Dermal or eye exposures to the solvents and other chemicals should be avoided through the use of
engineering controls or PPE, to minimize the risk of irritation. Potential exposures to degradation products
in workplace air and/or wastewater may also be possible.

5.6.3.2 Offsite Air Exposures

Exposures to the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent and degradation products may occur through inhalation
of offsite migration of air discharges. Bench-scale testing under this project estimated amine air emissions
from the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent to be less than 1 ppm, consistent with prior investigations. Other

 5-62
152

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

literature [14] identifies ammonia as the predominant air emissions with measured releases up to 305
milligrams per normal cubic meter (mg/Nm3) and amine emissions ranging from less than 0.1 to 11
mg/Nm3.

Published emissions rates of nitrosamines are primarily limited to studies completed in Australia [49] [50]
and Norway [8]. Low-level detection of nitrosamines has proven difficult. Nitrosamine air emissions were
not detected at a small-scale (1.2 tonne/day CO2 capture) pilot plant in Australia [50]. Comparable results
were anticipated for the UKy IDEA bench-scale facility (0.13 tonne/day CO 2 capture), which is about 10%
of the size of the referenced pilot plant in Australia, and no nitrosamine compounds were detected in air
emissions monitored.

Literature review identified nitrosamine emissions ranging from less than 1 to 3.1 µg/m 3 and nitramine
emissions ranging from 0.005 to an estimated 40 µg/m 3 [14]. As discussed in Section 4.1, nitrosamine air
emissions dispersion rates modeled in China [13] indicated relatively low or no risk to onsite workers or
surrounding communities for PCCCS scenarios at or below 146,000 tonnes CO 2 captured annually. At this
time, it is unclear whether the magnitude of increase in CO2 removal for a commercial-scale system will
correspond with a linear increase in nitrosamine emissions and, if so, whether ambient concentrations
may represent a more serious concern.

5.6.3.3 Offsite Water Exposures

Some of the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent and degradation products may be contained in wastewater
generated by the CCS Facility. Literature review [14] [50] suggests the main amine losses from a PCCCS
would be to wastewater from the absorber water wash control systems and that the amines could be
sources for nitrosamine formation. In studies of amine based PCCCS at the CO 2 TCM in Norway, no
nitrosamines or nitramines were detected in wastewater. Exposures to the wastewater directly or
exposures to surface water affected by the wastewater discharge or the deposition of the compounds
from air may occur. Deposition of the compounds from air to surface water may occur; however,
deposition of primary loop solvent or degradation compounds is expected to be minimal based on a study
of the amine-based PCCCS at the CO2 TCM [16] which found:

 “Most amines and amino acids used in carbon capture are highly soluble in water. The
 same is expected for the corresponding nitrosamines and nitramines. It is thus expected
 that these will dissolve in rain and fog droplets and ultimately be precipitated and end up

 5-63
153

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

 in surface water, rivers and lakes. The baseline studies conducted by the Norwegian
 Institute for Water Research (NIVA) and NILU were not able to show detectable amounts
 of nitramines or nitrosamines in the environment surrounding the CO2 TCM. The
 surveillance activities did, however, detect amines in air, soil, moss and water.”

Under this project, testing of the bench-scale CCS solvent stream analyzed samples of the UKy IDEA
Water-Lean Solvent and Secondary Loop solvent after continuous operating campaigns. Elevated chloride
levels ranging from 841 to 1316 ppm were identified, likely attributed to the source water and the
polyvinyl chloride (PVC) piping and packing materials in the polishing loop [47]. The observed elevated
chloride concentrations are likely the result of tetraglyme degrading PVC components within the
bench-scale CCS and leaching chloride into the CCS solvent streams. Tetraglyme is not compatible with
PVC, and damage to PVC tubing or seals can cause leaks, spills, and potential exposure to hazardous
chemicals. Use of compatible stainless steel vessels in a commercial-scale CCS Facility should mitigate the
potential for elevated chloride in CCS Facility capture solvent streams.

5.6.4 Risk Characterization

Risk characterization integrates the toxicity of a compound with the opportunity or level of exposure to
estimate the level of human health or ecological risks related to exposures to the compound. Lifetime
inhalation exposure to nitrosamines is the primary potential exposure risk for the constituents analyzed
for the CCS Facility in this EH&S evaluation and the prior 2019 evaluation for analogous technology [5].
Because the 2019 testing did not detect any of the nitrosamine compounds above the level of detection,
a value of one-half of the lowest detection level for each compound was used as a surrogate concentration
for the exposure concentration for the risk characterization. These values were compared to the
appropriate U.S. EPA RSL to provide a “worst-case” exposure scenario and risk characterization.

RSLs are based on a full-time industrial worker exposure scenario. These screening risk levels assume
workplace exposures occurring 8 hours per day, 250 days per year, for 25 years, or 50,000 hours of
exposure through inhalation. The individual nitrosamine congeners, their respective RSL for industrial
inhalation exposures and one-half of the value of the respective lowest level of detection (1/2 LoD ng/m 3),
are presented in Table 5-8. Calculations and presumptions were derived from testing completed in 2019
at a 0.7 MWe pilot PCCCS with a water wash system on the absorber to control amine air emissions to
less than 1 ppmv (actual amine emissions were less than 0.6 ppmv), with no detected nitrosamines at
detection levels three orders of magnitude lower (less than 0.0002 ppmv) [51].It should be noted that the

 5-64
154

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

concentration units have been converted from µg/m 3 (2019 sample result units) to ng/m3 for ease of
reading and comparison. Table 5-8 risk levels are theoretical extrapolations and may not reflect actual
risks through a lifetime inhalation exposure pathway of ½ LoD concentrations presented.

 Table 5-8
 Nitrosamine Risk Characterization Summary

 Constituent RSL ng/m3 (a) ½ LoD ng/m3 (b) Risk Level (c)
             NPIP                         4.5                        98                       2.20E-05
            NDMA                          0.88                     141.4                      1.61E-04
            NMEA                          1.9                      176.8                      9.31E-05
            NDEA                          0.29                     149.3                      5.15E-04
            NDPA                          6.1                      101.5                      1.69E-04
             NPY                          20.0                     236.9                      1.18E-05
            NDBA                          7.7                      343.2                      4.46E-05
 (a)
 RSL = Ambient concentration correlated to 1:1,000,000 chance of additional lifetime cancer indices from a
 workplace lifetime inhalation exposure pathway (8 hrs/day; 250 days/yr; 50,000 hrs/lifetime) theoretical cancer

     Risk Level = [(½ LoD ÷ RSL) ÷ 1,000,000]

As shown in Table 5-8, one-half of the lowest respective detection level is at least an order of magnitude
greater than the RSL for an industrial worker that would be exposed for a full work-life for each
nitrosamine constituent. This indicates that even though no nitrosamines were detected in the samples,
the current detection level limits prevent a determination whether nitrosamines are present in
concentrations that may pose a risk exceeding the generally accepted regulatory guidelines for risk levels
of 1E-04 to 1E-06. If amine emissions are controlled to less than 1 ppmv at commercial-scale, it is plausible
that any nitrosamine that may be present will be comparable to the pilot plant testing performed to date.

Although the total mass of emissions will increase for a commercial-scale operation, it is uncertain
whether the increase would be linear with the increase in scale; however, it is likely that the relative
concentration would remain similar. Additional sampling is recommended to assess lifetime inhalation
exposure scenarios for offsite residential receptors to confirm that lifetime inhalation exposures at
residential receptors meet generally accepted regulatory guidelines for risk. The calculated risk levels
presented represent an overly conservative assessment absent of ambient sampling data.

ALL4 Project No. 001242-0004.00                                                                    November 2025

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

6. CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

The scope of the assessment was limited to evaluating observed EH&S issues with the solvent, electrodes
and their degradation during long-term operation or a bench-scale CCS, and further extrapolating to a
commercial-scale CCS Facility for a theoretical NGCC EGU located on in the midwestern United States.
Information considered included UKy IDEA and EPRI technical reports identified in Section 1.3, literature
review materials cited in Section 4, and past EH&S assessments conducted on analogous technologies.

The objective of the evaluation was to determine if there were any unacceptable EH&S concerns that may
prevent implementation or environmental permitting of a commercial-scale dual-loop CCS Facility. If
significant concerns were identified, the assessment was to include possible measures to reduce the
concerns and incorporate these measures into the actual design and operation of the CCS Facility. The
results of the assessment serve as a foundation for conducting additional investigation of the technology
to evaluate and confirm the extent of potential EH&S impacts for system operation and consider potential
mitigation measures.

6.1 AIR EMISSIONS

Potential emissions from solvent and solvent degradation products were estimated using a mixture of
stream data, process knowledge, and testing data. Further evaluation using larger pilot-scale testing data
is recommended to refine potential human health and environmental impacts from air emissions from
the proposed CCS Facility. While the methods used in this assessment are useful to estimate
order-of-magnitude impacts, validation of process data for gas flow rates, solvent liquid flow rates, stack
parameters (height, diameter, gas velocity), flue gas composition, and abnormal operating conditions (i.e.,
startup, shutdown, and varying load demands) for the Project are recommended following completion of
an engineering design package for the commercial-scale CCS Facility to accurately quantify risks/impacts
to human health or the environment. Additionally, emissions were not calculated for loading/storage
operations, tanks, roadways, or fugitive sources because site-specific design data was not available for
these sources.

A pre-construction air quality permit will be required prior to beginning actual construction of the
Greenfield Plant or the CCS Facility at an existing NGCC EGU under a Retrofit Project. The Site will likely

 6-1
156

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

be subject to major NSR program permitting requirements, as installation of a CCS of this scale combined
with increases in projected actual emissions from NGCC CTG will likely exceed SERs under the PSD and/or
NNSR. Major NSR permitting provisions would require the source to implement BACT and/or LAER for
regulated NSR pollutants with significant project-related emissions increases. Currently, the emissions
increase of VOC under a Retrofit Project scenario would not trigger NNSR requirements; however, if
further emissions testing/analysis shows an increase over major source criteria (i.e., 100 tpy), then NNSR
provisions will be applicable to the Project.

Major NSR permitting for the CCS Facility as a Greenfield Plant is more desirable and streamlined when
compared to major NSR permitting provisions for the CCS Facility under a Retrofit Project. The installation
of a CCS Facility under a Retrofit Project could require control technology reviews for existing emissions
units that might not be able to demonstrate BACT or LAER for regulated NSR pollutants due to physical or
site-specific equipment constraints.

Installation and operation of the CCS Facility will control CO 2 emissions to levels below major NSR
emissions thresholds and SERs while meeting the stringent Subpart TTTTa CO 2 emissions limits effective
beginning January 2032.

6.2 WATER MANAGEMENT

Future characterization of Project discharges at bench-scale, with further development of the process to
include cooling waters, is recommended in order to further identify potential degradation products that
may impede commercialization. The analysis of water-lean solvent after long-term operation for certain
degradation products indicated that any such product identified thus far is not anticipated to impede
commercialization. However, due to the novelty of the technology at commercial scale, its
implementation at an existing facility may result in scrutiny from regulators that are evaluating the
proposed technology under the current ELG. The potential for the implementation of a site-specific BPJ
limit introduces the risk of inconsistent implementation of these limits across geographies, including on a
state-to-state basis within the Midwest. The potential for inconsistent implementations is an unavoidable
consequence of the proposed technology not being included within a federal ELG; however, this risk does
not represent an insurmountable obstacle that would impede commercialization of the technology
evaluated under this EH&S Assessment.

 6-2
157

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Water use, stormwater discharge permitting, and the process of obtaining a new or modified permit are
not anticipated to be impediments to commercialization of the proposed technology. Geographic siting
of the technology, on a watershed basis, is particularly relevant and may have significant implications on
the ability of a particular discharge to be permitted. The receiving water that a discharge would be
directed to is highly recommended to be an early part of the site screening process, as certain receiving
waters are impaired and have a TMDL that may not allow additional pollutant loading and may prevent
any incremental increase to the amount of that pollutant discharged to them. While the most stringent
or impaired water bodies may not allow this type of discharge to it, it is not anticipated that this would be
an impediment to commercialization of this technology. The discharge of as many wastewater streams as
possible, which is determined by the treatment and hydraulic capacity of the POTW in question, under an
indirect discharge permit is highly recommended. Indirect discharge to a POTW, where possible, is
anticipated to provide the most favorable regulatory conditions for operating the proposed technology.

6.3 WASTE MANAGEMENT

Wastes from raw material storage would primarily consist of out of specification, spilled, or excess
materials. Wastes from chemicals used in the CCS Facility will consist primarily of reclaimer sludge, spent
filters, spent GAC cartridges, and miscellaneous maintenance lubricants and fluids. Some materials may
be corrosive or contain toxic chemicals at concentrations warranting disposal as a hazardous waste that
should be readily managed and disposed of or recycled according to applicable state and RCRA
regulations.

If some of the CCS Facility wastes are characterized as hazardous, the volume generated may classify the
Site as a LQG and impose additional requirements for recordkeeping, emergency planning, and reporting.
From current knowledge, there does not appear to be any waste that would have unique or more
stringent restrictions or management requirements (i.e., specifically listed acutely hazardous waste).
Operations may be optimized to minimize generation of waste that is determined to be hazardous.

Waste types and volumes are not atypical for an industrial facility and do not represent a unique
environmental concern. These wastes can be minimized by using a well-managed material handling
protocols and procurement practices to avoid spills, contamination, and excessive inventory requiring
disposal.

 6-3
158

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

6.4 EMPLOYEE SAFETY AND ACCIDENTAL RELEASES

The primary health hazards from raw material handling will be due to the CO 2 scrubbing solvent being
identified as corrosive and an irritant. The solvent will be mixed with water to create an aqueous solution,
which will reduce the risk, but employees will need to be aware of potential hazards (e.g., eye burns, skin
irritant, inhalation irritant) and wear appropriate PPE. The remaining raw materials anticipated to be used
(e.g., minor amounts of maintenance items) do not represent unmanageable health concerns, but
appropriate precautions will need to be taken to avoid accidents and injuries.

Safety hazards include potential exposure to noise, heat, steam, and pressure vessels. These hazards are
not unusual for an industrial facility and can be managed with appropriate precautions to avoid accidents
and injuries, such as appropriate PPE. Additional employee hazards inherent to the process include
electrical hazards, unexpected releases of hazardous energy, and engulfment or exposure to hazardous
substances in confined spaces. Preventative measures for electrical hazards and unexpected energy
releases entail the implementation of LOTO procedures to protect operators.

Noise generated by the process is primarily derived from system pumps and blowers. The commercial
facility will increase occupational noise levels in the area; however, it is not anticipated the CCS Facility
will substantially increase noise levels relative to operations of an adjoining commercial-scale power plant.

6.5 OTHER REGULATORY CONSIDERATIONS

General regulatory requirements for transport of product gases produced by the CCS Facility were
evaluated and there were no impediments to commercialization identified for the storage and offsite
transport of CCS Facility product gases by pipeline, rail, or truck identified under this assessment.
Site-specific requirements for any pipeline construction activities with a federal nexus will require a NEPA
review and potentially an environmental assessment.

The Site will likely trigger reporting under EPCRA for chemicals used in the CCS Facility under the U.S. EPA
TRI program. Reported releases are publicly available and care should be made to accurately quantify
releases reported to the U.S. EPA TRI-MEweb portal. TRI reporting for NGCC power plant facilities is
common and does not represent an obstacle for commercialization of the CCS technology assessed under
this report.

 6-4
159

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

Because the Site assessed for future commercial deployment of this technology is a hypothetical
greenfield, CERCLA would not be applicable unless or until a regulated release would occur. The Site
selection process should assess contiguous property parcel(s) for potential contamination that could
migrate to the site and potentially subject the Site to mitigation and response requirements under
CERCLA. Conducting proper due diligence under the Site selection process will mitigate unanticipated or
uncontrolled EH&S risks from subsurface contamination or other hazardous material releases potentially
present at a site.

The risk of U.S. EPA applying the SNUR to UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent under TSCA is low because it is
widely known that amine-based solvents are used in carbon capture processes and their use has not been
found to present an unacceptable risk. Review of CCS Facility raw materials did not show any regulated
chemicals proposed for use that are likely to become subject to risk evaluations under TSCA that could
prevent or significantly delay their use in the CCS Facility.

6.6 TOXICOLOGICAL EFFECTS

As noted in Section 5.6.4, potential exposures to nitrosamines during operation of the commercial-scale
plant may exceed U.S. EPA acceptable cancer risk ranges. It is reasonable to presume that a commercial
CCS Facility may generate relatively larger amounts of nitrosamines, albeit at similar concentrations due
to increased size of process operations when compared to pilot- and bench-scale CCS. This could result in
increased risk of exposure and harmful health effects without additional emissions control measures.
However, uncertainties of the risk characterization suggest additional assessment of actual exposure
frequencies, durations, and exposure point concentrations (EPC) for nitrosamines is warranted.

6.7 MATERIAL RISK CHARACTERIZATION AND COMMERCIALIZATION CONSIDERATIONS

Due to possible irritation and other toxicities, direct exposure to amine-based CO 2 capture solvents should
be avoided. The primary and immediate health concern identified for the UKy IDEA Water-Lean Solvent is
the corrosive nature of the material. Direct exposure to the solvent may cause severe skin irritation,
damage to the eyes, mouth, throat, and respiratory system. Appropriate precautions and PPE should be
used when exposure may occur until more definitive testing results better quantify risk from potential

 6-5
160

 EH&S Assessment of Bench-Scale Dual-Loop Solution-Based Carbon Dioxide

nitrosamine exposure. Occupational exposure to degradation products present in CCS Facility exhaust
stream and water wash exit material should also be avoided.

Assessing potential nitrosamine exposures is challenging due to the limited bench-scale and historical pilot
studies, as well as detection methods that may not identify nitrosamines present at levels above those
considered potentially harmful by U.S. EPA RSLs. Due to uncertainties in the exposure scenarios
considered and data collected thus far, extrapolating these results to operation of a commercial CCS may
not accurately identify potential risks of nitrosamine exposure by site workers or the surrounding
community.

Due to the improbability of significant exposure to CCS Facility chemicals via ingestion or dermal exposure,
inhalation was the primary route of potential exposure evaluated in the assessment. An attempt to
characterize a plausible air emissions exposure based on potential nitrosamines generated was conducted
by using one-half of the lowest reported LoD of nitrosamines from August 2019 testing [51]. The
calculated risk levels from the 2019 testing campaign range from 1.18E-05 to 5.15E-04, are within the
generally acceptable range for industrial exposure, and incorporate considerable conservatism. At
present, there is insufficient data to confirm how emissions for a commercial-scale CCS Facility would
increase compared to current available test results from pilot- and bench-scale CCS testing by UKy IDEA.

SECTION 4: First aid measures
4.1 Description of first-aid measures
 General advice
 First aiders need to protect themselves. Show this material safety data sheet to the doctor
 in attendance.
 If inhaled
 After inhalation: fresh air. Call in physician.
 In case of skin contact
 In case of skin contact: Take off immediately all contaminated clothing. Rinse skin with
 water/ shower. Call a physician immediately.
 In case of eye contact
 After eye contact: rinse out with plenty of water. Immediately call in ophthalmologist.
 Remove contact lenses.
 If swallowed
 After swallowing: make victim drink water (two glasses at most), avoid vomiting (risk of
 perforation). Call a physician immediately. Do not attempt to neutralise.
4.2 Most important symptoms and effects, both acute and delayed
 The most important known symptoms and effects are described in the labelling (see section
 2.2) and/or in section 11
4.3 Indication of any immediate medical attention and special treatment needed

 Unsuitable extinguishing media

5.2 Special hazards arising from the substance or mixture
 Carbon oxides
 Nitrogen oxides (NOx)
 Combustible.
 Vapors are heavier than air and may spread along floors.
 Forms explosive mixtures with air on intense heating.
 Development of hazardous combustion gases or vapours possible in the event of fire.
5.3 Advice for firefighters
 Stay in danger area only with self-contained breathing apparatus. Prevent skin contact by
 keeping a safe distance or by wearing suitable protective clothing.
5.4 Further information
 Remove container from danger zone and cool with water. Suppress (knock down)
 gases/vapors/mists with a water spray jet. Prevent fire extinguishing water from
 contaminating surface water or the ground water system.

SECTION 6: Accidental release measures
6.1 Personal precautions, protective equipment and emergency procedures
 Advice for non-emergency personnel: Do not breathe vapors, aerosols. Avoid substance
 contact. Ensure adequate ventilation. Keep away from heat and sources of ignition.
 Evacuate the danger area, observe emergency procedures, consult an expert.
 For personal protection see section 8.
6.2 Environmental precautions

6.3 Methods and materials for containment and cleaning up
 Cover drains. Collect, bind, and pump off spills. Observe possible material restrictions
 (see sections 7 and 10). Take up with liquid-absorbent material (e.g. Chemizorb® ).
 Dispose of properly. Clean up affected area.
6.4 Reference to other sections
 For disposal see section 13.

SECTION 7: Handling and storage
7.1 Precautions for safe handling
 Advice on protection against fire and explosion
 Keep away from open flames, hot surfaces and sources of ignition.Take precautionary
 measures against static discharge.
 Hygiene measures
 Immediately change contaminated clothing. Apply preventive skin protection. Wash hands
 and face after working with substance.

SECTION 10: Stability and reactivity
10.1 Reactivity
 Forms explosive mixtures with air on intense heating.
 A range from approx. 15 Kelvin below the flash point is to be rated as critical.
10.2 Chemical stability
 The product is chemically stable under standard ambient conditions (room temperature) .
10.3 Possibility of hazardous reactions
 Caution! In contact with nitrites, nitrates, nitrous acid possible liberation of nitrosamines!
 Violent reactions possible with:
 Strong oxidizing agents
 Exothermic reaction with:
 acids

 Strong heating.

 Strong oxidizing agents

 4. First-aid measures
General Advice Show this safety data sheet to the doctor in attendance. Immediate medical attention is
 required.

Eye Contact Rinse immediately with plenty of water, also under the eyelids, for at least 15 minutes. In
 the case of contact with eyes, rinse immediately with plenty of water and seek medical
 advice.

Skin Contact Wash off immediately with plenty of water for at least 15 minutes. Immediate medical
 attention is required.

Inhalation Remove to fresh air. If not breathing, give artificial respiration. Do not use mouth-to-mouth
 method if victim ingested or inhaled the substance; give artificial respiration with the aid of a
 pocket mask equipped with a one-way valve or other proper respiratory medical device.
 Immediate medical attention is required.

Ingestion Do NOT induce vomiting. Call a physician or poison control center immediately.

Most important symptoms and None reasonably foreseeable.
effects
Notes to Physician Treat symptomatically

Protective Equipment and Precautions for Firefighters
As in any fire, wear self-contained breathing apparatus pressure-demand, MSHA/NIOSH (approved or equivalent) and full
protective gear. Thermal decomposition can lead to release of irritating gases and vapors.

 6. Accidental release measures
Personal Precautions Ensure adequate ventilation. Use personal protective equipment as required. Keep people
 away from and upwind of spill/leak. Evacuate personnel to safe areas.
Environmental Precautions Should not be released into the environment.

Methods for Containment and Clean Keep in suitable, closed containers for disposal. Soak up with inert absorbent material.
Up

 7. Handling and storage
Handling Wear personal protective equipment/face protection. Do not get in eyes, on skin, or on
 clothing. Use only under a chemical fume hood. Do not breathe mist/vapors/spray. Do not
 ingest. If swallowed then seek immediate medical assistance.

Storage. Keep containers tightly closed in a dry, cool and well-ventilated place. Do not allow contact
 with air. Incompatible Materials. Strong acids. Strong oxidizing agents. oxygen.

 8. Exposure controls / personal protection
Exposure Guidelines This product does not contain any hazardous materials with occupational exposure
 limitsestablished by the region specific regulatory bodies.

Engineering Measures Use only under a chemical fume hood. Ensure that eyewash stations and safety showers
 are close to the workstation location.

Personal Protective Equipment

 Eye/face Protection Wear appropriate protective eyeglasses or chemical safety goggles as described by
 OSHA's eye and face protection regulations in 29 CFR 1910.133 or European Standard
 EN166.

 Skin and body protection Wear appropriate protective gloves and clothing to prevent skin exposure.

    208                                                   Page 3 / 7

 Respiratory Protection Follow the OSHA respirator regulations found in 29 CFR 1910.134 or European Standard
 EN 149. Use a NIOSH/MSHA or European Standard EN 149 approved respirator if
 exposure limits are exceeded or if irritation or other symptoms are experienced.

 Hygiene Measures Handle in accordance with good industrial hygiene and safety practice.
